Breathe In (Breathe Out)

by thetalee

Summary

Evan Buckley Diaz died. For more than three minutes, his heart did not beat. He wasn't dead now, he was as healthy as someone could be, mere weeks after being struck by lightning. But sometimes...

Notes

Title is from "Breathe In, Breathe Out, Move On" by Jimmy Buffett

See the end of the work for more notes

And It Rained (It Was Nothing Really New)

Everything in Dr. Copeland’s office was selected with the singular aim of being welcoming and comforting, of projecting an aura of safety. The walls were painted a pale yellow, while the couch and matching chairs were a shade closer to gold. The (so very comfortable) couch had throw pillows of various colors and textures. Buck’s favorite was a big pink one covered in fluffy faux fur. It reminded him of cotton candy, kinda. Often, depending on what they were talking about, he dragged it into his lap and ran his fingers through it like he was petting Hoover.

He had it in his lap now, his fingers buried in the fake fur.

Copeland was in one of the arm chairs, simply waiting for him to say something. Eddie wasn’t there with them, he was outside in the waiting room. He’d looked concerned when Buck had mentioned wanting to talk to the doctor alone that day, but he hadn’t pushed the subject. Especially since it was their first actually scheduled session since the lightning. So it was just Buck curled up on the couch, fluffy pillow in his lap, while the doctor patiently waited for him to talk.

“I haven’t told Eddie about everything from my dream,” is what he finally said. “I actually… There’s a part of it I haven’t told anyone, actually.”

He’d told Eddie and the others all about his missing heart, getting ragged on for how even his subconscious was so terribly sappy. He’d told people the broad strokes and the unimportant details, like being a teacher instead of a firefighter, like Chimney and Maddie being married. He’d told Copeland, in one of their virtual sessions, about how his mind used her face and voice to shout at him, which had amused her greatly.

He had even spent an evening with Maddie, talking about the Daniel that he dreamed up. Listened to her wonder if the real one would have ever wanted to be a doctor. Debated what he would have actually looked like as an adult, and just where Buck got the details from to fill in the gaps.

But there was one part, a large part, that he hadn’t said a single, solitary word about. Not to anyone.

“Which part?” Copeland asked, politely curious.

“The end,” Buck answered. “Just before I woke up. Daniel stopped me.”

“From waking up?”

“From going through the door and-” He cut himself off and shook his head. “Dream logic. He tried to stop me from waking up. And he had some not nice things to say, while he was at it.”

“So you’re saying that, in this dream, your negative thoughts took the form of your brother.”

“He was mean,” Buck muttered. “But that’s not- I mean, it’s part of it, I guess. It was one of the things he said. Actually, it was something you said, too.”

The doctor’s eyebrows rose.

“And what would that be?” she asked.

“He- Well, first you asked if I even wanted to wake up,” he said, fingers digging into the pillow in his lap. “And he kept trying to convince me that it would be better if I didn’t.”

Copeland let out a sigh and leaned forward a bit, settling her elbows on her thighs. She didn’t look entirely surprised, he noticed. That fact had him frowning at her.

“I’ve brought up this concern before,” she commented. “Most recently after the incident with the bike and the drunk driver. Some of your actions, at times, display a severe lack of concern towards your own safety.”

“There’s a difference between that and this,” Buck insisted. “Putting myself in danger to help someone isn’t the same as a part of me not wanting to wake up.”

“While I would normally agree, it does paint a pattern,” she said. “And your job involves putting yourself in necessary danger. Danger that is as mitigated as possible. Was this the case with the drunk driver?”

“No,” he admitted sourly, feeling like he was being scolded again. “But- But being an idiot isn’t the same as wanting to die.”

“Do you want to die?” Dr. Copeland asked. Her gaze and her voice were both sharp.

“I’m not going to kill myself.”

“That wasn’t my question,” she said, though she relaxed a bit at his answer. “Suicidal ideation takes two forms, Evan. Active, where you throw yourself in front of the bus. And passive, where you simply don’t get out of the way of it.”

“I don’t want to die,” he insisted, eyes focused on the throw pillow he was clutching. “Really, I don’t. Maybe I- Maybe sometimes I don’t put enough thought into my own safety. But I don’t want to die. That’s why it’s…” He struggled for the right word for a moment, before settling on, “Bothering me. That’s why it’s bothering me that a part of my dream, my mind, was trying to convince me not to live.”

“How did you respond?” Dr. Copeland asked, and the question had him faltering for a moment.

“Respond?”

“To your brother, in the dream. When he was arguing against you waking up. You said that he said some not nice things.”

“I argued back,” he answered. “I laughed at him, at one point. And then Eddie showed up and punched him.”

“And how did you respond to me?” Copeland asked. “You said I asked you… What was it?”

“If I wanted to wake up,” Buck told her. “There was this whole thing. You- She? The you in the dream yelled at me about living for myself instead of other people. She - you? - only helped me after I gave her an answer she liked.”

“That was your subconscious, as well,” Copeland pointed out. “The me interrogating you about your reasons to live. The you that laughed at your brother when he held you up, and the Eddie that punched him. While Daniel, and what he represented, should definitely not be discounted, neither should those parts of it, as well.”

“I guess.” It was a concession given grudgingly. “It was still me, though. How am I supposed to handle this, knowing that a part of me didn’t want to wake up?”

“There are multiple ways of handling it. Of coping. I think for today…” She hummed, tapped her finger on her knee. “An exercise. I would like you to list off everyone in your support system.”

“A list, huh?” Buck let out a quiet huff. “Should I start with you? Or do it alphabetically?”

“However you wish. And then afterwards, I want to hear what answer you gave me in your dream.”

“Okay. Um. There’s you. Obviously there’s Eddie…”

The bedroom was dark, when Buck opened his eyes. He didn’t know what had woken him up, but that was not his immediate focus. His immediate focus was on-

The space beside him was empty, his arm going through the air to land on the sheets. He sat up, his hand immediately going to his chest to feel the quick patter of his heart, to reassure himself he wasn’t back in that dream. The alarm clock on Eddie’s side told him that it was a couple hours past midnight.

What had woken him up? Had it been Eddie getting out of bed? Had it been a noise somewhere else in the house? Or was it the rain pouring down outside, beating against the windowpane and filling him with anxiety?

When he was a kid, younger than Chris had been when Eddie first moved to the city, Buck had been terrified of thunderstorms. It was a fear he’d grown out of as he got older, but for a while there, thunder had been scarier than any monster lurking in the dark corners of his bedroom. Whenever the clouds began to rumble, whenever the glass in the windows shook, he’d go running to the safest place in the house he could think of: Maddie’s room.

He had vague memories of waking up in the middle of the night and trying to find refuge with his parents. They’d always send him back to his room, complaining about him disturbing their sleep for no reason. But every time he ran to his sister, Maddie would just lift up the comforter for him to clamber in beside her, pull him close and protect him from the storm outside.

Buck grew out of the fear long before she left for college. By the time he was driving from city to city on his own, thunder and lightning didn’t bother him a bit. The other drivers on the rain-slicked roads scared him far more. And in Los Angeles, when even the “wet” seasons were typically dry? A good storm was something to be both thankful for and to curse.

But that was Then, before he climbed that ladder in a storm and died for it. Now, when the room lit up with lightning and - one Mississippi, two Mississippi - the glass in the window rattled with thunder, he was that little boy again. Frightened and filled with the urge to go running for his big sister.

There was a small, unhappy cry from the room next door. A soft voice, as familiar to Buck as his own heartbeat, drifted along with it. It was a short and quick trip to find the source.

“Arrorró, mi niño. Arrorró, mi sol.” Eddie was singing quietly, swaying in place as he held a clearly upset Gavin. “Arrorró, pedazo de mi corazón.”

“I thought I was your sun?” Buck quipped, stepping into the room. His husband looked up from the toddler and smiled, bright even in the dark.

“You are,” was the immediate reply. “Did we wake you? I turned off the monitor so we wouldn’t.”

“Dunno,” Buck answered truthfully as he padded closer. “Just woke up and you weren’t there.”

“Sorry.”

“Don’t be. Our little guy scared of the storm?”

“I think he’s just upset that the thunder interrupted his beauty sleep,” Eddie told him. He hadn’t stopped swaying the entire time they talked. Gavin’s head was resting on his shoulder, eyes blinking slowly and heavily.

“Was that what your mom would sing to you?” Buck asked, reaching up to run fingers through Gavin’s hair.

“You think Mom sang Spanish lullabies?” The other man didn’t quite scoff, but it was a close thing. “It’s one Abuela used to sing. I don’t remember all the words, though.”

“I don’t know any lullabies. I feel like that’s something I should have stocked up on at some point.”

“You can look some up tomorrow,” Eddie said. “Why don’t you go back to bed? I’ve got him.”

“No, no, I’ll-” Buck was cut off by more thunder, and despite how much he tried, he didn’t quite manage to keep from flinching at the noise.

“How about,” his husband said, his gaze boring into him, “I bring him to bed with us?”

“Yeah, alright. That sounds good,” he answered, not quite meeting his husband’s eyes. “You hear that, little guy? You’re bunking with us tonight.”

Buck grabbed the gray blanket from off the rocking chair and followed Eddie back to their room. He slid back under the covers while Eddie sat against the headboard. They tucked a slightly more awake Gavin in between them, wrapped up in his blanket.

“Comfy?” Eddie asked, sounding amused.

“Very,” Buck replied, his entire body curved around the toddler in their bed, like a giant C.

“Alright then,” his husband said, before clearing his throat. “Arrorró, mi niño. Arrorró, mi sol. Arrorró, pedazo de mi corazón.”

More thunder rumbled outside. Buck twitched and almost reflexively curled closer to both Gavin and Eddie.

“Este niño lindo ya quiere dormir.” There was a pause. When Buck looked up, he saw Eddie was frowning. “Háganle la cuna… Háganle la cuna de rosa y jazmín. Esta leche linda-”

“Beautiful milk?” Buck blurted out. “Are you sure those’re the words?”

“Do you want to sing it, since you know it so well?” Eddie offered, eyebrows raised. Buck shook his head and mimed zipping his mouth shut. Nodding, his husband continued. “Esta leche linda que le traigo aquí, es para este niño que se va a dormir.”

Buck closed his eyes, letting Eddie’s voice drown out the rain and thunder.

“Arrorró, mi niño. Arrorró, mi sol. Arrorró, pedazo de mi corazón…”

Bobby, perhaps, was a bit too excited as he rushed for the stairs to answer the door. Athena clearly thought so, from the way she laughed at him as he ran by. There was no shame in his game, though. Not about this.

“Hey, Pops,” Buck greeted him when he opened the door. Gavin was held securely in his arms, and the diaper bag, with its iron-on LAFD patches, was hanging off his shoulder. “Thanks so much for this.”

Originally, it had been Maddie and Chimney who were going to watch the one-year-old while they spent some quality time with Chris. But apparently a doctor’s appointment for Jee-Yun landed on the one day of Chris’ Spring Break that they could make it to the zoo. Bobby had been very quick to say yes, when they asked if he would watch Gavin.

“I don’t think you need to thank him for anything,” Athena said, joining her husband at the door.

“You really don’t,” Bobby said, reaching out for his grandson and making impatient grabbing motions when Buck took too long to hand him over.

“I am gonna get him back at the end of the day, right?” Buck joked, rolling his eyes as he passed Athena the diaper bag.

“I make no promises,” Bobby replied, bouncing Gavin in his arms.

“Gwa’pop,” the boy said, happily clinging to him, and he felt something in his chest melt.

“Late night?” Athena asked, and the question had him looking back at Buck. Now that he was paying attention to him instead of his grandson, he spotted the signs of a sleepless night.

“The storm woke him up,” Buck answered, but something in his voice had Bobby feeling suspicious. By the way Athena was frowning, she was having the same thoughts. “But I need to get going. You be good, okay?”

“He’ll be perfect,” Bobby insisted.

“I wasn’t talking to him,” Buck said, laughing when Bobby rolled his eyes. “Okay, I’m leaving. Bye Gavin.”

“Bye-bye,” Gavin replied.

It took a bit before Buck could pull himself away to get back in the jeep. Athena ended up closing the door in his face before going downstairs with the diaper bag, which got deposited in the living room. 

“You know, I was thinking,” Bobby said as he carried Gavin down the stairs and into the kitchen. “How do you feel about cookies for lunch?”

“You’re going to spoil him today, aren’t you?” Athena asked, peeling a banana and setting it on the cutting board.

“Absolutely.”

“Nana,” Gavin babbled, reaching for Athena.

“Oh, you want some?” Bobby asked, snagging a piece of banana that his wife had just sliced.

“No,” the boy said, shaking his head, still reaching for Athena. “Nana.”

Athena paused mid-cut, turning her head to stare at him.

“Did he…?”

“I think he did,” Bobby said.

“Which one of those boys taught him that?” she asked, setting down the knife. She took Gavin from Bobby, letting out a sigh. “Cookies, then?”

“Sure thing,” Bobby said, pulling out the mixing bowl. “Nana.”

“I have a gun.”

Running the Numbers

Chapter Summary

In which Buck discovers a new skill, and is then promptly roasted for it.

Buckleys ran, it’s what they did. Margaret and Phillip ran from Daniel’s death and the things they’d done to try and prevent it. Maddie had run from her parents, and then from Doug, and then tried her best to run away from herself. And Buck, he ran from Hershey, ran up and down the hemisphere until finally stumbling to a stop in Los Angeles, and probably would have kept running if things had gone differently.

Dr. Copeland said that suicidal ideation took two forms, active and passive. Jumping in front of the bus versus simply not getting out of the bus’ way. And in hindsight, well. Being told that he might be, possibly, passively suicidal wasn’t exactly news to Buck, was it?

Hell, he’d nearly admitted as much once before, hadn’t he? After the factory fire, during the whole Daniel fiasco, he had told Bobby that he almost gave up. Despite having Eddie waiting for him outside, despite having Bobby and Maddie and Chris and Athena and- And all the others, despite his entire family, he almost gave up. He almost let that building become his funeral pyre, before they all rushed in to save him from himself.

(“I would like you to list off everyone in your support system.”)

How could he be trusted to have someone’s back in a fire, if he couldn’t even trust himself?

That was just the passive suicide, though. If Buck spent too long thinking about it, allowed himself to remember, there were so many times that his self-destructive tendencies crossed the line and became… 

He had seen the car pulling out, the day he crashed his bike. He had seen it and hadn’t turned, hadn’t slowed, hadn’t- 

( He remembered feeling disappointed, when he walked away. )

Buckleys ran and Evan Buckley had been no different, fleeing from Hershey with a jeep and a bag and nothing else. That had been a form of suicide, hadn’t it? An opportunity, yes. A freedom, definitely. But also a form of suicide, because it wasn’t like he’d cared about where he ended up. Only cared about getting away, about escaping. Cared more about the way he’d felt so alone and like he was drowning and like he was screaming himself hoarse with no one listening and-

When he entered the Fire Academy, when there were three other Evans in his class and so he became Buck, that had been a form of suicide, too. He murdered Evan to become Buck, and then he killed Buck 1.0 to become 2.0 and then 3.0. He killed himself a half dozen times even while he kept marching on.

(That was Buck 1.0. No one liked Buck 1.0. No, no, I’m a totally different person, now…)

So maybe that’s the real reason why the way Daniel had spoken to him in his dream, the way he’d tried to convince him to just not wake up, was bothering him. Not because this was brand new information, but because it was old news. An elephant in the room that he had spent so long ignoring, and now it was stomping on his sternum.

“I have a doctor’s appointment next week,” Buck blurted out as he pulled the jeep into the station’s parking lot. “With a Dr. Weaver. Copeland helped set it up.”

“Alright,” Eddie said, looking at him curiously. “Are you finally getting confirmation about the thing?”

The “thing” being his nigh-probable ADHD, the official diagnosis of which he’d been putting off because… Well, there were many reasons, but mostly he just hadn’t seen the point. And Copeland couldn’t do it herself, because she wasn’t that kind of doctor. She was the talking kind, not the kind who could prescribe medication and happy pills, nor could she make official medical diagnoses.

“No- Well, m- maybe,” Buck said. “But no, this is about something else.”

“And you’re telling me now, just before we’re on shift, because you don’t want to talk about it yet.”

“Sorry?” He meant it. “I mean, it’s absolutely something we should talk about, I just…”

“It’s fine, mi sol,” Eddie said, interrupting him and placing a warm hand on his thigh. “We’ll talk when you’re ready.”

Buck almost pointed out how that could very well mean never, but he chose to remain silent. Instead, he parked the jeep and hopped out, grabbing his bag out of the backseat while Eddie did the same. They were already dressed in their uniforms; they’d learned early on with Gavin that it was faster that way, instead of dressing into civvies and then putting their uniforms on at work. At the very least, it saved them time, which was spent trying to pull themselves away from their little guy.

They didn’t have to pull themselves away from Chris. The almost-a-teenager practically ran away from them, these days.

“What do you think is gonna be on the cake?” Buck asked as they walked inside. It was his first day back, a little later than he’d been cleared for, and Hen would inevitably have a cake at the ready.

“Something borderline insulting,” Eddie guessed as they crossed the bay to the locker room. “A high voltage warning.”

“Oh, that’s a good one.”

They deposited their bags in their lockers, and then headed for the loft. There was a banner hanging from the loft’s railing, and Eddie silently swore when he saw the amount of tallies on it. He probably hadn’t been ready to see an actual count of all of Buck’s near-death experiences.

“And that’s only counting what’s happened since I started here,” Buck pointed out, thinking about the motorcycle.

“Bubble wrap,” his husband muttered, nudging him to hurry up the stairs. “I am wrapping you up in bubble wrap.”

“He’d probably smother himself on it or something,” Chimney quipped from where he was standing by the top step. 

“He probably would,” Eddie agreed, sounding tired. 

Buck ignored them as bounded for the kitchen island, eager to see the cake. And then feeling so very disappointed when all he saw was a blank sheet of white icing.

“Don’t make that face,” Hen scolded, smacking him with a towel she grabbed. “It’s cake.”

“Yeah,” Buck agreed, trying not to pout. “And- And it’s a nice cake, it’s just…”

“Boring,” Albert finished for him, popping up with Ravi. “It’s a boring cake.”

“The original cake burnt down with the rest of the bakery,” Hen said, glaring at them both. “But if this one isn’t to your liking, I’ll just throw it in the trash here…”

“Now let’s not be hasty,” Buck said, dragging the cake closer to himself and farther away from her. “It’s not the outside that counts, it’s the inside. What kind of cake did you get?”

“Vanilla.”

Well. This morning was just a cornucopia of disappointments, wasn’t it?

“Here,” Bobby said, setting down a stack of plates, along with forks. He was wielding a large knife in his hand. “I’m sure it’s a very good cake, Hen.”

“Oh! Has anyone told you about Gavin’s new word, yet?” Buck asked, swerving the topic away from the (so incredibly disappointing) cake.

“Was it ‘titty?’” Ravi guessed, and Albert snorted out a laugh beside him.

“It was not,” Eddie said, sliding up to the group. He frowned at the cake, and then ducked his head when he spotted Hen’s glare. “And thank you for reminding us why neither of you are on the list of babysitters.”

“Apparently someone taught him to call Athena, ‘Nana,’” Bobby said, cutting the cake and dividing up the portions. Buck got one that was noticeably larger than the others, and he stuck his tongue out at Eddie’s fake pout.

“Oh, that was brave of you, Buck,” Hen said, shaking her head.

“It wasn’t me,” he responded. “I was trying to get him to say ‘Granny.’”

“It was Chris, apparently,” Eddie told them.

“He’s our delightful little troll.”

“And also the only one she wouldn’t be mad at for it,” Chimney commented.

“That, too.”

The cake turned out pretty good, actually. Maybe a bit dry, and Buck suspected that she’d grabbed it off the shelf at the store, but he wasn’t about to say anything. That seemed like a one-way ticket to another tally on the banner, and he wasn’t in the mood for that.

After the cake, and after they broke up to take care of their variously assigned chores, they got their first call-out of the day. The instructor for a spin class collapsed in the middle of it. Someone named Cam Lewis, according to the caller.

“LAFD,” Bobby hollered as they entered the building, Buck loping along beside him with Hen and Chimney right behind. Eddie and the others were getting the gurney. “Make room, everybody.”

The crowd parted for them, one of the cyclists hurrying to intercept them and lead the way.

“He went down in the middle of class,” she explained, a reusable bottle in hand. “He’s breathing, but we couldn’t get him to wake up.”

Another woman was standing over the patient, looking worried, but she quickly got out of the way for Chimney and Hen.

“That ankle is definitely sprained or broken,” Hen commented, peering down at it. “That happen before or after he passed out?”

“After,” the first woman answered. “Something was wrong with him. Maybe it’s that thing that happens? When your thighs poison you?”

“Rhabdomyolysis?” Chimney offered.

“We see a lot of it,” Hen said.

Buck wasn’t exactly certain what rhabdomyolysis was. He could probably ask, but opted on keeping his mouth shut, instead. He’ll look it up later.

“BP is elevated,” Chimney reported, frowning at the pressure cuff on the man’s arm. The oximeter he placed on Cam’s finger beeped, and it got an even bigger frown. “He’s also tachycardic. But I don’t think he’s having a heart attack.”

“Could be physical exertion,” Hen suggested, listening to the man’s heart.

“He was going pretty hard when this happened?” Bobby asked the two women that were hovering nearby, the one that had led them through the room and the one that had been by Cam’s side.

“Yeah,” one of them answered. “It came on suddenly.”

“His lips and skin look dry,” Hen said, already in the process of starting a line of saline. “Could be severe dehydration.”

“Dehydration? That doesn’t make any sense,” the first woman said, the one clutching the bottle. Eddie and the others arrived with the gurney, and she shuffled to get out of their way, too. “Everyone’s been hydrating.”

“And that’s water?” Bobby asked, gesturing at the bottle.

“It’s better than water,” she answered. “It’s Cam Lewis’ Taut Tummy Tea.”

Eddie frowned at the name, and then pointed at the man laying on the ground. When Buck nodded to confirm that yes, it was Cam Lewis leading the class that was drinking his product, his husband rolled his eyes.

“They did a pretty hard sell on it when we first signed up,” the second woman commented. She didn’t have a bottle, and she looked as enthused about the product placement as Eddie did.

Buck moseyed over to a stack of boxes containing the tea, his curiosity getting the better of him. Cam Lewis, meanwhile, awoke with a jerk and a shout of,

“I feel like I’m on fire!”

“You’re okay,” Chimney said, working to calm him down. “We’re flushing your system with saline. Try to relax.”

The second cyclist, the one without the bottle, asking “are you okay?” alerted them to the first woman swaying on her feet. Bobby moved just in time to catch her. The man farther back in the crowd wasn’t so lucky, nor was the third woman who collapsed.

“Dehydration, flushed skin,” Eddie reported, kneeling by one of them. “Probably tachycardic.”

“I have the same over here,” Albert called over from his.

“Alright.” Bobby gently lowered the woman he caught to the ground, moving out of the way so the others could get to her. He grabbed his read and thumbed it on. “Dispatch, this is Captain Nash, One Eighteen. We’re gonna need additional paramedic units at our location…”

“This Tummy Tea.” Hen was holding up the woman’s bottle. “What’s in it?”

“Fifty milligrams of B3 per serving, and magnesium,” Buck answered, frowning at the nutritional facts listed on the box, which he turned towards Cam Lewis. “How much of this did she drink?”

“A packet has two servings,” was the answer.

“That’s a hundred milligrams.” He glanced at the first woman to collapse, did his best to judge her size just by looking at her. “A woman her size should be having fourteen milligrams. So she’s taking in-” 100, divided by 14… “Seven-point-one-four times the daily allowance.”

When he looked up, everyone was staring at him.

“Did he just do math?” Chimney asked, sounding as shocked as Buck had been when the lightning struck. “In his head?”

“I think so.” Eddie’s brow was furrowed in confusion.

“Was… Was I right?” Buck asked, looking at Hen, because she’d know.

“You were,” she confirmed, and the amount of surprise going around right now would be insulting under normal circumstances. “And given that amount of B3, I think we’re looking at acute niacin toxicity.”

“Hydration all around?” Bobby suggested, just in time for someone else to collapse behind him.

“Immediately,” Hen concurred.

After that, it was mostly a matter of hooking people up to saline and passing around bottles of water. Hen and Chimney and Albert carted Cam Lewis off in the ambulance, to get his ankle looked at, and the rest of them piled into the engine.

“You’re on algebra duty,” Eddie declared the moment the doors were closed and they had their headsets on. “For the rest of, I don’t know. Forever. Have you been lying to me this whole time to get out of helping our kid with his homework?”

“No!” Buck cried, feeling very defensive. “This is… New. I think this is the lightning.”

“The lightning?” Bobby echoed from up front.

“Yeah, the lightning!”

“The Flash gets struck by lightning, and he can go fast enough to break time,” Ravi said, shaking his head. “You get struck by lightning, and now you can do basic algebra.”

“...yes?”

“That’s lame,” the younger man said. “That is so lame. The lamest superpower ever.”

“Hey!”

“Look out, it’s the Mathematician! Be careful or he’ll do an equation at you!”

“Ravi, I swear…”

Bet

Chapter Summary

In which the Mathematician uses his powers for eeeeviiiiiil

So Buck could do math now. That was apparently a thing. And he was quite proud of this fact, showing it off whenever he could. For example, he insisted on calculating exactly how many gallons of gas he needed to get them to the firehouse one morning, instead of doing the easy thing and just filling up. It was his new party trick, and it was annoying in that endearing way that only Buck and Chris ever quite managed. 

(Eddie was biased. He knew it, and he felt no shame about it.)

At that moment, they were gathered in the living room. Abuela was in her chair with one of her shows playing on the television, Gavin using poor Hoover as a jungle gym at her feet. Chris was sitting at the coffee table, homework spread out in front of him. Except he wasn’t doing it, because Buck’s new party trick was apparently far too much of a distraction.

“What’s the square-root of one hundred sixty-two?” Chris asked, scientific calculator at the ready.

“It’s not a perfect square, so…” Buck’s face scrunched up in thought as he handed Eddie the bottle of beer he grabbed from the fridge. “Twelve point seven two seven. I think.”

“He’s right!” Chris excitedly exclaimed, leaning back to show the calculator to Eddie, who looked away from his phone to see it for himself. “This is awesome, Dad. Pops knows everything.”

“Uh-huh.” Eddie hoped the skepticism didn’t show itself too much in his tone. From the amused smile playing on Abuela’s lips, he figured she heard it well enough.

“Not everything,” Buck said, sitting down on the couch and pressing into his side. “Just the math stuff. Turns out, I kinda love it. Now that I can actually do it, I mean.”

“According to this,” Eddie said, angling his phone so his husband could see the screen. “People can get new cognitive abilities after a serious injury to the central nervous system.”

“Which I definitely had!”

“Right, right.” He nodded. “But some doctors are saying it isn’t real.”

“Do you really think,” Buck started, sounding almost offended, “that I've secretly known the square root of one hundred sixty-two all these years, and just been holding out? No, c’mon. It’s the lightning!”

Eddie narrowed his eyes at him, contemplating. Neither of them particularly liked helping Chris with his math homework, both of them having done weirder things to get out of it. Once, Buck looked him dead in the eye as he squirted ketchup all over himself, so he could lock himself in the bathroom and hide for the next hour. But that was because neither of them really got it, which led to much frustration for everyone. Or so had been the case until recently.

Also? Buck would have absolutely taken it as an opportunity to show off, as he’d been doing ever since he discovered his new mental calculator app.

“Fair point,” Eddie agreed. He watched as Buck blinked at the probably-too-quick agreement, before he went back to the article on his phone. “Okay, so this guy literally got the power of electricity after being struck. Said the lights would turn on and off every time he walked into a room.”

Buck twisted around and stared intently at the floor lamp in the corner that they hadn’t bothered turning on, before raising a hand and snapping his fingers. Nothing happened, and he pouted in disappointment. Eddie, as he was both legally and morally obligated to do, leaned over to kiss it away.

“I wish I got something like that,” Buck told him, sighing with all possible melodrama. “ESP, or the ability to see into the future. Oh! Or read people’s minds.”

“All of those sound awful.” 

“Maybe this is just the first phase,” Chris spoke up, his calculator lying abandoned on top of a math worksheet. Eddie caught sight of some of the problem, and frowned when he noticed that one of them was asking for the square root of 162. 12.727 was written beside it. Had he been…? “Maybe you’ll get more powers the stronger you get.”

“Yes!” Buck excitedly pointed at him, bouncing in his seat a little. “Like an actual superhero.”

“And Ravi’s even given you a name to go by,” Eddie said, grinning at the scowl that blossomed across his husband’s face.

“A superhero name?” Abuela asked, turning her head to face them. “What is it?”

Buck narrowed his eyes in warning.

“Don’t you dare…”

“The Mathematician,” Eddie answered, grinning at him. His husband, in turn, grabbed one of the throw pillows and smacked him with it. “Hey, watch it! You’ll make me spill my drink.”

Ravi had been merciless with the teasing, ever since the call to the spin class. Albert and Chimney had, of course, joined in the moment they first heard the new moniker. At one point, Eddie found them crowded together, brainstorming lyrics for “the Mathematician’s” theme song.

None of the words had been particularly flattering for Buck.

“Can the Mathematician give me the answers for my homework?” Chris asked, peering at them hopefully.

“No,” Eddie said, smiling at the answering groan of disappointment. “You won’t learn if he just gives you the answers.”

“But I do like being the guy with the answers,” Buck told him.

“Yeah, yeah.” 

He watched as Hoover stood up, apparently having had enough of his ears being yanked on, and padded away. Gavin stared after him for a moment, before grabbing the coffee table and using it to take shaky, unsteady steps towards the couch. Eddie sat his drink down and leaned over to sweep the toddler into his lap, making him giggle and squeal.

“Wanna learn a new song, little guy?” Eddie asked, smirking at Buck.

“A new- No, no, do not.”

“It goes like, Na na na na na na, Mathman- Ow! That was my kidney.”

“You deserved it for being an ass.”

Eddie glared at him, situating Gavin so his ears were covered.

“Language, mi sol.”

He didn’t need superpowers to see the throw pillow smacking him in the face.

Eddie never once claimed to be perfect. Sometimes he was a weak, weak man with the moral backbone of a chocolate éclair. He knew this, he accepted this, and maybe he should feel guilty, but… But it was just so easy. So easy, in fact, that it would almost be understandable if someone on the outside assumed they’d been playing the long game and were now setting the bait.

Except that Buck, bless his heart, was nearly incapable of such long-term duplicitousness. Which actually made it even easier, to be honest.

It started when they got into work that morning, and Buck was once again bragging about his newly acquired math skills. One of the others - Eddie was pretty sure it was Ortiz - finally called bullshit, claiming there was no way that Buck, of all people, was managing to solve math problems in his head, that he had to be wrong or cheating, somehow. To which Buck responded, quite expectedly, with,

“Wanna bet?”

It was easy. It was so, so easy. Because Buck wasn’t lying or cheating, he really did have new math-related abilities thanks to being struck by lightning, even though Eddie still wasn’t entirely certain how that was possible. It wasn’t like the contents of a math textbook had been transferred through the power of electricity. Did it just do something so that Buck could actually recall all the math classes he couldn’t consciously remember?

Whatever, didn’t matter. What mattered was that all of their coworkers were used to pre-lightning Buck, the one who needed the calculator app on his phone to solve even the most basic of problems - not that Eddie was any better - and so they simply could not believe this new turn of events. And that, in turn, made them very easy marks.

“Wanna bet?” Buck asked Ortiz and the rest, and they did! They put actual money on the line that Buck was faking the ability to do math. Problem after problem, they threw at him, and he solved each and every one.

Which is how they got to this point, Buck leading a gaggle of his coworkers across the loft as he rattled off the solution to the latest of equations they’d thrown at him. One of the others had taken up the responsibility of double checking it on their phone, because they didn’t trust Eddie not to cheat if he was the one doing it. Which was definitely fair, but it was just insulting enough to ease the tiny amount of guilt he had for taking their money.

“Pay up, pay up,” Eddie demanded, shouldering his way to the front, hand held out so they could give him what Buck had earned. Even now, some of them were in various stages of shock and disbelief and disgruntledness. Which also made his guilt less of a problem, because if they were so stupid to keep betting actual money against his husband’s proven skills, then they were too stupid to be trusted with their finances.

Buck was grinning as he helped collect the winnings, which was also a huge benefit to this… Was it a scam? They weren’t scamming their coworkers, because a scam implied a lie, and neither of them were lying about anything. A scheme, it was a scheme. Buck was grinning, which was another benefit of this scheme, because Buck hadn’t been properly smiling for weeks now.

(Eddie did not know what Buck and Copeland had talked about, that session Buck asked to speak to her alone. And they still hadn’t talked about why Buck was seeing Dr. Weaver next week, if it wasn’t about finally getting an ADHD diagnosis.

Eddie did not know what was going on in his husband’s head, anymore, and he was worried.

Perhaps that superpower didn’t sound so awful, after all.)

“Easy money,” Buck commented, counting the winnings he’d collected.

“A fool and his money are soon,” Eddie yanked the money out of Buck’s hand, “Parted.”

He smiled prettily at the offended expression on his husband’s face.

“Could be plenty more to come,” Buck bragged, narrowly avoiding banging his head on a wooden beam that had been there for literal years. There was cushioning on that section. How many times had Eddie’s husband nearly given himself a concussion, before someone put that up? “I’m a savant now.”

Chimney said something that had Bobby laughing, but they were both over in the kitchen area, so Eddie couldn’t really hear what it was.

“I feel like I should be putting a stop to this,” the captain said, raising his voice.

“Everyone in this station has a gambling problem, Cap,” Eddie told him, sitting at the dining table. “Except us. We have absolutely no problem with it.”

“Uh-oh,” Ravi said, popping up from the couches. “Is the Mathematician using his powers for evil? Quick, someone alert the Justice League- Hey! Cap, help!”

Buck had bounded over and wrapped an arm around his neck, and was now in the process of giving the younger man a noogie.

“Okay, I should definitely put a stop to that,” Bobby commented, though he made absolutely no move to break it up.

All of this, the betting scheme and the talk about gambling and using Buck’s powers for evil, was giving Eddie an idea. A wonderful idea that would allow him to show Buck off like the man deserved. Because while he may not be as sociable as his husband, Eddie did manage to keep up with some of the going-ons around the station. He just needed…

“Hey, do either of you have Rosen’s number?” Eddie asked. Chimney and Bobby both paused in the process of carrying food to the table.

“Julie Rosen?” Chimney asked, glancing at Bobby. “From B-shift?”

“Yeah.”

“I do,” the paramedic said, pulling out his phone. Eddie figured he would, after working on B-shift for most of last year. “Do we get to know why you need her number?”

“Nope.”

Afterwards, he hurried downstairs for privacy, popping outside and dialing. Julie Rosen answered with a somewhat irritated,

“Yes?”

“Hey, it’s Diaz. Eddie.”

“The quiet one.” That took him off guard a little. “What do you want?”

“Is there room at the table this week…?”

That Lady Gaga Song

Chapter Summary

In which Buck and Eddie have a lovely night. And then Buck opens his mouth.

Chapter Notes

See the end of the chapter for notes

The last thing Buck was expecting when Hen and Chimney got back from transporting a patient to the hospital, was Denny Wilson hopping out of the ambulance.

They’d been called to a salon where some dude cut hair with fire. Apparently, his client at the time - a teenage girl skipping school with her friend - freaked out when he came at her with a small blowtorch. Totally understandable in Buck’s mind, because who in the world would be cool with fire that close to their head? Definitely not him, nuh-uh. He’d seen the results of mixing hair products with open flames, several times. 

Anyways, one thing led to another. A trash can caught on fire, the sprinkler system went off, minor chaos ensued. The last Buck had seen of Hen and Chimney, they were transporting the girl to the hospital, because she was allergic to water and was covered in hives thanks to the sprinklers. And apparently, at some point, they’d picked someone up.

“Go up there,” Hen ordered, almost snapping at her son. “Sit down and get started on whatever homework you have.”

“Uh. Hi, D.” Buck’s greeting barely got a grunt in response and he stepped out of the way as Denny stomped up the stairs, not nearly as excited about being there as he used to get. “Shouldn’t he be at home?”

“Yes,” Hen ground out. “He should be.”

He glanced at Chimney for help figuring out what, precisely, was going on. But the other man raised his hands to ward off his unasked questions, before practically fleeing up the stairs.

“Is he okay?” Eddie asked, having come down with Buck when they spotted the ambulance pulling in.

“For now,” Hen answered, before taking a breath, visibly trying to calm herself down. “Karen called while we were about to leave the hospital, because Denny wasn’t home. The bus didn’t drop him off, and according to his friends, he hasn’t been riding it for months. ”

“How was he getting home, then?”

“His father,” she practically spat out

Buck let out a low whistle, before sharing a look with Eddie. Denny’s father - Nathaniel, if Buck remembered correctly - hadn’t known about his son until Eva told him, using him in her spiteful attempt to take the boy away from Hen and Karen, years ago. At the time, Buck knew, the Wilsons had offered the man the opportunity to get to know his son, but the man never actually reached out. And because of that, Buck was pretty sure that Nathaniel had lost any and all legal rights as a parent..

But that wasn’t the important question. The important question was-

“So how did Denny end up with you?” Eddie asked before Buck could.

“He was with Nathaniel.” Ha! So he did remember right. “Apparently, he picked him up from school, and they got t-boned at an intersection. Denny’s fine, but the driver’s side got hit pretty hard. Nathaniel might need surgery.”

“Do we…” Buck glanced at Eddie. “Do we send well wishes or threats?”

“I want,” Hen said, “to strangle him.”

“Which one?” Eddie asked.

“Yes.”

The two of them shared another look, and then Buck reached out to pat her on the shoulder.

“C’mon, you should probably tell Cap that we have a temporary recruit,” he said, turning her and guiding her towards the captain’s office. Ravi and Albert were both upstairs, as was Chimney, so Denny wasn’t being left to his own devices. “I mean, our shift is nearly over, but still.”

“What did you guys do when it was Chris doing this? Sneaking around and lying about where he was?” she asked, and then dragged her hands down her face in despair. “Oh God, I’m asking Buck for parenting advice.”

“I’m choosing to not be offended by that.”

“We grounded him, if you’ll recall,” Eddie told her. “What was it you said at the time? I think it was, ‘kids crave privacy’ and ‘half the thrill is thinking you’re getting away with it.’”

Buck did not actually remember her giving them those nuggets of wisdom, but to be fair, he’d been in a daze from Kameron and Connor wanting to have his baby. He figured he could be forgiven for not being fully there for the conversation at the time.

“No more privacy for Denny,” Hen said, shaking her head. “He can crave it all he wants, we’re going full orwellian on him. He’ll be lucky if we don’t tag him like an endangered bird.”

“See, when I suggested doing that with Chris, someone acted as if I was the irrational one,” Eddie said, slanting a glance at Buck, who rolled his eyes.

“He was in a car crash today and I only know about it because I ran into him at the hospital,” she said, working herself up again as they got to the open office door. “What if I hadn’t been there? What if he’d actually been hurt?”

“If who’d been hurt?” Bobby asked, turning away from his computer to frown at them in obvious concern.

As one, Buck and Eddie gave Hen a gentle push into the room, before giving sloppy salutes and happily leaving the two of them to it.

“I’m asking Buck for parenting advice,” Buck mocked when they were far enough away that he wouldn’t be overheard, wagging his head back and forth as he spoke. “Egads, the horror, the shame.”

“I thought you were choosing not to be offended?”

“I changed my mind.”

Eddie let out an ugly snort of amusement, before wrapping fingers around his wrist and dragging him across the bay to the stairs.

“C’mon,” he said. “Let’s go make sure Denny looks properly miserable for when Hen’s done venting to Bobby.”

“Yeah, okay.”

Eddie leaned against the doorway to their bedroom, watching Buck get ready. He himself was already dressed in the suit he’d been bullied into buying last fall, while his husband’s shirt was still open and untucked, the red velvet jacket laying on the bed. His phone was beside it, with Maddie on speaker.

“I cannot believe,” Buck was whining, “that you’re having Ma and Pops over for dinner to help you with this little mystery, but not me.”

Apparently, some woman claiming to be a neighbor had shown up on Maddie and Chimney’s doorstep. She’d brought baked goods and made plans for a playdate between her son and Jee-Yun. Except she apparently lied about her name, and about where she lived, so there were some minor concerns about her motives.

“It was Howie that invited them,” Maddie retorted.

“Still!”

“Why are you complaining?” Eddie asked, breaking in. “We have our own plans.”

Buck turned at the sound of his voice, and Eddie caught his eyes flitting over him and his tongue peaking out to wet his lips. Eddie smirked and pushed off the doorframe, sauntering across the distance between them.

“Plans?”

“Uh, yeah,” Buck stammered, tearing his eyes away. “But Eddie hasn’t told me what they are. Somewhere fancy, though. He’s got me dressing up.”

“I figured it was my turn to surprise him,” Eddie told her, reaching out and doing up the buttons that his husband hadn’t even started on, yet. “It’s not a wedding, but…”

Maddie giggled on the other end of the line, and Buck rolled his eyes.

“I hope you have fun on your date, then,” his sister-in-law said. “It sounds like Athena and Bobby are here.”

“Tell me what Ma has to say about your fake neighbor!” Buck practically ordered.

“I will, I will,” Maddie promised. “Love you both, have fun!”

The phone screen lit up to tell them the call was ended.

“C’mon,” Eddie said, stepping back after he finished buttoning the shirt and ignoring his husband’s slight pout. “Finish getting dressed, we need to get going.”

“Do we have reservations?” Buck asked, hurriedly tucking in his shirt and grabbing the jacket.

“Something like that,” he answered, purposefully being vague and mysterious just to annoy the other man. He was being glared at, now, so it was working. “Come on.”

They headed for the front door, Buck pulling his jacket on as they went. Abuela was in the living room, sitting in her chair, while the boys were on the couch. Gavin kept bouncing and clapping his hands at whatever was happening on the television screen. Hoover lifted his head to look at him, before curling back up on his doggy bed at the end of the couch.

“Oh, muy guapo,” Abuela commented.

“Yeah, looking snazzy,” Chris agreed.

“You’re sure you’re okay for this?” Eddie asked, pausing by his abuela’s chair while Buck went to smack noisy kisses on the boys. Gavin giggled at it, while Chris complained and did nothing to escape. The decision to go out tonight had been a rushed one, so Carla wasn’t there to help.

“Si, si,” she said, waving his concerns away. “I am old, not incapable. I can watch the boys for the time you’re gone. Maybe I’ll call Pepa over, maybe not.”

Eddie wished he had the foresight to ask his tia himself, but again, rushed decision. He knew his abuela was perfectly capable of watching a twelve year old and a toddler for a few hours, but he still worried. They really needed to get her a LifeAlert, just for his own peace of mind.

Abuela shooed them out the door, and they headed for the truck, since Eddie was the one who knew where they were headed. Buck paused at the passenger-side door, in the middle of shrugging his suit jacket on, and cocked his head as he stared across the road.

“The For Sale sign is gone,” he commented.

The house across from them was put up for sale back in February, the timing of which had Buck complaining. If it had gone up just a couple months earlier, he said, then perhaps they could have convinced Maddie and Chimney to get that one, instead of the Not a Murder House. Across the street from them, instead of across the city.

“Guess we’re getting new neighbors, then,” Eddie said.

“So what are we doing tonight?” Buck asked, as they both hopped into the truck.

“I got us seats for a poker game.”

The look on his face was priceless.

The game was being held, as it always was, at Don Rivera’s, a Mexican grill. They parked in the back and made their way to the delivery entrance in the back, Eddie texting Rosen that they’d arrived. Buck was looking decidedly uneasy about everything, anxiously adjusting the cuffs on his jacket.

“Are we sure about this?” he asked.

“Mi sol, relax,” Eddie said, nudging him with his elbow. “It’s a good place to put your new skills to use. Maybe we can use ‘em in Vegas afterward. We never had our honeymoon.”

“This is not gonna end well,” Buck grumbled.

“Don’t be such a downer. It’s a low-stakes game.”

He knocked on the door and there was the buzz of an alarm turning off a moment later. It swung open to reveal Julie Rossen, dressed up in a teal cocktail dress.

“Don’t make me regret this, Diazes,” she said, turning and leading the way inside, not even waiting to see if they followed. The kitchen they were cutting through was bustling with activity, and they had to step around cooks during their jobs.

“Eds,” Buck said, and when Eddie turned to look, he was eyeing the place up. “You didn’t bring me to a mob establishment, did you?”

“Different kind of family.”

Their destination was in the restaurant proper. There was a large poker table set up in front of the bar, with Battalion Chief Williams and Mehta, the 133’s captain, already sitting..

“The one and only One Eighteen makes its presence known,” Williams commented.

“You make it sound like our house has never been here before,” he said, pulling a chair out for Buck before taking his own seat. His husband was still looking unsure about things. “I know Rosen here’s a regular.”

“True, true,” the chief conceded. “I’m just not used to having so many of you present. Not to mention an honest-to-goodness legend.”

“I still can’t believe you survived that,” Mehta said, and then followed it with a rushed, “Although we’re all glad you did.”

“Thanks.” Buck’s reply was subdued, though he smiled at Eddie when he hooked their ankles together.

“Let’s just see if he survives tonight,” Williams said, as the cards were dealt and the chips parsed out.

Eddie glanced at his own cards, a nine and a four, both hearts. Two pairs, with what was on the board. But his cards weren’t the ones that mattered. He was only planning to sit a couple rounds, before bowing out and taking his proper place as this night’s arm candy.

At the end of the table, Williams smiled to herself as she placed one of her chips in the middle.

“Did you see anything?” Mehta asked. “A bright light?”

“I saw that while I was still on the ladder,” Buck answered, not really looking his way.

The captain hummed to himself, before throwing out his own chips with a,

“I’ll go in for three.”

“Why not?” Rosen said, putting in her own bet. “I’m in this far.”

“I’ll raise you two,” Eddie said, which Buck followed with a shrug and a,

“Sure, why not?”

“Call,” Williams said, matching their bets, with Mehta following along.

“Alright, two pair,” Eddie told the table, placing down his cards. “Nines and fours.”

“Two pair, queens high,” Williams countered, looking smug. Mehta tossed his cards down in disgust; he clearly didn’t have anything to beat it.

“Straight,” Buck said, revealing his own cards, a five and a six. “To the seven.”

Oh yes, Eddie thought, taking in how the battalion chief’s face froze in shock. Tonight was going to be so very good to them. Except there were two problems that he really, really should have foreseen. One: Buck had no poker face, and two: the people at this table were not the gullible idiots at the station.

Which was made evident a couple rounds later.

“I’ve heard people that get struck by lightning sometimes develop special skills,” Williams casually commented as she placed her bet.

“Uh, skills?” Buck was not casual. Buck was visibly nervous. “Like what? Woodworking?”

“High-functioning stuff, like languages or music,” the chief said, staring at him. “Or math. Like counting.”

Oh, shit.

“I’m out,” Eddie answered, drawing the heat of her attention off his husband. He threw his cards down and leaned back. “Too rich for my blood.”

“Yeah, no.” Rosen was shaking her head. “Math is not Buck’s thing. Everyone at the station knows that.”

Julie Rosen might have just earned herself a cut of the profits.

Eddie stood back with a beer and watched as his husband played round after round, the mound of chips in front of him growing. This was a very good idea. There was nothing better than watching Buck literally flushed with victory, and smiling bigger and brighter than he had in weeks.

Rosen bowed out after a couple more rounds, joining Eddie on the sidelines and quietly mocking him for the lovestruck expressions he was apparently making. Not that he paid much attention to it, because he was too busy struggling with the temptation to just climb into Buck’s lap. Or to drag him out of his seat and into the bathroom.

Mehta lasted a few more rounds, and Williams longer still, but it was almost an inevitability for Buck to be the last man sitting, leaning back in his chair and looking so deliciously smug. Eddie couldn’t resist going over and slapping a kiss on him, right over his birthmark.

“It looks like quite the haul,” Williams said, and at least she didn’t sound too upset about the way things turned out. “How do you want your winnings?”

The answer to that question was apparently, “in food.” Williams led them back into the kitchens, where she had one of the chefs start wrapping up expensive cuts of steak. They both ignored Buck’s pun - “You were right, it was a low steaks game!” - as she led them to the door.

“You’re always welcome back,” she told Eddie, and then she pointed at Buck. “He gets left at home. At least until his new superpowers wear off.”

And then she let the door close in their faces.

The ride back home was quiet, the radio on but turned down low as the both of them basked in their victory. Eddie was riding high on a mission well accomplished, with the steaks in the backseat and Buck’s hand in his, and they were going home to a full house. It was a good night, the best night they’d both had since the lightning strike, and he was hoping to ride the high of it for at least a few more days.

And then Buck opened his mouth.

“Copeland thinks I might be passively suicidal.”

Chapter End Notes

The author knows nothing about poker, and now regrets not tossing the scene out with the bathwater.

It Was a Good Night

Chapter Summary

In which there's talking.

It had been such a good night. Perhaps the best night in weeks. 

Being able to show off? Being the center of attention, not because he’d messed up or was broken or hurt, but because he was doing something well? Buck’d had fun. It had been a wonderful night, even if he started it uncertain and unsure and not a little anxious. But after that first round, with the shocked look on Williams’ face and the pure adoration on Eddie’s, all of that had gone away. It all went away and he lost himself in the sheer enjoyment of kicking other people’s asses at a game he always, always lost at.

He’d had so much fun, and it had been such a wonderful night - even if the battalion chief totally called him out afterwards - and both he and Eddie were riding high on it on the way home.

So of course Buck had to go and ruin it.

He wasn’t sure why he chose that moment to blurt it out. Perhaps he was being his self-destructive self again. Perhaps he was just feeling too comfortable. Perhaps he was a little bit tipsy from the drinks at the poker game. Regardless of the why, it was still done. He opened his mouth and blurted out,

“Copeland thinks I might be passively suicidal.”

Eddie didn’t say anything at first. His initial reaction was to hit the brakes, and it was a good thing they were already coming up to a stoplight, because he hit them hard enough to send Buck jerking forward. Then Eddie turned his head and looked at him, a conflict of emotions on his face. He simply stared at him, lips parted a bit.

When the light turned green again, and the car behind them blared its horn, Eddie finally faced forward again. Buck heard him hit the truck’s turn signal, and then they were pulling into the parking lot of a store, coming to a haphazard stop between parking spaces. The silence when the engine was turned off was almost deafening.

And still Eddie didn’t say anything. He was half-turned in his seat, staring at Buck with his brow furrowed. Several times he opened his mouth, like he was about to speak, only to close it again. Buck sat there and watched his husband watch him, all those feel good feelings of the night burnt away as his anxiety flared back up.

Finally, finally, just as Buck was contemplating hopping out of the truck and running away - just into the store, swear - Eddie spoke.

“Copeland thinks what?” he asked, voice sounded a bit strangled.

“Um,” Buck answered, oh so eloquently. “She, uh. She thinks that…

“That you want to kill yourself,” Eddie finished for him, except that hadn’t been what he said.

“No. She thinks I’m passively suicidal. Maybe. Possibly.”

“She maybe, possibly thinks this? Or she thinks you maybe, possibly want to kill yourself? Passively.”

“Yes?”

“Buck,” he said. “Please.”

Buck chewed on the inside of his lip, regretting ever-so-much the way he just threw himself into this conversation. There was no way this was going to end well, not that he could see.

“Daniel tried to stop me from waking up, in my dream,” Buck said, looking out the windshield instead of at Eddie. “When I was about to open the door, he got in the way. He… He asked me if I wouldn’t rather stay, when I knew - when he knew - that staying meant not waking up. That not waking up meant dying. He asked if it wouldn’t be better if I was the one that left first, for once.”

“You didn’t tell me about that part.” Eddie’s voice wasn’t accusing. He was just stating a fact.

“I know,” Buck said, nodding. “I didn’t tell anyone, not until I told Copeland.”

“So, because of a dream, she…?”

“It was me,” he insisted. “It was me arguing to stay, arguing to- To let myself die. It might have been Daniel saying it in the dream, but it was my dream, my brain.”

“Okay. And because of this, Copeland thinks you want to- To-”

“Not jump in front of the bus,” Buck said. “But maybe I wouldn’t… Maybe sometimes I wouldn’t hurry to get out of the way of it, either. Passive, not active.”

“So you don’t want to live,” Eddie said, voicing worryingly flat and dull, and Buck was quick to shake his head.

“I want to grow old with you,” he said, meaning every word of it. “And I wanted to wake up. But- But a part of me didn’t want to wake up. And sometimes…”

“Is this new?” his husband asked. “Since the lightning strike?”

“You know it’s not,” Buck answered, finally turning to look at him. Eddie’s face was a facade of cracked and crumbling stone. “We’ve talked about this before, even if… Even if we weren’t talking about it.”

“He had everything that he thought he wanted,” Eddie said, quoting Buck’s own words from so long ago. “And he still wasn’t happy.”

“I am happy with you,” Buck told him, much as he had that awful night. “You make me happy. But sometimes… Sometimes I’m just… Not happy, for no reason. I'm a mess, babe.”

“I knew that when I kissed you,” the other man told him, reaching over the center console and gripping his hand. “Is this why you’re seeing Dr. Weaver next week?”

“Yeah. Copeland’s not, y’know, that kind of doctor. Weaver can actually diagnose things.”

“Okay,” Eddie said, taking a breath and letting it out. And then, “You threw yourself in front of a drunk driver.”

“I did.”

“That doesn’t sound very passive.”

“I know.”

“And this isn’t new.”

“I guess not,” Buck hedged, shrugging. “I just hadn’t really… Thought about it like this, I guess.”

Eddie simply nodded. His free hand was resting on the steering wheel, index finger tapping, and Buck could practically see the man’s mind working. Eddie was no doubt thinking back on everything, looking at it all through the new filter he’d been given, much as Buck had done after he realized the implications of that part of his dream.

The tapping stopped, finger frozen as it hovered above the steering wheel. The hand holding Buck’s tightened.

“Buck,” Eddie said. “ Evan, did you… Your motorcycle, was that…?”

Buck did not answer, and that was answer enough. When he looked, Eddie’s eyes were shut tight and his jaw was clenched.

“Okay,” the other man said again. “Okay. Okay.”

“I’m sorry,” Buck said. Eddie’s eyes snapped open to stare at him incredulously.

“You’re-? Chingada madre, why are you apologizing!?”

“I don’t know. It seemed appropriate.”

“You’re an idiot,” his husband told him, sounding so fond it almost made Buck ache. The responding squawk of offense was interrupted by his phone. “Abuela?”

“No,” Buck said, shaking his head as he answered it. “Hey, Mads.”

“Hey,” she answered. “Everything okay? I didn’t interrupt anything, did I?”

“No, no, we’re just on our way home,” he told her. Eddie squeezed his hand before letting go. The truck rumbled to life a moment later and they were soon pulling back out. “What’s up? How did dinner go? What did Ma say about your fake neighbor?”

“She thinks the woman might’ve been casing the place,” his sister said. “They’re going to set up a sting, for when she’s supposed to come back.”

“Oh, sounds exciting.”

“I just want to feel comfortable in my house again. What about you guys? I thought you’d be home by now, or else I wouldn’t have called.”

“Eddie took me to a poker game.”

There was a beat of silence on the other end of the line, which was quickly followed by his sister’s laughter. In the background, Buck could hear Chimney say something, but he couldn’t quite make the words out.

“Yeah, yeah, laugh it up,” he said, not feeling offended at all.

“I’m- I’m sorry.” Maddie did not sound very sincere, especially with the way she was still laughing.

“No you’re not.”

“You’re right, I’m not. How much did you lose?”

“It was a low steaks game,” Buck told her, smirking as he watched Eddie roll his eyes. This was a much better topic of conversation than the one they’d been having. “But I’ll have you know, I won.”

“Uh-huh.”

“I did! I’m even banned from future games, now.”

“I don’t believe you.”

“That’s fine,” he said. “That just means you don’t get any of the porterhouses.”

“Porter-? Oh my God, low steaks.” Maddie let out a pained groan. “I hate you.”

“Impossible, I’m too lovable.”

“So you won steaks at a poker game?”

“She asked me how I wanted my winnings,” Buck said with a shrug, even though his sister couldn’t see it. “I saw them on our way in and I wasn’t about to pass up the opportunity. Porterhouses are expensive.”

“Don’t you have house payments? And children to feed?”

“And they’re gonna be fed well.”

They talked for a bit longer, hanging up just as the truck pulled into the driveway. The porch light was left on for them, though the house was silent when they went in. They checked on Chris, who was still up and who let out an annoyed groan when they reminded him about school in the morning, and they checked on Gavin, who was fast asleep in his crib. Hoover lifted up his head from where he was curled up in the corner of Gavin’s room, just long enough to get pets and scratches as his tail thumped against the floor, before laying back down.

“I told you,” Abuela said, stepping out of her room, already dressed for bed. “I am not incapable. You and Pepa, you think I’m just going to break at the slightest breeze.”

“Perdón, perdón,” Eddie said, raising his hands. “We just worry.”

“Well, don’t,” she said, sniffing haughtily. “Did you two have a good evening?”

“We did,” Buck answered with a glance at his husband. Until he ruined it, at least.

“Good, then-” Abuela let out a yawn, shook her head. “Bed. Buenas noches.”

“Buenas noches,” he and Eddie chorused as she went back into her bedroom, closing the door behind her. 

They followed her example, heading into their own room. Except Eddie didn’t seem to be in any hurry to get out of his suit, beyond taking off and throwing his jacket onto the bed. He sat down in the chair they kept in the corner, one that had been in the living room of the old house. He dragged his hands down his face, looking so very stressed and tired, and Buck was struck with the urge to apologize again.

“So I guess we’re not done talking about it,” he said instead.

“The last time we didn’t talk about something,” Eddie said, “I ended up accusing you of cheating on me.”

“Yeah, but… I don’t know what else there is to talk about, Eds. My brain’s fucked up, I’m seeing a doctor next week.”

“I don’t want to make the same mistake Maddie and Chimney did,” his husband told him. Buck looked away as he sat down on the end of the bed, choosing to instead look at the little bonsai tree someone gifted Eddie forever ago and which everyone else had been making a concerted effort to keep him from killing.

“Well, if there’s another citywide black out, it’s not like you’ll have to worry about me being home alone for all of it.”

“Buck.” Eddie apparently did not appreciate his flippant tone.

Buck let out a sigh.

“You think I should tell everyone else?”

“I think you should at least think about it,” Eddie said. “Maybe not everything, maybe not…”

He trailed off, and Buck turned in time to catch him with his eyes once again scrunched closed and his jaw clenched.

“I definitely think you should tell Maddie,” Eddie said after a moment, opening his eyes.

“Yeah. Yeah, okay.” Buck said the words reluctantly, dropping his gaze again. “But the others, I… I don’t know.”

“Why not?”

“I just…” He fumbled for the words. “I don’t know.”

Eddie let out a sigh and pushed himself out of the chair, moving to sit next to Buck instead. He wrapped an arm around him and pulled him close, and Buck let his head flop down against his shoulder.

“Like I said, you don’t have to tell them everything. You can just tell them whatever Weaver says, next week.”

“Alright,” Buck said, however reluctantly. “Thanks for taking me out tonight. I had fun.”

“Good,” Eddie said, bending his neck to kiss at Buck’s birthmark. “I’m glad. That’s all I wanted from tonight.”

They stayed sitting for a bit longer, leaning on and pressed against each other, but they eventually had to get up and get ready for bed, changing out of their fancy clothes and jostling each other in front of the bathroom sink as they brushed their teeth. After checking on Chris again, to make sure he was actually winding down, they slid under the covers.

They fell asleep the same way they had every night since Buck got out of the hospital: With Eddie twisted around him, ear pressed against Buck’s chest.

Buzz Buzz

Chapter Summary

In which there is an embarrassing accident, and Buck puts his foot in it.

Chapter Notes

See the end of the chapter for notes

Eddie, somehow, managed to not freak out either during or after that awful, horrible conversation with Buck. He was proud of himself for not verbalizing his inner monologue of “what the fuck what the fuck what the actual fuck.” He stayed calm, he didn’t panic - which, of course he didn’t, because he doesn’t panic.

The closest he came to panicking, if he did that sort of thing, was when he thought back on everything he knew, now cast in the bright light of revelations. All of Buck’s stupid stunts in the years they’d known each other, like the bicycle and the drunk driver, like when he climbed the crane during the sniper mess, and the argument after the train wreck with Abby’s fiance. Or during that fire at the factory, when Buck got separated from them, when they all rushed in to find him collapsed on his knees, surrounded by flames and giving up.

That wasn’t what had Eddie (nearly) panicking, though. That memory simply had his mind jumping to the start of that shift, to Buck standing in the loft telling them all of how he used to get himself hurt for attention. The story of Buck crashing his motorcycle, when he got kicked out of school and he was angry at himself and dreading facing his parents and-

Oh, that story took an even darker twist now, didn’t it?

Especially when, after Eddie asked Buck directly, the other man didn’t give him an answer.

Eddie didn’t freak out during the conversation, however much he wanted to. Nor did he freak out at all during the night, one spent clinging to Buck as it slowly dawned how close they’d come to a world where they never met. That wasn’t to say Eddie didn’t freak out at all, however, because he did. He just did it the next morning, while Buck was getting Gavin changed and dressed, behind a locked door and with the shower running.

The day after the poker game, after that god awful revelation, they had a twelve-hour overnight shift. Eddie used to hate those shifts, used to hate having to get ready for work in the early evening, especially when they were on a weekday. Because what was the point of a half-day if the half of the day he got to spend at home was the same half Chris spent at school? He still wasn’t fond of them, but they weren’t quite so damning now that one son was practically a teenager, hiding himself away in his room instead of spending time with his fathers, and the other wasn’t even in preschool yet.

But today, though? Today it gave Eddie the time to freak out and then prepare himself for hours spent stuck with the others, knowing that he couldn’t say anything to them without betraying Buck’s trust. It gave him time to freak out - in the shower, where Buck wouldn’t hear or see - and then be as clingy as he wanted to be. And Eddie knew he was being clingy, but Buck didn’t seem to mind, and he was being just as clingy, besides.

(Abuela gave them a Look at one point, which was how Eddie knew for certain that they were being clingier than usual, but she didn’t say anything about it.)

Their shift started as the sun was setting, casting everything in hazy gold. They entered the station holding hands, and it was with much reluctance that Eddie let go while they put their bags away. He knew full well that it was going to be a daunting shift, a shift that was going to be spent pretending that everything was Perfectly Fine, Thanks, and one where he’ll spend the time dreading every alarm. Because what if this time, on this call, Buck decided not to get out of the bus’s way?

Nothing changed since yesterday, Eddie knew. This has always been the reality of things. But now he knew about it, now he knew that every stunt was a game of chicken with Buck’s own mortality. And that changed everything.

Eddie also knew that if he started acting differently, that if he started treating Buck differently and tried to hold him back and shelter him, the other man would snap and snarl. It was doubtful that things would escalate as they had when Bobby tried the same, but it would still be a messy and painful affair.

The others were already gathered around one of the tables in the station loft, when the two of them climbed the stairs. Buck threw himself into a seat and cheerfully asked,

“So what’s the update with Denny?”

“He spent last night and today holding a hunger strike,” was Hen’s tired answer. “Ma came over and talked to him.”

“How did that go?” Albert asked, picking at a bowl of pretzels that was sitting between him and Ravi.

“She got him to eat. Beyond that, I don’t know yet.”

“Enough about that, though,” Chimney said, leaning across the table. “I heard that the Diazes spent last night at a poker game.”

“You mean you overheard Maddie on the phone with me,” Buck retorted. Eddie couldn’t see it, because the man had his back to him, but he knew that the words were accompanied by an eyeroll.

“Chief Williams sends her regards, by the way,” Eddie told Bobby, as he finally took a seat at the table as well.

“And then she banned me from coming back.”

“That’s what happens when you use your powers for crime,” Ravi said, nodding sagely, and then nearly falling out of his chair as he ducked to avoid a swipe from Buck. It was only through Albert’s quick reflexes that the bowl of pretzels wasn’t overturned.

“He used his powers to get steaks,” Chimney told the others.

“Porterhouses,” Buck proudly clarified.

“How many?” Bobby asked.

“Enough for Sunday.”

“Good man.”

The shift that night was turning out to be one of those that was busy, but not with anything labor intensive. No big traffic accident, no minor fire, nothing that would require pulling the trucks in and out. Not yet, anyway. Instead, they were mostly medical calls, with the ambulances being sent out again almost as soon as they were pulling back in.

Eddie and Buck were sent with Hen for one call, while Albert and Chimney were out with the other ambulance. Dispatch wasn’t able to say what was wrong, only that a man reported his wife as being “injured” and was reluctant to explain further, because he didn’t want it recorded. So it was a sex call, almost definitely. The only question was what kind of sex call.

“Slipped and fell in the shower,” was Buck’s guess. Which was a safe guess, because they’d responded to several of those over the years.

“Got too adventurous and pulled a muscle,” was Eddie’s.

“You two are both ridiculous, and I cannot believe you’re trying to guess how this woman hurt herself,” Hen said, shaking her head at them. She was trying for stern and judgmental, but ruined the effect by following it up with, “Dispatch said it was internal, she probably tore something.”

When they got there, the husband was quick to answer the door. He didn’t look like someone who’d gotten frantically dressed after falling in the shower, so that was probably Buck’s guess taken out of the running, then. 

“Thank goodness. Please come in,” he said, letting them into the house. “I was afraid they wouldn’t send anyone.”

He was leading them towards the sound of a woman in distress. A lot of distress. Just what kind of sex-gone-wrong call was this?

“You told dispatch your wife was injured?” Hen said, trying to get more information.

“She’s, uh… This way.” He was still embarrassed to explain, apparently. “I wanted to take her to the ER, but every time I tried to move her…”

They ended up in what Eddie figured was the living room, going off the furniture. A woman was sitting on the leather couch, hunched over and clutching her stomach. Also fully dressed. Maybe they were wrong and it wasn’t a sex-related injury?

But then why was the man so embarrassed about it?

“Oh, no,” the woman groaned. “You called Nine One One?”

Correction: Why were both of them so embarrassed about it, if it wasn’t sex-related?

“I told you I was going to.”

“I told you not to!”

“Hi, I’m Hen,” the paramedic interrupted, kneeling in front of the woman. “What’s your name?”

“Patsy.”

“Okay, Patsy. Can you describe the pain for me?”

“It’s kind of a stabbing, combined with some shooting. It gets worse when I mo-” She interrupted herself with a pained yell. “When I move.”

And now there was buzzing. A loud buzzing, that had them all looking around for the culprit.

“What is that?” Hen asked.

“I swear to God,” the man finally burst out with. “I don’t know what happened. We were having a romantic evening, and then…”

“Okay, let’s have you lie back, so I can figure out what’s going on here.” Hen urged Patsy to lay down, an act accomplished with much pained exertion and yet more buzzing. The paramedic grabbed her stethoscope, listening and feeling around the poor woman’s abdomen. Whatever she found, it had her eyebrows rocketing sky-high. “The buzzing’s coming from… Inside the house.”

Ohhhhhhh.

“I get you were having a romantic evening, sir.” And judging from all the lit candles scattered around the room, Eddie figured they were close to being called for an entirely different kind of mishap. “But what exactly were the two of you doing when the pain started.”

“We were, you know,” the husband stammered. “Being intimate?”

“Just the two of you? The old-fashioned way?”

Buck let out a snort behind him, and Eddie blindly reached back to smack him.

“I may have purchased something newfangled,” the husband told them, lifting up a chain necklace. It looked like something was supposed to be hanging off it, but it had been removed so that only the base of whatever it was remained. “To uh, increase her pleasure.”

“Oh, like a-” Buck’s statement was interrupted by the woman’s pained scream, but the husband nodded, obviously filling in the blank.

“Yes, yeah. We were having a… A really good time,” he said. “She seemed happy, so I was happy. I was, uh. I was using it on… On the outside. And I lost it. That’s when the screaming started?”

“I think it’s moved into her bladder,” Hen announced.

Ouch. Wait, if he was using it on the outside, and it ended up in her bladder, then… Did it go through her urethra? Ay, Dios.

“I’ll get the morphine,” Eddie said, unslinging his own medical bag from his shoulder.

“Can you get it out?” Patsy asked.

“Not on this couch,” was Hen’s answer. “They’ll do it at the ER.”

“And tell this humiliating story to new people? Great!”

“If it makes you feel any better,” Eddie said, getting the morphine drip set up. “They’ve all heard it before.”

(“And then!” Maddie cried, red faced and laughing, four glasses of wine deep. “He tried to tell us that he tripped over backwards and fell on it!”)

“Thank you,” Patsy said. “It doesn’t.”

Fair enough.

“I’m sorry, honey,” the husband said. “I just wanted to make sure you were happy.”

Patsy gave a disgusted little shake of her head, and the husband turned to Buck for reassurance.

“So this could happen to anyone?” he asked.

“Uh, well, um.” Buck floundered for what to say. “Listen, they probably sell fifty million of these every single year. Y’know, half as many people buy blenders, and there’s seven thousand blender accidents a year, so the probability of an accident like this one is… Point oh-three percent. Huh.”
