“I just read this article, ‘Is your partner less satisfied than you think?’” the husband said. Eddie caught Hen rolling her eyes at the words. “They said eighty percent are not satisfied. I’ve only ever been with Patsy, that didn’t seem like good numbers for me.”

“For you? For anyone.” Buck, when Eddie glanced over, was looking a bit panicked. Oh dear. “If- If I do the math on all the people I’ve been with, that’s… Wow.”

Time to put a stop to that line of conversation.

“Buck?” Eddie interrupted. “Let’s move her.”

“Right, uh, I’ll- I’ll get the gurney.”

Getting Patsy onto the gurney - folded so it was more chair than bed - and into the ambulance was unpleasant for everyone involved. Mostly for poor Patsy, who cried out in pain every time she was jolted too much. Eddie was quick to sit up front with Buck, leaving Hen to ride in the back with the poor woman.

(The husband, who Eddie eventually learned was named Ralph, followed behind them in his car. Hopefully after blowing out all the candles, because it would be very awkward if they came back from getting a vibrator unstuck from his wife’s bladder, only to find their house burnt down.)

“This is going to be a long drive to the ER,” Eddie said. “Hen said to try and avoid hitting any potholes.”

“Huh?” Buck glanced up from his phone. “Oh, right.”

Eddie narrowed his eyes at him, and then at the device in his hand.

“Who are you calling?”

“N- No one,” his husband said, but was very obviously avoiding his gaze.

“Who are you texting, then?” Eddie asked, reaching over and snagging the phone from the other man’s hand. “Taylor. You’re texting- Why are you asking her if she was satisfied with her experience!?”

There was a thump behind them, and Buck fumbled to start driving and also for an answer.

“Well, I- I just-” his husband stammered. “Eighty percent, babe! Those are not good odds for me!”

Eddie wasn’t sure what face he was making, but when Buck glanced over at him, he visibly winced and focused back on the road ahead.

“I’m sorry?” Buck tried, and Eddie glared. “I love you?”

“Try harder,” Eddie said, leaning against the cab door. The phone buzzed in his hand, though he didn’t spare it a glance. What did he care if Buck managed to “satisfy” Taylor fucking Kelly? 

“If it makes you feel better,” Buck said, trying for a smile. “You always leave me satisfied.”

Eddie grunted and tossed the phone onto the seat between them.

“You’re an idiot,” he said.

“You love me.”

“But am I satisfied?”

The smile dropped from Buck’s face.

“You are, right?” his husband asked, somewhat panicked at the possibility that he wasn’t. “Right?”

Eddie shrugged, shifting his gaze to look outside.

“Right? Eds, c’mon, you’re… Aren’t you? Babe?”

Maybe it was petty, but he felt it was well deserved.

Chapter End Notes

"A woman got a vibrator lodged in her bladder and needed emergency surgery to remove it."

Home Visit

Chapter Summary

In which there is a scheduled visit.

On Saturday, they have their monthly home visit from Deidra. Buck secretly kinda hated them, because he always felt on edge, half-certain that the social worker was going to find something, some reason to take Gavin away and never return. This was to be the third visit so far, with three more in the wings before everything was finalized, and his fears had yet to be founded.

Today was going to be worse, because-

“Shit,” Buck cursed, jolting at the sound of knocking at the front door and nearly toppling over a bag of flour. It didn’t hit the floor, but a cloud of it did cover him. He pointed a finger at Chris. “Don’t repeat that.”

“Is that Ms. Deidra?” the preteen asked, looking up from sorting out baking ingredients with his brows scrunched up. “She’s early.”

“She is,” Buck agreed, heading for the door. Hoover got up from his place below Gavin’s high chair to pad along beside him.

Today was going to be worse, because it was just Buck there at the moment. It wasn’t supposed to be; Abuela was out with Pepa, shopping or bonding or tormenting innocent strangers, Buck didn’t know. And Eddie still wasn’t back from his veteran’s group, was still talking to Stanley, but had said he would be back well before Deidra was supposed to be there. Except, apparently, the social worker had moved up the timetable for the day.

Joy.

“Deidra!” was how he greeted her when he opened the door, trying his best to not sound or look as nervous as he felt. “Come in, come in.”

“Buck, Hoover,” she greeted in turn, patting the pooch’s head with the hand that wasn’t holding a clipboard. There was a large purse hanging from her shoulder. “You’ve got…”

She gestured at Buck’s front, and he hurriedly started wiping at his shirt. All it really accomplished was sending up small white clouds, though.

“Yeah, sorry, uh. We were about to bake some cookies for Chris’ class. I was going to use Abuela’s recipe, but it turns out all she has written down are the ingredients but not the measurements? And then we were going to use the one I have, in one of my cookbooks, but it’s all in metric and that became a mess when I converted it to cups and everything, and-” It struck him that he was rambling, and he snapped his jaw shut. Taking a breath through his nose, he tried again. “Anyway, uh. Come in.”

“Thank you.” Deidra looked and sounded amused, though. “Where’s Eddie?”

“He and Abuela are both out,” Buck explained. “He said he’d get back before you got here, but…”

He trailed off, figuring it would be rude to point out how she was here three full hours before she was supposed to be.

“I know, I’m early, sorry about that,” she said. “But I was already close by for something else, and I realized everywhere else I have to be today is on the other side of the city.”

“No, no, it’s fine.” Hopefully it came across as convincing.

“I saw you’re getting new neighbors across the street.” Deidra was moving around the living room, looking about as she marked something down on her clipboard.

“Yup. I was planning on saving some of the cookies to take over.”

The moving van arrived just about an hour before, along with a truck that was almost as stupidly big as Eddie’s, and a sensible looking minivan. When he peeked at them through the window, Buck spotted a middle-aged couple - husband and wife, he assumed - along with a small gaggle of children. One of them seemed to be about Gavin’s age, so maybe there’d be playdates in the future?

(That reminded him: he needed to ask Maddie for an update on the false neighbor sting, when he talked to her tomorrow.)

Deidra’s visits had a routine to them, or at least she was doing the same thing now as she had the other times she was over. She did a circuit of the living room, followed by the dining room, and then Gavin’s room. She also poked her head into Chris’, after asking for his permission. She chatted with Buck all the while, asked some questions about Gavin, and jotted things down on her clipboard.

Her last stop was the kitchen, which was embarrassing. Baking supplies were spread across the entire surface of the table, which Chris had continued to organize while Buck was busy. Gavin, in his high chair, had also decided to entertain himself by throwing his cereal everywhere.

At least Hoover was being helpful in cleaning that up.

“Someone got bored, I see,” Deidra commented, stepping around the dog. “Hello Chris, how have you been?”

“Good,” the preteen answered. “I get to be Pops’ sous-chef today.”

“What about Gavin?”

“We decided he was the commis chef,” Buck told her. “I’m sorry about the mess, really, I-”

“It’s fine,” she interrupted, smiling at him.

Gavin was reaching for him, so Buck got him out of his high chair and bounced him on his hip as Deidra checked the cabinets and the fridge, making yet more notes on her clipboard. This part, the social worker making sure they weren’t just letting their kids starve, was the least nerve wracking part of the process. Food was how Abuela expressed her love and care, and there was never going to be a lack of it in their house.

“Those are some big steaks,” Deidra commented as she closed the fridge door.

“Pops and Dad won them in a poker game,” Chris oh-so-helpfully provided.

“That was date night,” Buck sheepishly explained. “They’re tomorrow’s dinner.”

The social worker nodded, looked around the back room with the washer and dryer and glanced out the backdoor, before coming back into the kitchen.

“Well, everything looks good, as always,” Deidra said, slipping the clipboard into her purse, and Buck couldn’t help the relieved breath he let out. He escorted her to the door, but she paused before opening it and glanced back at the kitchen.

“I didn’t want to ask in front of Chris,” she said, voice hushed, “but how are you doing?”

“Oh, I’m… Still not dead.” The joke fell flat. “Started back to work this past week.”

“Good. You’re looking better than you did the last time I was here.”

The last time had been just after he got out of the hospital, so it's not at all a surprise to hear.

The door opened, nearly hitting them both.

“Is that- Deidra!” Eddie stopped mid sentence, freezing on the threshold. “You’re…”

“Early, I know,” she said, nodding at him as she slipped past. “And also leaving already. I’d stay and chat, but I have to be across the city.”

“Oh, no, that’s- That’s fine.”

“See you next month, Diazes.”

They both stepped out on the porch to see her off, lingering until she was in her car and driving away. As soon as she was, Buck slumped against Eddie’s back and smacked him on the shoulder.

“Sorry, mi sol,” his husband said, turning enough so he could kiss him on the temple and take Gavin into his arms. “I was talking to Stanley.”

“I know, you told me. Everything okay?”

“Yeah, everything’s fine.”

Buck stared at him for a moment, before shrugging and letting it go. If Eddie said everything was fine on that front, then he’ll believe him until the man himself told him otherwise.

(Eddie had spent the time with Stanley, after the group split apart, venting about Buck and the confession from poker night. But his husband didn’t need to know that.)

“Well, I was about to start making cookies for Chris’ class before Deidra showed up. Three hours early, God.”

“Is that why you’re covered in flour?”

“It might be.” There was flour all over the back of Eddie’s shirt now, too. Woops. “If you’re nice, I’ll let you lick the spoon.”

“And if I want to lick something else?” Eddie asked, leering at him as they stepped back inside.

“Then you’ll have to contain yourself until tonight, Mr. Diaz.” Buck laughed at his husband’s exaggerated pout, before bounding to the kitchen. “Alright! Sous-Chef, is everything measured?”

Eddie did, indeed, get to lick the spoon when they were all done.

Maddie and Chimney, and Jee-Yun, arrived early on Sunday morning, at Buck’s request. Well, he specifically asked for Maddie to get there early, the other two just tagged along. They were early enough that Abuela and Pepa were both still at church, and Chris had yet to be dragged out of bed.

Jee was left to play in the living room with Gavin while Eddie distracted Chimney, because he knew well why Maddie was there. Buck dragged his sister out onto the back patio, making a pit stop for glasses of lemonade, and they both lounged in the chairs while they watched Hoover sniff around.

“So,” he said, after the silence lingered a bit too long. “How did things go yesterday?”

“Oh, it went well,” Maddie answered, and he couldn’t quite tell if it was sarcastic or not. “Turns out Rhonda-”

“Rhonda?”

“Her real name. She apparently did live on that street, but then she got arrested for - get this - fraud and forgery. Of coupons.”

“Okay.” Buck drew the word out, digesting this new information. “But why was she casing your house?”

“Turns out she wasn’t, she was looking for her mail.”

“Her mail.”

“Uh-huh.” Maddie nodded. “She was still in jail when we bought the house. When she got out, and restarted her ‘business,’ she thought it was still abandoned. So that’s where she had all the money orders sent.”

“So the lesson here,” he said, “is to make sure the house is actually abandoned still, before you have the evidence of your illegal activities mailed to it.”

“I mean, I’m sure that’s a lesson to be learned from this,” his sister admitted with a shrug, and then sat her glass down on the table between them. “Now, what’s going on?”

“What?” Buck asked, startled. “What do you mean?”

“You asked me to come over before everyone else,” she said, staring at him patiently. “Eddie was not at all subtle about distracting Howie while you dragged me out here. What’s going on?”

Buck gnawed on his lip, trying to figure out how to broach a subject he did not want to talk about. With Eddie, he’d simply blurted it, but that probably wasn’t the best way to do it.

“Do you remember that dream I had? While I was…?” he finally asked, waiting for her to nod before he continued. “I didn’t… I didn’t tell you about all of it.”

Maddie sat patiently while he spoke. She didn’t interrupt while he filled in the blanks he had very intentionally left out in the first telling. She didn’t say a word, but when he risked a glance her way, Buck got to watch his sister’s face collapse in real time as he told her how the dreamed up version of their dead brother argued against his waking.

Because Maddie wasn’t stupid and she understood the implications faster than he had, once he took the time to think about it.

“Copeland, she uh, she compared it to a bus,” Buck said. “Jumping in front of one, versus simply… Not getting out of the way.”

“I’ve always said you were going to get yourself killed,” Maddie said, wiping at her face. “I remember, you know? Back in Hershey, the way you looked at me after your bike accident. You were drowning, and I knew that if I didn’t get you out of there…”

“It wasn’t an accident.”

His sister’s eyes went wide.

“What?”

“When I crashed my bike, I… I saw the car pulling out,” he told her, letting his gaze drop to the glass of lemonade he was still holding. He’d barely drunk any of it. “I saw it, and I didn’t…”

Maddie covered her mouth to muffle the sob she let out.

“Oh, Evan,” she whispered, and Buck felt so guilty for the anguish in her voice. “That night I called you from Boston? When I found out that Howie was looking for me?” He nodded, his gut clenching in anticipation of whatever was about to come out of his sister’s mouth. “I walked into the ocean.”

When Eddie stepped out to check on them, ten minutes later, they were still clinging to each other and crying, Hoover doing his best to paw his way between them. Buck barely lifted his head at the sound of the door opening, but he watched his husband watch them for a moment. Then Eddie nodded to himself and said,

“I guess I’ll keep Chimney busy for a bit longer.”

He closed the door behind him when he went back in, and left them to it.

La Cucaracha

Chapter Summary

In which there are announcements and a review. And also some singing.

Chapter Notes

See the end of the chapter for notes

Monday’s shift started fine. Sunday had been weird and kinda hectic, what with the way Buck and Maddie came back inside having obviously been crying and then had to dodge Chimney’s worried probing. But Monday was fine, to start with, if maybe a bit weird. Bobby was acting shifty about something, which would probably be concerning if Buck wasn’t preoccupied with yesterday’s talk with his sister and tomorrow’s appointment with the doctor. And Jones, who was generally quiet and kept to himself, to the point that one could forget he was there half the time, even as he was the one driving them around, spent the morning grumping at his phone something fierce..

And then there was Hen.

“Denny is still upset with us,” she said as they sat down with their lunch. Bobby made them spaghetti with sides of garlic bread, and Buck was looking forward to diving right in. “Because he got caught lying, and now he’s being punished for it.”

“Grounded?” Buck glanced at Jones, who was already sitting at the end of the table, angrily chewing his own bread and glaring at his phone.

“Grounded,” Hen confirmed with a nod. “He’s lucky we aren’t selling his bike for scrap. Right now it’s currently chained up for the next, I dunno. Forever.”

“How’s Nathaniel?” Eddie asked, talking around a mouthful of food, in the exact way they’d get on Chris for doing. Hen scrunched up her nose in disgust.

“Getting out of the hospital this week,” she answered. “We’re gonna let him have supervised visits, but the moment he does something to piss us off? It doesn’t matter how much Denny hates us for it, Nathaniel is gone until he’s eighteen.”

“I mean, I kinda feel for him,” Albert chimed in from where he and Ravi were sitting together. “Having a kid you didn’t know about suddenly pop up at your front door? I can understand wanting to get to- What?”

He’d interrupted himself because Chimney, sitting on the other side of Hen from him, was frantically motioning for him to shut up.

“Yeah, no,” Hen said, throwing her bestie a look. “Nathaniel’s known about Denny for almost as long as Eddie’s been working here.”

“Oh.” Ravi frowned. “So he just didn’t…”

“We offered, he never took us up on it.”

“Where’s his mom?” he asked. “Do you know?”

There was a moment of brief yet profound silence, where it felt like everyone in the station stopped talking. Realistically, it was just their little group, but it felt like all conversation in the building ceased. During that time, Buck met Chimney’s wide-eyed gaze, and then cast about for a subject change, screw being subtle.

“Jones!”

The man in question looked up, startled to be addressed.

“What do you want, Diaz Number Two?”

“Uh, what’s got you looking all…” He gestured at him with his fork. “Like that?”

(“So we’re not going to answer my question then?” Ravi grumbled, low enough that only Albert could hear. Or at least, if the others heard, they just ignored it, too. “Alright.”)

“My mother,” Jones answered, tossing his phone onto the table top with an annoyed scoff.

“Oho, are you joining the club?” Buck asked. “The Shitty Parents Brigade?”

“What? No.” The man looked legitimately insulted by the prospect, which was, itself, insulting. “I’m not like you sad sacks, I get along with both my parents.”

“Hey, I’ve never had any issues with mine,” Ravi protested, the only one of them who really could.

“You’re both freaks who don’t belong,” Chimney told him, not even looking his way. “C”mon, Jones, give us the gossip.”

Jones scowled at their group, but apparently decided he’d rather not pass up the opportunity to vent.

“She’s playing matchmaker,” he complained, using a fork to stab at his food. “It doesn’t matter how many times I tell her I don’t want to date, every few months she’ll pop back up with a friend’s daughter or niece. She asked me to come over this weekend and practically entrapped me.”

“I’m sure it wasn’t that bad,” Hen tried.

“No, it was,” Jones retorted without missing a beat. “I walk in and there’s her friend and her friend’s daughter sitting on the couch. And when I tried to escape, she went on about how she’s worried for me, all alone at home, and how my brother and sister are both married and oh, when was I ever going to give her grandkids?”

“So how’d you leave it?” Chimney asked, clearly curious.

“We agreed to text each other and then meet up somewhere.”

“You could always just… Conveniently forget,” Buck suggested, only for Chimney to shoot it down with,

“He’s not gonna ghost her.”

“Plus Emma’s already texted me,” Jones informed them, frowning at his phone again. “And she’s picked a place. If I don’t go, I’ll never hear the end of it.”

“I feel ya,” Eddie said, twirling spaghetti on his fork. “I hate to think what mi tia y abuela would have done if they decided I was single for too long.”

“Hey, maybe it won’t be so bad,” Albert offered, clearly going for optimism. “You never know, you could end up planning a second date.”

“Doubtful,” Jones said, shaking his head. “I’ll just go and pretend like I actually want to be there, tell Mom that I tried, and then not have to worry about it again for another few months.”

He stood from his seat, gathering his phone and dirty dishes before heading for the to dump them in the sink. Bobby passed him on the way, carrying a homemade cake covered in chocolate icing, that he sat down on the table between them all.

“For me?” Chimney joked. “My birthday was last month.”

“What is this?” Buck asked, narrowing his eyes at it. With the way the captain had been acting so far that day, he was not trusting this. “What’s going on?”

“Nothing, it’s cake. Nothing, nothing at all,” Bobby denied, which just made Buck even more suspicious. It was the same for everyone else, too, as they were all staring at him, waiting for the other shoe to drop. “But that does remind me, I have a couple announcements.”

Yup, there it was. Beside him, Eddie dropped his fork into his bowl and pushed it away.

“Don’t keep us waiting, Cap,” Hen said, placing her elbows on the table and leaning on them.

“First off, we’re getting a new probationary firefighter in a couple of days,” Bobby announced. “He’s actually a transfer from the One Thirty-three. A favor for Captain Mehta, apparently he wasn’t quite fitting in over there.”

“What’s his name?”

“Dalton Wolfe.”

Ravi let out a groan and dropped his head onto the table with a thump. Albert hesitantly reached up and started patting his back in a “there there” fashion.

“You okay?” Eddie asked, leaning forward a bit and frowning in concern.

“He was at the academy when I was doing the peer instructor thing,” Ravi said, or at least Buck thought that’s what he said. The younger man’s face was pressed against the table, and so the words were a bit muffled.

“Wait,” Buck said. “Is he…?”

“Yup.”

“Did Mehta say why he wasn’t fitting in?” Eddie asked, and Buck figured he also realized that they’d heard of Dalton Wolfe before, just not by name. It looked like they probably were getting the opportunity to witness Hen or Athena tearing “the Douchebag” a new one.

Buck would need to put popcorn on the next station shopping list.

“I didn’t ask for details,” Bobby answered. “Didn’t want to be biased when he got here. But Mehta did say something about owing it to Buck?”

“Hold on. Is this revenge for beating him at poker!?”

“What was the second announcement?” Hen prompted, talking over Buck.

“This week, I’ll be seeing everyone individually for your annual reviews.” The captain’s announcement was accompanied by groans and sighs. “And remember, while it’s not department policy, it is my policy. The annual review is not just an opportunity for me to offer you constructive criticism, it’s an opportunity for you to do the same for me. Please, tell me how I’m doing.

“Now, dig in.” Bobby pointed at the cake. “Chim, you’re up first. I’ll see you in my office.”

“Ready when you are, Cap,” Chimney said, sounding oh-so-excited (not) as he reached for the cake and knife, and started cutting himself a slice. Bobby nodded and walked away, presumably for his office, leaving them all to complain about him in his absence.

“I never know what to say when he asks that,” Eddie said, slumping into Buck’s side.

“I’m just gonna tell him that the captain’s truck is always clean,” Hen said, snagging the knife out of Chimney’s hand so she could get her own slice of cake.

“You did that last year,” Chimney pointed out.

“Has anyone complimented him on his super-organized storage cubby?” Buck asked, to which his husband answered with,

“Twice.”

Well, damn.

“You guys sound pretty desperate,” Chimney said, while sounding pretty smug. “I submit that what you’re all nervous about is the review itself. These things don’t concern me. I was here before all of you. I was here before Cap, and I will be here when everyone is gone.”

“Like a cockroach,” Hen said.

Buck felt Eddie lift his head up off his shoulder, and knew he’d had the same thought.

“La cucaracha, la cucaracha, ya no puede caminar,” they both belted out. “Porque no tiene, porque le falta marihuana que fumar!”

“I apologize to all of you,” Hen said, passing the cake and knife down the table to Albert and Ravi, “for whatever I said to inspire that. But Chimney, I feel like I should remind you that you did punch the captain’s kid.”

The smug look dropped from Chimney’s face.

“You don’t think he’s still holding a grudge, do you?”

“I guess you’re about to find out.” She turned to Ravi. “Now what’s the deal with this new probie we’re getting?”

Chimney was, not that he would admit it to any of the chuckleheads he worked with, a bit anxious when he stepped into Bobby’s office. It was entirely Hen’s fault, for reminding him of his past stupidity. He didn’t know why he hadn’t thought of it himself, all things considered, but now he couldn’t not think about it.  Maybe he should have requested that Reynolds be the one to give him his yearly eval?

The B-shift captain may have been a stick in the mud, but Chimney hadn’t done anything to piss him off.

“Take a seat,” Bobby told him, gesturing at one of the chairs in front of his desk. He had Chimney’s big red personnel file sitting open in front of him, and he handed over a blue clipboard with a copy of the annual review attached. “Look it over.”

“Outstanding, outstanding,” he muttered, going down the checklist. “Outstand- What.”

It was the one for Personal Relations. Its score was as high as the others before, but it was the comment beneath it that stuck out. There, in Bobby’s neat little handwriting, was,

“Did not physically assault any of his coworkers this review period,” Chimney read aloud. “Gold star.”

The captain had the audacity to grin at him

“That was the standard we were going with this time,” Bobby said with a cheeky little shrug. “And you did outstanding. Now, you’re still seeing Frank, right?”

“Once a month.”

It had been part of the agreement that allowed Chimney to come back to work a year ago. The only reason he got that much, he knew, was because he ambushed Buck while he was home alone and they were both off the clock. And also because Buck had actively campaigned against Athena, or anyone else, arresting him for assault.

Only by the grace of the person he hurt, did Chimney even have a job to be reviewed.

“Good, good. Now, everything else is much the same,” Bobby said, trailing his finger down his own copy of the review. “Except for leadership.”

Chimney looked, and saw that it was his lowest score. But that was okay, because,

“Everybody knows I don’t aspire to the big chair. I’m pretty open about it, actually.” Plus, with that mark on his file, he wasn’t sure he’d even be allowed to sit in the big chair. Regrets, regrets. “Now’s the part where I review you, right?”

Bobby leaned back in his chair.

“Lay it on me.”

“Cap, I knew from the day you arrived here that you would be a guiding light for this house,” Chimney said. “And I look to you as that guiding light, because you… You, uh… You light the way to… Guide us?”

Across from him, the captain sighed, before grabbing a pen from the cup and turning the Chimney’s personnel file around.

“Sign and date here,” Bobby said, handing him the pen. “And then get out.”

“Will do, Cap.”

The appointment with Dr. Weaver was scheduled for just after they got off shift. There was enough time to keep from being late if they ran over on a call - unless it was something major - but not enough time to even bother with heading home, first. Carla knew about it, though they hadn’t told her what Buck was going to the doctor for, and so she was fine with hanging out at the house for a bit longer.

Buck spent the time in the waiting room, waiting for the nurse to call for him, as a mess of anxiety. He kept bouncing his knee, only stopping when Eddie reached over and laid a hand on it. Except then he started bouncing the other one, so it didn’t actually accomplish anything. He was certain the other people in the room with them were getting annoyed, but he didn’t let himself look around and confirm that suspicion.

“Hey, it’s going to be fine,” Eddie said, squeezing his knee. “You just go in there, they do their tests, and you’re done. That’s all.”

“Yeah, tests to confirm that I really am defe- Hey!” Buck rubbed at the spot on his arm where his husband had oh-so-rudely reached up and pinched him. Hard. “That hurt.”

“That’s what you get for trying to insult one of my favorite people.”

The nurse calling his name cut off Buck’s toothless retort, and the anxiety from before came rushing back.

“Here goes,” he said, getting to his feet.

“Do you want me to go back with you, or stay here?”

Buck thought about the kind of questions Weaver might ask him, and how much he did not want to answer them in front of Eddie. Even though his husband already knew at least most of it, even though they would absolutely talk about it later, either secluded in their room or while sitting on Copeland’s couch.

“Stay here,” he answered, trying for a smile.

“I’ll be waiting.”

This, Buck thought, as he headed for the nurse that had called his name, was probably going to be about as enjoyable as those first sessions with Copeland.

Chapter End Notes

Q) why isn't Chimney being sent to the academy?
A) because Ravi's already back at the station, and so Bobby doesn't need to lure him back.

Howdy, Neighbor

Chapter Summary

In which Buck meets the new neighbors, who will absolutely not cause issues in the near future.

“At least it’s not blood thinners again,” Eddie joked. Buck tore his eyes away from the prescription bottle he’d been staring at, spinning it between his fingers, long enough to make sure his husband saw his eye roll.

The appointment with Dr. Weaver had taken forever. So much of it involved talking, talking, and yet more talking. Questionnaires and going over comments from Copeland and his check-ups over the last month or so, since the lightning strike. And then it turned out that maybe he should have let Eddie go back with him, because the doctor wanted to talk to him, too.

By the end of it, Buck was rubber stamped with two new labels, neither of which were surprising, but which felt heavy all the same. Weaver opted to kill both birds with one stone. Or rather, treat both with one pill. They’d gone to get the prescription filled afterward, and Buck had to sit through the pharmacist listing and going over the side-effects and what he shouldn’t take with it, as if he hadn’t sat there and read through them all on his phone while he was waiting.

Eddie was driving them home, now. He hadn’t even asked or offered, he’d simply taken one look at Buck’s dazed expression and dug the Jeep’s keys out of his pocket. Which meant that Buck would have to readjust the driver’s seat again. Oh well.

“I won’t traumatize everyone if I cut myself in the kitchen again,” Buck agreed. There was still a pale scar on his finger, nearly faded now, from when he did exactly that while on the blood thinners.

“When are you going to start taking them?” Eddie asked, glancing at him and then putting his eyes back on the road.

“In the morning. So I’m not taking them in the middle of a shift.”

“Good plan. And what do you want to do now?”

The question had Buck sighing as he let his head fall back against his seat. He knew what he wanted to do: go home, grab Gavin, and curl up with him until it was time to pick up Chris. And then go back home and do the same, except with both boys. But Buck also knew what Eddie wanted him to do, which coincided in a very annoying way with what Buck knew he should do.

“Visit Pops before we head home, I guess,” he reluctantly answered.

“As you wish, mi sol.”

Buck narrowed his eyes.

“Did you… Did you just Princess Bride me?”

“I might have.” Eddie looked and sounded so very smug as he said it, and Buck’s typical response to that would result in them crashing. So he restrained himself to shaking his head fondly and turning the radio on. Taylor Swift was playing on the third channel he tried, and he raised the volume to drown out Eddie’s complaints.

It was far too soon after that, when they were pulling up to the curb of the Grant-Nash household. Buck’s leg had started bouncing three turns before, and he was tapping the pill bottle on his knee. Eddie, after he turned off the engine, reached over and placed his hand over both.

“They aren’t blood thinners,” Eddie said, correctly guessing at least part of the cause for the anxiety. “You can just tell him that it’s for ADHD.”

“Because he won’t be wondering why I’m taking an antidepressant for that.”

“We can blame it on the Adderall shortage.”

They could, that was a distinct possibility. But it wouldn’t be the truth, and Buck was horrible when it came to lying to a person’s face. He absolutely did not want to try and lie directly to Bobby’s face, especially with Athena right there. It just would not end well.

“C’mon,” Buck said, slipping the pill bottle into his pocket and hopping out of the Jeep. “Let’s get this over with.”

They knocked when they got to the front door. Buck had a key, given to him some time ago, but they always made a point of knocking. It didn’t matter the time of day, it didn’t matter if Athena or Bobby had called them to come over. They always, always knocked; May had learned that lesson for them.

“Buck, Eddie,” Bobby greeted them a few moments later. He glanced between them, looking put out and disappointed. “You didn’t bring my grandkids.”

“You literally just saw them on Sunday,” Buck pointed out, rolling his eyes as he and Eddie walked through the door and down the stairs.

“Chris is still at school,” Eddie helpfully reminded their captain.

“And what’s your excuse for not bringing Gavin to visit?” Bobby asked.

“We live five minutes away, you could come visit them, y’know,” Buck pointed out. “And uh, we just got back from a doctor’s appointment, haven’t been home yet.”

“A doctor’s appointment?” Athena asked. She was partially leaning through the entrance to the dining area, and so that’s where Buck went.

“Another one?” Bobby’s frown, for all that Buck wasn’t looking at him, was practically audible. “Is everything okay?”

“Yeah, yeah. Everything’s… Yeah.” Buck threw himself into one of the chairs at the table, knowing that his words didn’t sound the least bit convincing. He pulled the pill bottle out and set it down in front of him. “He put me on bupropion. Hundred-fifty milligrams, once a day.”

“That’s an antidepressant,” Athena noted, taking the seat across from him. Her tone was mild, almost purposefully so.

Buck didn’t know what the doctors put May on after her suicide attempt, if they put her on anything at all. But it made sense that Athena would be the one to recognize the name of the medication, that she would know what it was for. Briefly, before he could stop them, Buck’s thoughts got ahead of him and he found himself wondering how many sleepless nights she spent spiraling down rabbit holes of her own.

“It is.” Buck yanked his thoughts back on track. Eddie was standing behind him, his arms a comfortable weight on his shoulders. “It’s also used off-label for ADHD.”

Two birds, one pill.

He watched Bobby and Athena exchange looks, communicating in their own non-verbal way. And then Bobby pulled out his own chair, sitting down next to his wife.

“Alright,” the captain said, the word coming out tired. “What are the side-effects?”

“The- Oh.” Buck was expecting more of an interrogation, was expecting them to want to know just what the doctor had said and what he had told the doctor, that he was being prescribed an antidepressant. This was much better to talk about. Hell, he’d practically memorized the entire list, already. “The common ones are headaches, dry mouth, nausea, sore throat, restlessness, trouble sleeping, constipation. And the dizziness. Can’t forget the dizziness. But all of that should really only be a problem for the first couple of weeks or so.”

“And the uncommon?” Athena asked, looking very much like she expected one of those to start showing right that moment. Which, with Buck’s luck, was very fair.

“Vision changes. Eye pain, or swelling, or redness. Oh! And seizures.”

Drinking apparently made the dizziness worse, and could also make the seizures more likely. Or so Buck had read.

There went his wine nights with Maddie.

“We also need to monitor his blood pressure, apparently, since it might raise it,” Eddie added, thumbs rubbing circles against the back of Buck’s shoulders. “But we can do that at home or at the station.”

“I’m gonna start taking it tomorrow morning,” Buck told them. “Start of the shift, so we hopefully won’t be on any calls when I need to take it.”

“And what happens if you miss a dose?” Bobby asked. “If we’re on a scene that carries over, and don’t make it to the station in time?”

“Nothing.” Buck shrugged. “I’m supposed to either just take it as soon as possible, or skip it entirely.”

“You will tell me the moment you start feeling dizzy,” Bobby ordered. “And the vision one. The moment you feel dizzy or start having issues with your vision, you tell me. I don’t care what you’re doing, I don’t care if you think it’ll pass. You tell me.”

“Aye, aye.” Buck gave a mock salute. “I’m going to tell Hen and the Hans- Oh, that would be a cool band name. I’m going to tell them about it, too. Just in case something happens. Like we’re on a bridge and it collapses or whatever.”

“Why was that the first scenario you thought of?” Eddie asked, leaning over him.

“It just was,” Buck said, shrugging. “Though that reminds me, we never had a September Disaster last year. I mean, there was that blimp, but that was in August.”

“September Disaster?” Athena asked, quirking an eyebrow at him. 

“Don’t ask,” Eddie said, cutting Buck off before he could even start. “Or ask Bobby. We need to be getting home so Carla can go home.”

“Next time the two of you show up at our door, I expect at least one of the grandkids with you,” Bobby said, getting out of his chair to walk them to the door.

“You live five minutes away,” Buck reminded him again.

“That’s right, five minutes away, which means you should be bringing them over more.”

“You see them almost every weekend, Pops!”

“Not the point.”

“Oh my God.”

“What is it, Teek?”

“Oh my God, oh my God, oh my-”

“Teek!”

“Sorry, sunshine, just- Oh my God.”

“What, did Carlos reveal another secret wife or something?”

“Fuck you, no. No, Dad-”

“Your dad has a secret wife?”

“No! His girlfriend-”

“Has a secret wife.”

“Damn it, Buck. No, she has a not-so-secret husband.”

“...You’re on speaker. Eddie! Get over here.”

“Apparently Dad’s new girlfriend is in an open marriage? The guy came into the station, because Dad grabbed the wrong pair of sunglasses…”

Hoover was an active dog. Which wasn’t really much of a problem, because both Buck and Eddie were active people. Most of the time, they got away with simply letting him loose in the backyard, especially when Jee-Yun was there to chase and be chased. But that was only a stop-gap measure, good for keeping him from destroying the house while they were at work, like he did when he was at the Wilsons’.

On days they had off, if they weren’t too tired or too busy with errands, one of them would wrestle Hoover’s little booties onto his feet. Buck had ordered them soon after they got the pooch, because the sidewalks of Los Angeles could get really, really hot. And then they had to dig their heels in the moment they clipped the leash to his collar, because the dog was ready to shoot off like a rocket.

These days, this was done with Gavin in his little carrier and wearing his dinosaur-print bucket hat.

They usually tried to walk him together, one of them holding Hoover’s leash - and keeping him from making a mad dash for freedom - and the other carrying their kid. Some days, like today, one of them ended up doing the dog walking while the other ran an errand. Today, that was Buck, while Eddie made a quick - “Yes, Buck, quick. That’s why you’re not coming with me.” - trip to the store before picking Chris up from school.

Buck didn’t have a set path when he walked with Hoover. They mostly meandered, usually in the direction the dog was most interested in. As long as it kept them south of West Adams Boulevard. Or, more accurately, away from the freeway on- and off-ramps. Along the way, Buck would stop to let Hoover sniff at whatever caught his attention and in turn be stopped by random people who wanted to pet the dog, or coo over the baby, or both.

Honestly, the walks wouldn’t take nearly as long if he didn’t have to spend so much time fending off flirtatious advances. Buck couldn’t decide if it was Hoover, Gavin, or the combination of them that attracted so many.

They were just coming back from the walk, almost home with Hoover flagging and Gavin getting restless, when their path brought them in front of their new neighbors. Buck wasn’t sure if he liked the house, to be honest, with its front yard dominated almost entirely by pavement. It probably made for an easier time mowing, at least.

The new neighbors, the middle aged couple that were moving in while Deidra was making her visit, were getting out of a car as he passed. Buck hadn’t met them yet, even though he totally meant to bring them some of the cookies he and Chris baked. Except he made the mistake of trusting both Diaz boys alone with them, and, well. It was a miracle there was still enough for Chris’ class.

No time like the present.

“Hey!” Buck called out, pausing to wave at them. “I live just across the road there. You just moved here, right?”

“We did,” the man said, coming over. He was older than Buck, and about his height. His wife was much shorter; she was carrying a baby in a pink dress, a matching bow in her hair. “I’m Edward, this is Shanna.”

Buck blinked. Then he blinked again.

Edward. And Shanna.

Huh. Alright.

“And this is Clara,” Shanna said, lifting up the baby’s hand in a wave.

“Right, uh. Buck,” he said, reaching out to shake Edward’s hand. “This handsome fella is Gavin, and this other handsome one's Hoover.”

The dog had taken a seat on the grassy part of the easement, patiently waiting for this impromptu meet-and-greet to come to an end so he could get inside. Gavin, in contrast, was getting increasingly annoyed and making it everyone’s problem.

“How old is he?” Shanna asked, adjusting her own kid in her arms.

“A year last month,” Buck answered, glancing over when he heard Eddie’s truck. He waved at his husband as he pulled into their driveway. “Yours?”

“A couple months, yet. She’s our youngest.”

Buck had already guessed as much, having spotted the teenagers that first day.

“Have you lived in Los Angeles long?” Edward asked, before he had the opportunity to start gushing about Chris. “We just moved up from San Clemente, for my work.”

“A few years,” Buck answered. Gavin, tired of his irritation being ignored, let out a wail and threw his hat to the ground. Edward was quick to pick it up and hand it back. “Thank you. I think that means I need to get this little guy inside. It was nice meeting you both.”

They said goodbyes, and then Buck was hurrying across the road with Hoover leading the way. The driver’s side door of the truck was open, Eddie sitting sideways with his feet hanging out. He leaned forward when Buck got to him, and got the soft kiss he was clearly asking for. Chris, clambering out of the truck on the other side, made dramatic gagging noises.

“Hey, babe. Store trip go well?”

“Got the goods and the boy,” Eddie confirmed, reaching back and grabbing the shopping bag from the seat, along with Chris’ school bag. “Making friends?”

“Yeah, our new neighbors.” Buck looked over, but the two of them were gone, probably went inside. “Guess what their names are. Go on, guess.”

“Wesley and Buttercup.”

“No, not even close. It’s Edward and Shanna.”

Eddie blinked, and then blinked again.

“Huh,” he said. “Alright.”

“Pretty much my reaction.”

Vigorous in Word and Action

Chapter Summary

In which Al and Ravi have their employee evals, and Chimney makes a phone call.

The bupropion came in the form of a little pill, white and circular. Buck took his first dose at breakfast, washing it down with his morning coffee. They’d told Chris and Abuela about it, the latter with more details than the former. Chris had very easily accepted the explanation of “it’s to help me feel better,” even if he was still curious about the details. Carla also knew, because they made a point of telling her about any medication in the house.

The orange pill bottle was tucked away into the medicine cabinet, since they were only working half a day and so there was no need for Buck to tuck it away into his work bag. They were taking the truck that morning because Eddie was driving; it might have been too early for side-effects to appear - Buck had literally just taken the first dose, after all - but that wouldn’t necessarily be the case by the time their shift ended, so they agreed it would be better safe than sorry.

Buck did not quite bound into the station as he usually did, and then he volunteered to stop by the locker room to put away his and Eddie’s things. He was perfectly willing to admit that he was putting off the conversation with the paramedics, even as he knew that it would be better to get it out of the way as soon as possible.

They were gathered around the kitchen island when he finally made his way up the stairs. Eddie, standing to the side of them all, was looking proud of himself. So Buck figured he had taken it upon himself to herd the cats.

“Not dizzy yet,” Buck announced as he joined them, because he had never been one for easing into anything.

“That’s good to know,” Bobby said with a nod.

“Was that a concern?” Ravi asked from where he was leaning against one end of the island, Albert right beside him.

“Uh yeah.” Buck shifted his feet nervously, and tried to think of just what and how much to tell them. “My doctor put me on some new meds that I started today. Bupropion.”

Even discounting her time in medical school, he knew Hen used to be a pharmaceutical rep. He expected her attention to zero in on him with that new piece of information, he expected for her eyes to narrow behind her glasses. What Buck did not anticipate, but really should have, was how Chimney stood up straighter as his face twisted in a frown.

“That was the first thing they tried Maddie on,” the man said, squinting up at him in clear concern. “What’s going on? Everything okay?”

“No, no- I mean, yeah, everything’s-”

“Wait, is this related to why she was-?” Chimney’s teeth clicked with how fast he snapped his mouth shut, glancing at the others around them with wide-eyes.

Eddie might have kept the man busy on Sunday morning, but he would have had to have been blind to have missed how upset Maddie had been after Buck talked with her. And Chimney wasn’t stupid, of course he would connect that to Buck telling them about being put on an antidepressant. Buck was the idiot for not expecting this to happen.

“It’s also used off-label for ADHD,” he offered, knowing it was a weak explanation as the words left his mouth. But thank God for Albert, because he popped off with,

“Because of the Adderall shortage, right?”

“That is a reason.” It wasn’t the reason, but still.

“So what are the possible side-effects?” Hen asked, pulling out her phone to no doubt look it up herself. “You mentioned dizziness. What else-? Oh.”

Eddie, blessed Eddie, was holding out a folded up sheet of paper. Hen was quick to yank it out of his hand, unfolding and slapping it down on the countertop. Buck could see, written in his husband’s very pretty handwriting, a list of what he recognized as the side-effects he’d looked up yesterday and obsessed over as they waited for the prescription to be filled

It was even organized into categories: Common, Uncommon, and “911.”

“When did you make that?” Buck asked as the paramedics- Well, as Hen and Albert bent their heads together and read through the chart. Chimney stood aside, frowning at him still.

“While you were helping Chris with his math homework yesterday,” Eddie answered.

“One word,” Buck said, cutting Ravi off as the younger man opened his mouth, a Mathematician joke doubtlessly about to tumble out of it. “Just one word, and I will- I don’t know what I’ll do, but it won’t be pleasant.”

“Cap, he’s threatening me. Go light up the Bat-Signal- Ow!” Ravi rubbed at his arm, where Buck punched him.

“Could we please not hit each other.” Bobby sounded very despairing as he shook his head. “Albert, when you’re done reading through that, meet me in my office. You’re up first today.”

“Okay, Cap.” Albert was clearly unenthused about it, which didn’t seem to bother Bobby any.

“C’mon.” Eddie put his hand to Buck’s lower back, guiding him to the stairs.

“Where are we heading?” Buck asked. He wasn’t complaining - Chimney was looking more and more like he wanted to ask questions, audience be damned - but he was curious.

“To check your blood pressure.”

“What?” Buck nearly stumbled on the stairs, and probably would have if not for that hand steadying him. “Eds, I literally just took the first dose less than an hour ago. That is not enough time for it to even properly get into my system, much less start raising my blood pressure. If it’s going to do it at all.”

“Humor me.”

“Fine, fine.”

Buck allowed himself to be directed to the back of one of the ambulances. He sat his pretty self down as his husband clambered inside and dug out one of the blood pressure cuffs, before hopping back out and wrapping it around Buck’s bicep. Eddie pumped it up, and the cuff tightened, and Buck sat patiently through it.

The thought struck him that Eddie was probably doing this because it was something he could do, in the face of all the things he couldn’t do anything about. This, and making that chart, and taking over the driving for the next couple of weeks.

“One-ten over seventy,” Eddie announced.

“See? I’m perfectly fine.”

“For now,” his husband said, yanking the cuff off and wrapping the pump around it. “We’ll check it again later.”

Buck sighed and reached with both his hands to cup Eddie’s face.

“Hey, I promised you, remember? I’m not going to go anywhere,” he told him, leaning forward to kiss him. “But it really, really sucks that you decided to play doctor while we’re at work.”

“If you behave through this shift,” Eddie said, climbing back in to stow the blood pressure cuff away, “and don’t do anything stupid, we’ll play doctor when we get home.”

“Liar,” Buck accused without any heat. “We’ll get home, eat dinner, and then crash after putting Gavin to bed. Don’t give me an empty promise.”

“I didn’t say we were going to play sexy doctor,” Eddie pointed out, sitting down next to him. “I told you, I want to check it again later.”

“You are such a tease.”

“And you’re both disgusting,” Hen declared, turning the corner to stand in front of them, hands on her hips. “It’s only been five minutes. Please don’t tell me I have to disinfect anything in there.”

“Nope.”

“Not because of us, anyway,” Eddie added.

“Good,” Hen said. “Now get out of the way, so I can do inventory before we’re called out.”

“Yes, ma’am.”

The two of them hopped to their feet, and then scurried off to do their own chores. Buck caught sight of Chimney headed their way, and pulled Eddie along even faster. He did not feel like having whatever conversation was bound to follow, if the other man caught him without the others.

Albert had honestly not been looking forward to his own review. Not because he thought he was going to get a bad one, but simply because he hadn’t figured out a single thing to offer Bobby, for when the captain flipped the script around. Now he was straining his brain for something, all while half-listening to the captain go over things.

“You and Ravi are managing to work together without your relationship interfering or bleeding over, which is excellent,” the older man was saying. “And the scores on your evaluation are good. A mixture of outstandings and exceeds standards. What concerns me, though, is initiative and leadership.”

Albert glanced down the list, spotting the two easily because they were the lowest of the bunch.

“Well,” he said. “I don’t really want to be in charge of anyone, Cap.”

The captain sighed.

“You and your brother both, it seems. Which is fine,” he said. “But there’s also your initiative. Or lack of it, I guess.”

“What do you mean?” Albert asked, sitting up straighter in his seat. This conversation was fast becoming uncomfortably familiar to him. It felt like he’d taken a step back to years before, and his father was asking what he was doing with his life.

Not that Albert thought of Bobby as a father, or believed him to be anything like his actual father.

“I have noticed,” the captain carefully said, “That… There are instances where you’ll anticipate the needs of your partners, having something like a c-collar on hand, ready to give it to them. And times where you come up with a genuinely good idea, that no one else had thought of yet. But also, and I’ve noticed this especially since Chimney came back to A-shift with us, there are times that you’ll stand back and let him and Hen handle everything, and wait for them to tell you what to do.”

Oh. That’s what this was about?

“Of course I do.” He was feeling more than slightly confused about this critique. “They know what they are doing. They have been doing it for far longer than I have.”

“And I appreciate that you’re aware of that fact,” Bobby told him, smiling a little. “It isn’t a bad thing, necessarily. This is just a pattern I’ve seen starting to develop, and I don’t want you… I don’t want you getting complacent, I suppose. Especially when there might be a day that they’re not with you.”

“Alright.” That was fair, he supposed. “Was there anything else?”

“No, that was it.”

“Excellent,” Albert said, hurrying to stand. “Then I’ll-”

“Yeah, yeah, yeah, sit back down,” the captain ordered, looking amused. “You have to sign and date the copy in your file. But before that, it’s my turn. Hit me with it.”

“Uh.” Shit, what did he have to say? His brain was entirely blank. Quick Albert, say something. “Um….” Something more than that. Oh! “Your storage cubby is very organized, sir. It’s easy to find what’s needed.”

Bobby stared at him for a moment, clearly expecting more to follow.

“Is that it?”

“Yes?”

The sigh that the captain let out was very disappointed, but there wasn’t much that Albert could do to help him there. He could not think, not at that moment or in the time since the announcement about the evaluations, of an actual critique to offer the man. There was almost certainly one to be given - Bobby was not a perfect man, and Albert knew that - but he couldn’t think of it.

“Sign and date here,” the captain told him, sounding dejected as he spun the red personnel folder around.

Albert did so, and then hurried to escape.

Maddie’s work schedule only mostly aligned with Chimney’s own. There were days, like today, where he worked and she was at home with Jee-Yun. Which was a good thing. Because there were days, like today, where he desperately needed her to answer the phone and he didn’t want to wait until she was on break.

“Hey Howie,” she greeted him, and he felt his nerves calm a little at the sound of her voice. He could hear Jee-Yun talking in the background, telling her mother something about the cartoon she was watching. “What’s up? You literally just started your shift, didn’t you?”

“Yeah, yeah I did.” There was a brief moment where he wondered if he should be asking Maddie about this, but it passed. Chimney had seen how upset she was on Sunday, had asked about it only to be rebuffed. She clearly wasn’t the one being left out of the loop. And he would talk to Buck directly, but it was very clear that the man was dodging his attempts to do just that. “I just… You talked to Buck. On Sunday.”

“Howie…”

“I know, I know, I-” Chimney let out a breath, checked to make sure there was no one around his little section of the sidewalk, that there was no one eavesdropping on him. “He told us about starting on some new meds today. You know, to make sure we knew about it if something happened. Eddie gave us a list of side-effects, including the severe, Do Not Pass Go ones.”

“Okay…” Maddie drew the word out. He could tell from her tone that she hadn’t known about the meds, but she also wasn’t surprised. “Well, thank you for keeping me up to date on my brother.”

“It’s just that I recognized the name of what he’s on,” Chimney said. “You talked to him on Sunday, and whatever you talked about… Maddie, is Buck okay?”

Once upon a time, Chimney fucked up. Severely. There had been no excuse for it except stress and temporary insanity. He recited his mea culpas, he did his best to rebuild the bridges he burnt. The process was slow and grueling and awkward and painful, but he was succeeding in that endeavor. With the exception of that not-so-minor hiccup back in November. But more than a year and a half later, he still felt like he needed to make up for it.

“Buck’ll be okay,” was Maddie’s answer.

It wasn’t a yes. 

It was also probably, almost definitely the best he would get from her about this.

“Okay, that’s… Okay.” Chimney knew far too well how good a Buckley could be at keeping family secrets. He could try to get more out of her, but he’d have better luck getting blood from a stone. “I need to get back in there, before we get called out. I love you.”

“I love you, too.”

“Tell Jee that Daddy loves her.”

“I will,” she said, laughing. “Now go.”

After the call ended, after he tucked his phone back into his uniform pants, he lingered outside for a bit longer. The temptation was there to keep trying to get Buck to talk to him, to try and get Eddie or Bobby to tell him what was going on. But those avenues would be just as fruitless, he knew.

He didn’t like this feeling of knowing something was wrong, but not knowing what was wrong.

“Do you miss helping at the academy?” Bobby asked, and Ravi was quick to shake his head and answer with a,

“Nope.”

He’d been summoned to the captain’s office shortly after Albert had come out for his own turn in the hot seat. This wasn’t his first time sitting through one of Bobby’s evals and it hopefully wasn’t going to be the last. That didn’t mean it wasn’t anxiety-inducing, though.

“It wasn’t for you?”

“I didn’t mind it,” Ravi answered, shrugging. “It was a nice change of pace, but I like being here more.”

“We like having you here,” Bobby told him. “I think spending time back at the academy helped you, though. I’ve noticed you’re a bit more confident than you were.”

“Thanks, Cap.”

“You’ve got high scores throughout,” the captain said, gesturing at his own copy of Ravi’s eval. “Including leadership. Which, I am sad to note, is apparently not common at this station. Have you thought about trying for a leadership position in the future?”

“Uh. Maybe?”

In truth, he hadn’t given much thought about it. And even if he was hoping for a captaincy of his own, it probably wouldn’t be at the 118. Not unless Buck got struck by lightning again. Which, given the man’s luck, actually wasn’t impossible or even improbable.

There was Hen, too, though he wasn’t sure how interested she would still be in it, with the way her first go of it went.

“Speaking of the academy,” Bobby said, and Ravi wasn’t sure he would like where this was going. “When the new probie starts, I think I’d like for you to take point in training him.”

Yeah, no.

“Uh, I think…” How to put this politely. “I think Buck would be a better fit for that, Cap.”

“You know Wolfe already though, don’t you?”

“I do, so I’m comfortable in telling you that Buck would be better.”

Ravi remembered very well the day Wolfe looked at him and his name tag, and then asked if he was a Slurpee Indian or a Casino one. It wasn’t the worst bout of racism he’d been exposed to in his life, not by a long shot. But it was how he’d asked the question in front of everyone, either completely ignorant or totally uncaring about the wrongness of what he was asking, that really stuck out to Ravi. That, and the way Wolfe kept getting his name wrong. He’d had to be doing it on purpose, too, with how often Ravi himself or those around them would correct his pronunciation. 

Honestly, he was shocked that the guy hadn’t washed out already. He’d be an utter ass to the instructor, almost certainly because it was a woman, and Ravi knew there were others at the academy that Wolfe had been at least tangentially offensive towards. And with the way he was being transferred to their station, with a vague reasoning of “wasn’t quite fitting in,” he’d eat his hat if he hadn’t continued the habits at the 133.

Ravi would eat his turnout gear if Wolfe wasn’t related to one of the chiefs, because there was absolutely no other reason he would be transferred and not simply given the boot.

“Are you sure?” Bobby asked.

“Oh absolutely, Cap.”

“Alright, what is it,” the captain demanded, folding his arms on top of Ravi’s personnel file. “What’s wrong with the guy?”

“Just that it would… It would be best if you kept him with Buck or yourself. Maybe Jones? Just keep him away from me. And Al and Chimney. Maybe Eddie? Oh, and definitely keep him away from Hen.”

Bobby rubbed at his face and grumbled something about a Gerrard that Ravi didn’t quite make out.

“Alright. I guess I’m calling Mehta after this and asking for more details,” the captain said. “But moving on from my future migraine. It’s your turn to review me.”

“Ah, oh, uh…” Ravi fumbled for something to offer the man. “Um. Oh! You are vigorous in words and action.”

Bobby stared blankly at him.

“Al and I had Chinese last night,” Ravi sheepishly confessed. “That was my fortune cookie.”

“Sign, date,” Bobby told him, pushing his personnel file across the desk. “Then go away. Please.”

“You got it, Cap.”

They're Written in Blood

Chapter Summary

In which someone learns what safety regulations are for. The hard way.

Chapter Notes

See the end of the chapter for notes

Before Los Angeles, before he joined the fire department and finally found one that suited him, Buck had an assortment of different jobs. Odd jobs that he took up and put down like different hats. Bartender, ranch hand, construction worker, and countless others. But one thing they all had in common was this:

The safety regulations were not a fucking joke.

Sure, there were people he worked with that thought they were stupid, that thought they were a waste of time and that it would just be easier if they were ignored. There were supervisors that were of the same mind, and Buck never worked under them for long if he could help it. Because the thing was, he knew damn well how serious those safety regulations were. One of the guys he worked with, the ones who framed houses, taught him what happened when a safety rule - in this case, wearing safety glasses while working - was ignored.

That same guy also taught Buck that a lack of depth perception did not affect a person’s ability to throw a punch, after he made a poorly timed pirate joke.

(He deserved that one.)

Unfortunately, not everyone took these lessons to heart. How many calls did they respond to that wouldn’t have happened if people didn’t believe that OSHA was some bogeyman, out to ruin their day? How many times did they have to patch someone up, rush them to the ER, because of their insistence that the regulations were more like guidelines than actual rules?

Too many, including this one.

They were sent out to a commercial bakery because of a, quote, “horrible accident.” Vague enough to already imply something awful and probably stupid. But then they got more details - the supervisor got herself stuck in one of the equipment - and it was definitely both awful and stupid. For a reason that Buck was not privy to, but which had Albert scowling petulantly at the captain, Bobby decided to leave Hen and Chimney behind for this particular call.

When they arrived, they were let in by a frantic woman whose white apron was splattered with blood.

“She’s over here,” the woman said, leading them through the bakery floor. There were a dozen other people around, dressed similarly to her - white apron, blue shirt, plastic hair cap - and scrambling to get out of their way while also straining their necks to get a good look at what was going on. “I shut off the machine as quickly as I could, but I’m afraid it’s too late. She’s still unconscious.”

She led them to one of the large, industrial stand-mixers. The safety cage was lifted up, and the vic was bent over the lip, half-inside the drum. When the workers got out of their way, Buck was able to get close enough to see that the woman’s arm was twisted around the mixing wand. There was blood stirred into the batter.

Ravi, standing beside Buck, grimaced a bit but didn’t rush to go puke, as he had during that chainsaw call a year and a half ago. They grew up so fast.

“What’s her name?” Eddie asked as he and Albert slipped off their medical bags and got to work.

“Reyna. Please help her.”

“She’s breathing, but her pulse is racing,” Albert announced, having reached in and felt around her face and neck. “I’ll put her on oxygen. Eddie, start a line?”

“Wide open,” Eddie confirmed, pulling the necessary things out of his bag.

“We’re gonna have to take this thing apart,” Bobby said, examining the mixer. “Buck?”

Buck, already digging through his own bag for what they would need, simply handed the captain a socket wrench.

“Ready to go,” he told him.

Together, they started taking out the nuts and bolts, detaching the safety cage and handing it off to Ravi and Jones, who sat it down out of their way. They moved on to the rest of the machine, doing their best to pull it apart as carefully as possible before handing it to the other two, to reduce the risk of injuring the woman any further.

“I’ve got no pulse in her right hand,” Eddie announced.

“That’s because, uh.” Albert was peering into the dough-filled drum, where the arm was trapped by the machine. “It looks like her arm isn’t attached anymore.”

“What about her arteries?”

“I think the dough’s serving as a compress,” the paramedic answered. “Cap, we should take the mixing wand with her. If we remove it, I’m afraid she’ll bleed out. Either here or in transit.”

Bobby was about to answer, except that was when Reyna started waking up. It started with some groans, muffled by the oxygen mask they’d put on her, which then escalated into screaming as she struggled to get up. Albert, having bent down to grab a roll of gauze, rushed to stop her.

“Stay still, Reyna,” Albert told her as Eddie hurried to get a shot of morphine, reporting how much he was giving her as he did so. “Just breathe. We’re giving you something for the pain.”

“Alright, it looks like we’re fully dismantled,” Buck reported, pausing to try and swallow against a suddenly dry mouth before handing the last piece to Ravi and the last of the nuts and bolts to Jones. The last before they were freeing the woman, at least. An act which would relieve the pressure from her wound, something they didn’t want to do just yet.

“This side, too,” Bobby said. “Albert, let us know when it’s safe to separate this from the mixing wand.”

Buck and the captain gripped the sides of the machine, holding it steady as Albert quickly wrapped the gauze around the mixing wand and the woman’s shoulder. When he was done, he stepped back and nodded to them, saying,

“We’re set.”

“Alright, Buck. Slowly and carefully,” Bobby instructed. “One, two, and lift.”

Together, they lifted the machine off Reyna, who wailed a bit as the movement and loss of pressure had her feeling the pain more. The mixing wand was left behind just as they wanted, sticking straight up from dough and gauze. They dropped the hunk of metal onto the floor beside the drum, before assisting in getting the woman out of the drum and onto the gurney. Without disturbing the mixing wand.

“This,” Buck told Ravi a bit later as they climbed into the engine, sans Eddie and Albert; they were transporting Reyna in the ambulance. “Is why we do not ignore OSHA. Did I-” He paused to cough and clear his throat. “Did I tell you about the guy who lost his eye?”

“Are you okay?” Ravi asked, ignoring his question in favor of another.

“I’m fine,” he said. “Just, mouth’s gone a bit dry.”

That was on the list of side-effects, he remembered, and clearly so did Bobby. The captain twisted around in his seat to fix him with a look.

“I’m fine!” Buck insisted. “Still not dizzy.”

“You will tell me the moment you are, though,” Bobby ordered.

“I will, I will,” he promised. “Can we get to the station, please? I would really like to get something to drink.”

It looked like he would have to start keeping a water bottle on hand. At least until his body adjusted, however long that would take.

Joy.

Oh!

“Jones,” Buck blurted out, getting the man’s attention; his eyes flickered from the road to look at him in the rearview mirror. “You never told us how that date went. Or, wait, did you even have it yet?”

“It went,” Jones answered.

“And?” Ravi pressed, sitting forward to peer at him.

“The place we went to had good garlic bread.”

There was silence as they waited for him to expand on that. The silence went on for a bit, until Ravi finally broke it with,

“Is that all?”

“Yup,” Jones answered.

And then he spent the rest of the drive back to the station in stone-faced silence, because the man opened up about his personal life about as often as supercontinents formed.

“So,” Bobby said, pen tapping against the desk.

“So?” Eddie echoed, looking up from his copy of the employee eval form.

It was the start of their next shift, and it had barely begun before Eddie was being summoned into the captain’s office for his review.

“How is he doing?” the captain asked. “Really?”

Eddie could not say that he was surprised by the question. Honestly, he’d expected to be trapped and interrogated in regards to Buck’s wellbeing. He just thought Bobby would have a bit more patience, that he would last a day or two longer.

Apparently not.

“He’s doing fine,” Eddie answered, as vaguely as possible. He didn’t know what Buck wanted shared, so he wasn’t going to share more than the bare minimum.

“Uh-huh. Anything else pop up?”

The day before, Buck started complaining about his mouth and throat being dry. Eddie got back with Albert from dropping off the bakery supervisor at the hospital to find his husband standing by the station’s sink, refilling the largest glass they had. When they got home after their shift, they dug out one of their reusable water bottles and today it was practically attached to Buck’s hand.

“Nope,” Eddie told him, popping the “p” much as Buck would do. “His blood pressure stayed normal, and he’s still not dizzy.”

He was a bit restless, but Buck was rarely not restless, so Eddie wasn’t certain that counted.

“What about you?”

“Me? I’m fine, Cap,” Eddie said, brushing it off and pointing at something on his sheet. “These scores are good, right?”

“Yes,” Bobby said, nodding slowly. “Except for leadership, because apparently that’s a rare quality in this station. But let’s get back to you.”

“I thought we were talking about me.”

“Exactly.” Another nod, sharper. “How are you doing?”

“I just told you, I’m-”

“I know enough to know that Buck isn’t as okay as he’s been seeming,” Bobby interrupted. “Which means that I know enough to know that you’re not as okay as you’re acting.”

Eddie heaved out a sigh and felt himself slumping in the uncomfortable office chair.

“I just don’t know what to do,” he confessed. “I don’t know what I can do. I’ve been checking his blood pressure, I’ve started driving us places in case he does start getting dizzy. But beyond that? I just… There’s nothing I can really do, and I hate it.”

“I am entirely certain that you’re doing a lot more than that,” Bobby told him. “Even if you don’t realize. Have you been talking to someone?”

“Copeland, we’re seeing her again at her office in a week or so. And I’ve been talking to Stanley, at the VA group.”

“That’s good. And I know why you might be reluctant to talk to me about it, considering the roles I fill both in and out of the station. But I know that Athena would be happy to listen, if you asked.”

“Thanks, Bobby. I’ll, uh. I’ll think about it.” Eddie pushed himself out of his chair, unclipping his copy of the review and setting the clipboard on the desk. “I’ll just-”

“Nu-uh, wait. You still have to give me my review.”

“Oh. Uh.” He tapped his foot for a moment, thinking. “You know, I feel like I haven’t really had the chance to collect my thoughts for that. Since we were talking about… Y’know. So I’m gonna go out there and, uh. Do some collecting. I’ll get back to you.”

He hurried out the door, hearing the captain calling him back with,

“Get back to me? Eddie? Eddie! You didn’t sign and- Eddie!?”

“No surprises here,” Bobby said, handing Hen a clipboard with her employee eval, before sitting behind his desk and opening up her red personnel file.

It was her turn in the hot seat, shortly after they all watched Eddie just about fly out of the office and hide elsewhere in the station. The captain had come out shortly afterwards, with Eddie’s file and a pen, and they got to see him get increasingly more annoyed the longer it took him to find their wayward coworker. Eventually, Buck had taken both pen and file from him, at which point they all learned that he could apparently forge his husband’s signature.

“Outstanding in every aspect of your performance,” the captain continued. “And you did a good job when you were covering as captain, back in the fall.”

“A good job?” Hen couldn’t help but scoff. “Cap, I almost literally crashed and burned. I couldn’t balance work with school or home, and flunked a test so badly I had to beg the teacher for a second chance.”

Nevermind that she ended up dropping out of medical school anyway, that was neither here nor there.

“But as captain,” Bobby stressed, “you did a good job. Some people said that if it had been the week after your test, you wouldn’t have had a problem at all.”

“Some people, huh? And who are these people?”

“Buck,” the captain admitted with a shrug.

Hen still felt rather guilty, to be honest. She knew, they all knew, that Bobby had been setting the younger man up to be captain while he was gone. And then he simply wasn’t. They hadn’t been given a reason, Buck only told them all that it wouldn’t be him. And he looked absolutely crushed as he did so.

So yes, Hen still felt a bit guilty about getting the temporary position in his stead. Though that guilt was tempered by pride, because god damn it, she deserved it just as much as he did. No, considering what she went through under Gerrard and all the captains between him and Bobby, all the time and sweat and tears she put into this career, she could honestly say she deserved it more. Not that she would ever, ever say that out loud. Not unless the only people around her were Karen and Chimney.

“He wasn’t wrong,” Hen said.

“It is a bit ironic, though,” the captain said.

“What’s ironic?”

“While you were in medical school, none of your LAFD Continuing Education hours were met,” Bobby told her. “Your paramedic certification is about to expire. In ten days.”

“Oh.” Fuck.

“Don’t worry, though. I got you an extension.”

“Thank you,” Hen breathed out, feeling relieved.

“Okay, I was completely honest with you, now you be completely honest with me.” The captain leaned back in his seat, arms wide. “Go ahead, hit me.”

“Uh…” She hated this part. Every year, it was the same thing. And every year, she gave the same answer. “The captain’s truck is very clean.”

Admittedly, Bobby never used the captain’s truck, not unless there was an emergency. Chimney used it more during his weeks as interim captain than Bobby did his entire time with them. It didn’t make it any less than true, even if maybe it was more applicable to Reynolds. But Reynolds never asked for a counter-review.

(No wonder Ravi liked B-shift so much.)

“Thank you,” Bobby said, insincerity dripping from each and every letter. He picked up her file and dropped it in front of her. “Sign and date, and then you’re free to go.”

Hen did not waste any time.

Chapter End Notes

I want to preface this by saying that I love and adore Hen, but I don't think the person who wrote this episode actually watched the first part of 6a. Because uh. They have Bobby telling Hen that she carried the station on her back while keeping up with her medical studies. But she actually did the exact opposite of that? Like, it was a plot point. The fact that she didn't manage that.

Hype Hen up as much as you want, she deserves it, but also...

(I'm still a bit bummed they decided to have her drop out of med school. I understand why, but she would have been an awesome doctor.)

Dizzy, Dizzy

Chapter Summary

In which Bobby finally gets useful feedback. Also featuring an accident at a bodybuilder competition and a surprise visit that no one wants, not even the visitor.

Buck put a lot of effort into his body, this was not a fact he was ashamed about or reluctant to share. The majority of the exercise equipment that now populated the detached garage at home was his, and he spent a lot of time using it. This was not counting the required hours of exercise when they were on shift, either. And while he would be the first to admit to being a bit (or maybe more than a bit) vain, most of what he did focused on function over appearance.

He built his muscles so he could run up and down multi-story buildings in full turn-out gear while carrying tools or hoses or victims. There was also - no shame in his game - the desire to be able to pick up and carry his kids whenever they wanted, no matter how big they got, although Christopher had long outgrown the desire to be carried around by his fathers. And the ability to lift Eddie up in his arms whenever the occasion called for it?

Definitely a perk.

But bodybuilding? Making a sport out of weightlifting and gaining muscle? That was something Buck never really got into. He understood it - again, he would freely admit to being (more than) a bit vain - but he never really got into it.

Dispatch sent them to a bodybuilding show downtown. It wasn’t the first one they’d been called to, it wouldn’t be the last. Although this one was more than a bit rowdier than Buck was comfortable with. Thankfully, the cops had beaten them there, so they weren’t entirely on their own.

“We’re still working on crowd control,” Athena told them, meeting them at the entrance of the stadium. She had to raise her voice to be heard by the crowd, angrily chanting something about someone. “But the stage is safe for you to work.”

“What’s with all the rage?” Bobby asked.

“It could be the steroids,” Hen guessed, and yeah. Roid rage was definitely a thing. 

There were always steroids being used at these events, even as they all got upset and angry whenever someone was discovered to be dosing. It was the appearance of gaining all that muscle mass naturally that mattered, after all. As soon as that facade was broken, it didn’t matter if literally everyone else was using, that person was dirt beneath their shoes.

“Your victim’s name is Biggs,” Athena explained, leading them through and around the upset crowd. “Former bodybuilder, used to be a legend.”

So they’d put him on a pedestal, which made it even worse when he was caught out.

The stage was populated by a woman in a pants suit that Buck assumed was in charge of the show, and a bunch of muscular men in speedos. But they weren’t Eddie, and these days that was almost a requirement, so Buck barely paid them any mind.
