The only one that was important was the young man crouched over their victim, helpfully wearing a shirt with BIGGS written on it in glittery letters, who was clutching at his bloody bicep.

“Please help him,” the younger man begged. Buck figured he was the guy’s son. “His arm blew up, he’s bleeding out. We can’t stop it.”

“Let’s take a look,” Hen said, as she, Chimney, and Albert sat their medical bags down and crouched around him.

“Clear blood is never a good thing,” Chimney commented, gingerly poking at the hole in the guy’s muscle. “Pus, so an infection of some sort.”

“I don’t think that’s pus,” Hen said, digging through her bag.

“What is it, then?” Albert asked.

“Synthetic oil,” the pantsuit woman answered. “Meant to enlarge his muscles.”

“That could explain why everyone’s so angry,” Buck commented.

“Nobody likes a cheater,” Eddie agreed, as they both went to assist the venue’s security and the cops with crowd control. They were getting more and more upset by the second.

“They’re liking him less by the minute,” Bobby said. “Ma’am, it’s not safe. We need to get you out of here. Ravi?”

“Got it, Cap,” the younger man said, and Buck turned his head to watch him lead the woman off the stage. Ravi took the long way around the crowd, instead of the direct path through that Athena led them, with one of the cops splitting off to escort the pair. Thankfully the crowd was busy directing its vitriol at the injured Biggs, so they weren’t accosted by anyone.

Buck allowed the chatter between the paramedics, Biggs, and Biggs’ son to fall into the background, focusing instead on the increasingly aggravated mass of bodies in front of him. He stood between Eddie and Bobby, shoulder to shoulder, acting as a human wall between them and the target of their ire. 

“Cap!” Hen shouted from behind them. “He’s tachycardic, we have to get him out of here!”

“That’s gonna be tricky right now,” Buck shouted back, shoving at a member of the crowd who decided to lunge at them like a mosh pit. The guy fell back easily, which was good. Because that was the moment the dizziness finally hit. It felt like the ground jolted and spun beneath his feet, even as he knew that the stage itself was perfectly still. He stepped back, shaking his head like that would help in any way, and blurted out, “Pops.”

Eddie took one glance and immediately stepped in front of him, putting his own body between Buck and the rowdy crowd. Buck reached out and grabbed the back of his husband’s uniform shirt to steady himself, even as he felt Bobby’s hands grab at his arm and side.

“Sergeant!” the captain called, getting his wife’s attention. “We have to move. A little help?”

The dizziness was passing quickly, thankfully, though Buck was reluctant to let go of Eddie just yet. He looked over to watch Athena stomp across the stage and yank a microphone out of someone’s hands, before turning around to address the audience.

“Ladies and gentlemen,” she started, tone demanding both attention and respect. “I don’t know if what this man did is illegal. What I do know is that, pursuant to section One-Four-Eight-Point-Two of the California State Penal Code, it is a misdemeanor to willfully obstruct emergency personnel from discharging their official duties. So let me advise you: Do. Not. Obstruct.”

They took the instructions to heart, easily guessing from Athena’s demeanor that she was not in the mood to be tested. They parted like the sea before Moses, allowing them to get Biggs out on the gurney. Eddie practically dragged Buck out of the building, grabbing him by the wrist and not letting him go until they were back at the engine, and then only because he needed both hands to shove Buck into the cab and then into a seat.

“I’m fine,” he told them all, easily accepting his bottle of water when Eddie forced it into his hand. “It was just for a couple moments, but it passed. And hey, I told you the moment it hit.”

“You did,” Bobby said, nodding. “You're the man behind for the rest of the shift.”

Buck sighed, but didn’t argue. He was honestly expecting it.

“You’re not drinking,” Eddie tutted, and Buck rolled his eyes even as he sipped at the water. It was helpful, he wasn’t going to lie; ever since that bakery call the day before, it felt like his mouth was always dry. Not horribly, not parched, just constantly. “And we’re taking your blood pressure again when we get back.”

“Do you see what I’m having to deal with, Rav?” Buck asked the younger man, who did not appear like he wanted to be dragged into this conversation. “It’s all nag, nag, nag. Never get married.”

“I’ll be sure to tell Al.”

“Buck,” Eddie interrupted, and then nodded pointedly at the bottle in his hand. Buck rolled his eyes, but took another sip anyway.

“I was dizzy,” he pointed out. “For a moment. Not dehydrated. I don’t know what drinking water is going to help with.”

“It’s good for you. Drink.”

“I think I much prefer this attitude in other areas of our life…”

They bickered the entire way back to the station, with Ravi and Bobby occasionally joining in. Jones, in the driver’s seat, ignored them as he always did. When they arrived, after Bobby helped Jones back into the bay, Eddie immediately started manhandling Buck again. He dragged him out of the engine and then towards their second ambulance, the one that hadn’t been taken out on the call.

“It’s not going to be any higher than it was yesterday,” Buck said, leaning against one of the ambulance doors. “Or the day before.”

“Just sit down and let me check it,” Eddie retorted, hopping out of the vehicle with the blood pressure cuff. Buck shook his head but did as his husband asked, letting him wrap the cuff around his arm.

“Maybe we should start checking yours,” he said, scuffing at the bay floor with his foot. “I’m willing to bet it’s too high.”

“It’ll be entirely your fault,” Eddie told him, watching the gauge. “How bad was the dizziness?”

“Not too bad, more disorienting than anything,” Buck answered. “It only lasted a couple seconds, honest. The timing just sucked.”

Eddie let out a hum, and then nodded.

“One-twelve over seventy,” he said, undoing the velcro and yank off the cuff.

“If you’re both done,” Bobby said, poking his head around the ambulance, “Buck, you’re up.”

“Y’know what, I am suddenly so faint.” Buck flopped backwards, starfishing as best he could with his legs hanging over the edge. “I don’t think I‘m feeling up for the review, Cap. Maybe… Maybe we should postpone it until, I don’t know. Next year? I’m sure I’ll start feeling better by next year.”

“He’s fine,” Eddie said, carefully stepping over him to put away the blood pressure cuff.

“Eds,” he whined. “In sickness and in health! You’re supposed to have my back.”

“C’mon, Buck.” Bobby clapped a hand on his knee and gave it a shake. “Let’s get this over with.”

Buck moaned and whined, even as he pushed himself up and hopped out to follow the captain. When they got to the office, he threw himself into one of the seats in front of the desk as Bobby bustled about, pulling things out of his filing cabinets. One of them, a copy of his employee eval attached to a clipboard, was shoved into his hand before Bobby took his own seat.

“I don’t know why you all hate this so much,” the captain said, opening up Buck’s red personnel file. “It’s not like I’m putting you through torture.”

“An argument could be made,” Buck quipped, grinning at the look the other man shot at him.

“First off, besides the dizziness today-”

“Which only lasted for a moment, and I told you right away.”

“You did,” the captain agreed, nodding. “But besides that, and the dry mouth, has there been anything else with your meds?”

“Nothing else.”

“Good. So…”

Buck sat and listened as Bobby went over everything, following along with him on his own copy. Everything was fine, it seemed. Well, almost everything. Because there was one score that caught his eye that was very much not fine.

“Professionalism, camaraderie, initiative,” Bobby listed off. “Don’t have a problem with any of those. And your PT scores are right there. I think that does it, really.”

“Does it really?” Buck asked, glancing up at him.

“Listen,” the captain said. “I know everyone’s nervous about reviewing their captain’s performance. But we can just keep this casual, I promise.”

“No, no, this is still about me,” he interrupted, and then pointed at his paper. “My PT scores are not “right there.” They’re low, but you marked them as outstanding.”

“Yes, considering that…”

“Considering what? That I had an accident on the job?”

“Buck,” Bobby said, looking him straight in the eye. “You died.”

“I know,” he said. “I was there. And it was only for like, a moment, and then I got better.”

Judging from the way Bobby’s eyes widened and his jaw dropped a little, that answer may have been a bit too flippant.

“So, what?” Buck continued before the captain could start a concerned tirade. “Are you just gonna take it easy on me forever?”

“I don’t think that’s entirely inappropriate.”

“Cap, come on.” This was so very frustrating. Hadn’t they talked about this very thing twice before? He was sure that they had. “You- You can’t do that, okay? I am a member of this team, and you have to hold me to the same standards as everyone else. You can’t treat me any differently, you can’t cut me any slack. Okay?”

The entire time he spoke, he watched the captain’s eyebrows rise up on his forward. When he was finished, Bobby gave a small nod.

“You’re right,” he said.

“I know,” Buck replied, though something about the other man’s tone had him regretting speaking out.

“My thought was that you’d earned it,” Bobby told him. “But it just goes to show that sometimes, the teacher can learn from the student.”

“Great, great,” Buck said, rushing to get out of his seat. “So that means I don’t need to review you, right? Right, so I’m gonna-”

“No, no,” Bobby cut him off, shaking his head. “Sit back down. You just said it, I can’t cut you any slack.”

“But…” Damn it, he wasn’t ready for this. What the hell could he say for the- Wait. “No, I just gave you your review.”

“What?” Bobby narrowed his eyes as he watched Buck grab a pen and reach across to sign the official copy of the eval. “You said that was still about you.”

“And it was. But, I mean- Cap, we’ve had this argument before, remember?” He tapped at the desk with the pen, before slipping it back into the cup. “You need to work on your objectivity.”

“Huh.” Bobby rubbed at his chin, lost in thought. “That might be the only helpful review I’ve gotten back this entire week.”

“You’re welcome,” Buck replied, cheerful as could be as he scurried for the exit. “Thanks for the clipboard, bye!”

“Wait. Buck, no, bring that-”

He shut the door behind him, cutting off the rest of the captain’s words.

“I’m calling bullshit,” Buck said, adjusting the phone on his shoulder. “On account of the fact that I have a basic understanding of human anatomy.”

It was Friday, and they had the day off following their twenty-four. After napping a little, he and Eddie took care of chores around the house. They’d also taken Hoover for a walk together, Eddie insisting he go with, just in case Buck got dizzy again. Which hadn’t happened yet, but it wasn’t impossible.

Shanna was outside when they came back. Buck tried to say hello, tried to properly introduce Eddie to her, but she’d given them an almost unfriendly look before ducking through her front door. Buck couldn’t understand it, because she and Edward had been perfectly friendly the other day.

What the hell had Buck done, for her to make such an about-face?

Currently, Buck was at the dining table folding towels. Eddie was in the backyard, keeping an eye on Gavin as he chased Hoover around. TK had called in the middle of it, and was now trying to convince him that his father had busted open the frozen chest of his girlfriend’s husband while trying to do CPR.

“I was there,” TK said. “I saw it happen.”

“I believe you that you were there and that you saw it,” Buck said. “I’m just not believing that it happened that way you said it happened. There’s just no way.”

“Well it did!”

“You said he was frozen solid. Being frozen solid does not suddenly turn your chest cavity into a hollow-”

Knocking at the door interrupted him. Abuela startled in her chair, having been in the middle of a nap while supposedly watching one of her shows. She started to get up, but Buck waved for her to sit back down as he passed.

“I don’t know what to tell you, sunshine,” TK continued in Buck’s ear as he opened the door. “I was there, I saw it happen. I-”

“Teek, I’ll have to call you back.” Buck hung up and slipped his phone into his pocket, not feeling very guilty about it. “Deidra, what’s up? Didn’t we already see you this month?”

The woman being at his door when she was supposed to be always made Buck feel nervous. Her making a surprise visit? Without any forewarning? That had his stomach twisting like snakes.

“Can I come in?” she asked, instead of answering.

“Of- Of course.” Buck stepped to the side, letting her in. “Come in, sit down. Uh, do you want something to drink? I can get you something to drink.”

“No, thank you,” she said, shaking her head. “Is Eddie home? I saw his truck.”

“Yeah, I’ll- I’ll go get him.”

This was not good, was the thought that swirled around his head as he hurried to the backyard. Eddie was crouched down beside Hoover, giving him scratches, and he raised his head when he heard Buck. He clearly saw something to be concerned about on his husband’s face, because he shot up and asked,

“What’s going on?”

“Deidra’s here,” Buck answered, bending to grab Gavin when the little guy ran up to him. He’d started walking on his own very recently, and much like his cousin, he was now a little menace running everywhere. “I don’t know why.”

“Whatever it is,” Eddie said, coming up to place a hand on his lower back, “I’m sure it’s fine. You said it’s just Deidra?”

“Yeah, just her.”

They went inside, Buck bouncing Gavin nervously in his arms, Hoover padding along behind them. Deidra was sitting on the couch, chatting amiably with Abuela, but the atmosphere was still anxiety-inducing. Buck hovered behind his husband, arms tightening around their son. He knew the woman wasn’t going to just yank him out of his arms, but…

“Hi, Deidra,” Eddie greeted, shaking her hand when she stood up. “What brings you here?”

“Nothing good, I’m afraid,” she answered, sighing.

“What does that mean?” Buck asked, terrified of the answer.

“I have no intention of taking Gavin away,” Deidra said, and the words had him relaxing a little. Only a little. “But there was a complaint.”

“A complaint?” he echoed, feeling hollow.

“¿Qué significa eso?” Abuela demanded, enraged. “What kind of complaint?”

“There were concerns about your lifestyle.” Deidra spat the word out. “And that you were acting inappropriately in front of your children.”

“Our- What?” Buck shared a look with Eddie, not really understanding what was going on. “Acting inappropriately? What?”

“Obviously it’s nothing to worry about on its own,” the woman continued. “We’re required to look into it, but I was literally just here the other day. That’s why they have me as the one following it up. I’ve come by and looked in on you every month this year. We all know it’s a bogus complaint, and after I’m done here I’m going to slap a big ‘Waste of Time’ stamp on it.”

“But someone still called you on us,” Eddie said, voice strained with the effort to keep his temper in check. “Because, what? We’re two men who’re married with kids?”

“You can’t tell us who it was, can you?” Buck asked, already knowing the answer.

“It was anonymous. They knew your name and your address, but they didn’t give us theirs,” Deidra said. “But, no. Even if we knew, we can’t tell you.”

It was probably for the best, Buck thought, looking at the visibly raging Diazes. Abuela looked about ready to hunt the person down and beat them to death with her own hands. And there was no telling what Pepa or Athena or Maddie would do, when they heard about this.

But he would still very much like to know just who in their lives decided to do this to them.

Venting

Chapter Summary

In which Eddie vents, Bobby kidnaps the kids, and Buck's Sunday starts off poorly.

They almost forgot to make adjustments to their Saturday morning routine. Typically, if they had the day off, Buck would take Chris to his equine therapy while Eddie went to his veteran’s group. Except that now, at the moment, Buck wasn’t supposed to be driving.

He’d gotten dizzy again, a couple hours after Deidra left on Friday. Just like the first time, it passed as quickly as it hit, or so Buck claimed. To Eddie, it seemed like it might have lasted a little bit longer, might’ve come on a little bit stronger. But that might have just been his warped perspective, seeing it as worse than it really was. It did emphasize that Buck shouldn’t be driving until he adjusted to the bupropion and was no longer at risk of random bouts of dizziness. No one wanted him to suddenly get dizzy while behind the wheel, after all. They’d seen the results of that.

Unfortunately, that put a snag on their Saturday. If they’d thought of it ahead of time, they could have asked Carla, but they didn’t and now it was too late to expect her to rearrange her morning just for them. Eddie offered the suggestion that he could just skip his group and take Chris himself. Except Buck took great exception to that idea.

“Shut the fuck up, no you won’t,” had been his exact words. And then he kept telling Eddie to shut up every time he opened his mouth to argue his point.

Luckily, though, they had other options. Such as one that had been complaining just the other day that he wasn’t getting to see his grandkids often enough. Bobby, when they called him to ask, had been almost too eager to get up early on his day off and take Chris for them. He showed up on the morning of as they were still getting the kid ready, and then proceeded to steal Gavin as well.

Eddie wasn’t very surprised to come home later and find that Bobby still hadn’t brought either of them back, even though the equine therapy ended before his group did. Nor was he surprised at the sight of Athena’s car sitting out front.

“We’re making bets on where Bobby took them,” Athena informed him when he joined them on the back patio. Someone - Eddie assumed it to be Abuela - had made lemonade, and he pouted until Buck produced an empty glass and poured him some. “Want to get in on it?”

“Unless they snuck it past us this morning, Chris didn’t pack any bags, so not camping,” Eddie said, thinking aloud as he perched himself on the arm of Buck’s seat. There was an empty chair on the other side of Abuela, but… It was on the other side of Abuela. “A museum.”

He distinctly remembered Bobby mentioning that he used to take Robert Jr. to the museums in St. Paul, back when they had their vacation last year.

“But which museum?” Buck reached out and smacked his shoulder. “You’ve gotta be specific. I already guessed the Old Station Twenty-Seven.”

“Oh, that’s a good one.” Old Station 27 was a firehouse in Hollywood that the LAFD turned into a museum. It was only open on Saturdays and then only for a handful of hours, which was incredibly inconvenient when one had a full schedule, so Eddie had never been through. “The Natural History Museum. Or the Science Center. Maybe both, they’re right next to each other.”

“Good guesses,” Buck said, nodding approvingly. “Ma guessed the zoo, and Abuela refused to be any fun.”

Abuela scoffed and reached over to shove at him, Buck laughing as he twisted to avoid it.

“Wherever he took them, good luck,” Eddie said, sipping at his lemonade. “Chris knows exactly how to manipulate him and Gavin likes to make occasional sprints for freedom.”

That had been true even before Gavin fully got his feet under him. More than once, and especially in the days since the little guy started to actually run around, Eddie caught Buck looking up child leashes while clearly debating the merits of them in his head.

“It’ll be fine,” Athena said, flapping her hand without a care. “Bobby’s a big boy, he can handle it.”

“It’s lunch time,” Abuela commented, glancing at the small watch on her wrist. “Will you be staying, Athena?”

“I don’t see why not.”

Abuela nodded and pushed herself out of chair. She headed back inside, pausing to give Buck a look.

“I’m coming,” the man grumbled as he stood up as well. He slapped a kiss on Eddie, before following after her in much the same manner as Hoover was doing. Eddie watched him go, slipping down into the now empty chair.

“They told me about the visit yesterday,” Athena said, after a moment or two of silence. “Isabel seemed about ready to tear someone a new one.”

Eddie let out a humorless laugh.

“I was about ready to tear someone a new one,” he told her. “It’s lucky for everyone that Deidra couldn’t tell us who it was.”

“So you have no idea who it could have been?”

“Not a clue,” Eddie said, shaking his head. “No one at the station, that much I’m sure. Bobby’s done a good job weeding out anyone on our shift that might’ve before I even got there, and I’m pretty sure the same could be said for B and C shifts.”

“Maybe someone at Chris’ school?” Athena offered.

“It would have to be someone there, or one of our neighbors,” he said, and glanced about. They had a privacy fence and a bunch of trees along the edges not covered by the garage, but he was still left feeling paranoid about this. “No one else knows our names and address. But the timing is weird, isn’t it? We’ve been living here for months now, and everyone at the school has known about Buck for much longer.”

“What about the new neighbors?” Athena asked, gesturing vaguely behind her, in the direction of the street. “The ones Buck told us about, with the names.”

“Edward and Shanna? He said they were friendly. I don’t know, I haven’t talked to them.” He shook his head again, trying to keep his temper down. It had been steadily rising again as they talked. “He was torn up about it yesterday. Just about refused to let Gavin out of his sight, like he was afraid Deidra would change her mind and show back up to take him away.”

Eddie wondered if that contributed any to his dizzy spell, the stress of Deidra’s visit and her news.

“God, I can imagine,” Athena said. “I know how I was when they took May away, after…” She trailed off, and Eddie watched her fingers flex and tighten on her glass. He didn’t know precisely what happened with May before he was around, but he had a general idea. Buck had more details than him, presumably gathered through comments from the young woman herself, as well as Harry, Athena, and Michael. “How have you been? With everything?”

“You’re not going to ask how Buck’s been?”

“When I want to know how Buck is, I ask Buck,” she said, almost chiding. “And besides, I’m sure everyone’s asked enough. I know Bobby told you I’d be willing to listen, if you wanted.”

Eddie craned his neck to look over at the backdoor, debating with himself. It was so convenient, Abuela dragging Buck inside and leaving him alone with Athena. Convenient enough that he could almost believe they’d planned it. He just wasn’t sure how much he could say, not without betraying his husband’s trust.

(He was having sympathy pangs for Chimney, now.)

Bobby had implied he knew something when they talked in his office. Enough to know that things weren’t really okay, which reinforced the impression Eddie got when they told him and Athena about the new medication. The way she immediately recognized what it was, and the way they immediately let it go without pressing for details.

They knew something, probably enough to know what was going unsaid. And Eddie had told Buck that he didn’t want to make the same mistakes Maddie and Chimney did. So…

“Just you?” Eddie asked, needing to make sure. If he was tempted to ask Buck to stay back during calls now, simply for his own peace of mind, then Bobby would be much worse. And they’d already seen how that would play out.

“I won’t say a word to your captain,” Athena promised. “Or his father.”

“I have been freaking the fuck out for the past week and a half,” Eddie blurted out in a harsh whisper. “I knew things weren’t okay after the lightning. And I- We’ve all heard the way he talks about himself, sometimes, and I’ve always hated it. But Athena, I didn’t know how bad it was, and I have no idea what I’m doing…”

Bobby came back with the kids a few hours later, walking through the door with Chris chatting away and Gavin dozing against his shoulder. The toddler was clutching a toy firetruck that he didn’t have that morning, and Chris excitedly dug out a model fire engine from the gift bag Bobby was carrying so he could show it off.

Buck was almost unbearably smug the rest of the day, for guessing right.

Buck loved Sundays, he looked forward to them almost every week. More specifically, he loved and looked forward to Sunday dinners, especially now that they mostly took place at their home. Spending the evening surrounded by his family, serving them food that he helped make, that was his little slice of heaven.

It probably went without saying, although he said it many times before, that he hated working on Sundays. This Sunday shift was looking to be especially bad, because Bobby asked him to show up a bit earlier than usual. Buck wasn’t sure why, was a bit hesitant to find out, but he did it anyway. So did Eddie, whether he wanted to or not.

The captain was waiting in the bay when they showed up, the remnants of C-shift slowly winding down around him and getting ready to leave for the day. He motioned for Eddie to take their bags to their locker, and had Buck follow him to his office. The obvious desire for privacy did not make things any better.

“Our new probie is starting today,” Bobby said, once the door was closed behind them. “Dalton Wolfe. Did Ravi ever mention him to you? I know you made a point of spending time with him, when he was helping at the academy.”

“Uh, not by name,” Buck hedged. The captain sighed at the answer.

“Mehta was vague when he was asking for the favor, and even vaguer when I talked to him later,” he said. “And when I suggested to Ravi that he be the one to train Wolfe, he shot me down and refused to give me a straight answer as to why. But he did emphasize how he thought you would be a better fit for the job.”

“I don’t like where this is going, Cap.”

“I know, and I’m sorry.”

Normally, Buck would leap at the chance to mentor the new guy. Normally, he’d be running high off the endorphins for the rest of the day, if not the week, from the proof that Bobby trusted and believed in him. Normally, he’d leave the room after this conversation and literally skip across the station to share the news with Eddie.

Normally.

“Did Mehta say anything about why he was being transferred and not simply given the boot?” Buck asked.

“The kid is Torres’ nephew.”

“The deputy chief!?”

Bobby nodded grimly.

Captains didn’t actually have the power to fire someone. They sent a recommendation of termination to the chiefs, who then decided whether or not to follow through. But it was pretty much accepted that the process was just a formality, really. Especially if, say, the firefighter in question got caught stealing the ladder truck to have an afternoon tryst during work hours. And definitely if the captain found out about said tryst because someone called 9-1-1 to ask why the ladder truck was at their apartment building, and should they be worried?

(Buck deserved to be fired that day, and he was never going to argue differently.)

But when the chief rubber-stamping the paperwork also happened to be the uncle of the person being recommended for termination…

“Well. Maybe he isn’t as bad as they’re implying?” Buck offered, clutching that strand of hope.

“That’s the assumption we’re going with,” Bobby agreed. “We’ll give him the benefit of the doubt, until he proves otherwise. If worse comes to worse, I’ll bounce him between shifts and transfer him when his probationary period is over.”

“Maybe not C-shift, though,” Buck said, recalling Ravi’s comments about how the guy treated one of the female instructors.

“I’ll take that under advisement,” Bobby said, letting out a tired sigh and glancing at his watch. “Everyone should be getting here, including our new probie.”

Dalton Wolfe arrived after everyone else had, a scant few minutes before the shift officially started. He was young, young enough that Buck wasn’t even certain he could legally drink yet, and had a couple inches of height on him. Which was not something Buck was used to.

Wolfe followed behind Bobby almost lazily, when the captain led him to the station loft for introductions. And those went okay, for the most part. Nothing horrific, not until=

“Peer Group Instructor Panikkar!” the probie blurted out, and quite cheerfully, too. Except the name came out more like, “Panicker.”

Everyone stared, except for Ravi, who simply sighed and stomped away. Albert was quick to follow after him.

“It- It’s Panikkar,” Buck said, and then repeated it again, slowly. “Panikkar.”

“Isn’t that what I said?”

“We just call him Ravi here,” Bobby said, always the diplomat. “Buck? Why don’t you give him a tour.”

“You got it, Cap.” Buck tried not to let his reluctance show as he threw him a salute.

He should have made Mehta pay through the nose at that poker game.

How Not to Avoid Your Audit

Chapter Summary

In which the probie continues to have interpersonal difficulties, and Buck ruminates on soulmates and exes.

The worst, most insidious part about the new guy, Eddie found, was how easy it was to miss some of his more unfortunate traits until he was blatantly shoving it into the faces of those around him. And even then, if you weren’t paying attention or simply didn’t know better…

Although, some things were impossible to miss. Like the time when, while Eddie was helping Buck show him the ropes, they passed by Tanika Clarke checking equipment. And Dalton Wolfe, a probie mere months out of the academy, stopped and offered to show her how to do it. Even though Clarke had been one of the people brought in to cover for those injured in the truck bombing years ago and never left.

Buck very quickly herded him far, far away from Clarke, before she could murder the kid with an oxygen tank.

(“One of the instructors is a woman, and he does not take her seriously. At all,” Ravi complained to them, months ago. “He’s like, middle of the class? But he’s tried correcting her more than once.”)

While the probie was busy in the station’s gym in between calls, or rather, busy watching himself flex in the mirror, Buck let slip to Hen and Chimney that the guy’s uncle was one of the chiefs. Which of course meant that the entire station knew within the hour. Not that they needed the gossip mongers for that, because Eddie caught Wolfe bragging about his Uncle Randolf to some of the others shortly afterwards. Either way, everyone knew who to properly direct their ire towards when the time came, which had almost definitely been Buck’s intent.

Wolfe was excitable in a way that was similar to, but not nearly as endearing as, Buck. The probie very clearly got into the job for the thrill of it, rather than to help people, because he would leap when the bell rang and then droop in visible disappointment when it turned out to be a minor traffic accident or something medical. He got very excited when they were sent out because of an actual fire, nearly tripping as he rushed into his turn-out gear, though it didn’t last long once they got there.

“Dispatch said a man broke in after hours,” Bobby explained as he led them into an accounting firm. Wolfe’s face, when they encountered a lot of smoke in the halls and no flames, was almost comicable. “Tried to burn the office down. LAPD’s on their way, but the caller said the victim’s no longer a threat.”

Ravi and Jones didn’t go up with them, opting to wait by the engine with the excuse of bringing up any equipment they needed. Personally, Eddie figured that Ravi, at least, just wanted distance between himself and Wolfe.

“And all those banker’s boxes equal more than a few matchboxes,” Hen remarked.

“Many unhappy returns,” Chimney quipped.

“Why didn’t the sprinklers go off?” Buck asked, glancing at the ceiling as Fire Marshall Buckley made a brief reappearance.

“Somebody’s quick on their feet,” Eddie replied, pointing out the broken glass in the fire extinguisher case, which was missing. “Avoided some serious damage.”

The office they piled into smelled of cooked meat. There was a man laying on the ground, surrounded by burnt tax returns, all in black except for his legs. His legs were red and blistered and charred.

“I tried to move him, but his skin fell off,” a man, the most stereotypical looking accountant Eddie had ever seen, was telling Bobby. “I didn’t know what to do.”

“You did your part in putting out the fire,” the captain assured him. “Now let’s step aside and let my team finish here. Buck, Eddie, check for hotspots. Chim, with me.”

Chimney followed Bobby and the accountant into one of the other offices. Hen and Albert knelt down by the vic and got to work, while Buck and Eddie started hitting any spots still smoking. Wolfe, not having been given a job by Bobby, simply stood back and watched, peering over the paramedics’ shoulders. Which was fine, as long as he wasn’t in the way.

Too bad that the probie apparently couldn’t stop himself.

“Pulse is weak,” Albert reported, feeling at the vic’s neck.

“And breathing is shallow,” Hen replied, stethoscope on and pressed against the man’s chest. “Sir, can you hear me? Can you tell me your name?”

The man didn’t even twitch.

“No response,” she said, needlessly. “Probably burn shock.”

Albert started to say something back, reaching into his bag to pull out the oxygen, but he was interrupted by Wolfe blurting out,

“Just use the salts.”

Both paramedics paused what they were doing to stare up at him. If looks could kill, they’d be burying the kid.

“We don’t know the damage done to his respiratory system,” Hen explained, tone sharp enough to peel flesh.

“But-”

“Probie!” Buck barked, shoving the fire extinguisher he’d been using into his chest and cutting off whatever else was about to be said. Wolfe let out an “oof” and fumbled to grab it before it hit the ground. “If it smokes, spray it.”

“I know how to do my job,” the probie whined.

“So do they. Now do yours and let them do theirs.” Buck eyed him for a bit longer as the kid sulked but did as he was told, before handing Eddie a burnt wallet. “I found this over there, I think it belongs to the perp. Take it to Bobby for me?”

“The perp, huh?” he teased, taking the wallet and handing his husband the extinguisher he’d been using. “It’s so far away, but I guess I can.”

Buck rolled his eyes, but he also smiled, so it was a win in Eddie’s book.

Bobby was in an adjacent office, standing just inside the doorway as he watched Chimney finish checking the accountant, who was holding an oxygen mask of his own to his face. The captain turned when Eddie walked in, curious about what he was coming to him about.

“Buck found this,” Eddie explained, holding out the wallet for him to take. “He thinks it belongs to our would-be arsonist.”

Bobby opened it and took out a driver’s license, half of it melted and bubbled.

“Mason Lewis,” he read aloud, and then handed the ID to the accountant. “You know him?”

The accountant set down the oxygen mask, much to Chimney’s apparent displeasure, and took a moment to put his glasses back on.

“He’s a client who’s being audited,” he explained, squinting at the picture on the warped piece of plastic.

“If he was as smart about his finances as he was about setting fires,” Chimney said, packing his bag back up, “I think I know why he’s being audited.”

Bobby took the ID back and slipped it into the wallet.

“Remind me to give this to Hen, so she can hand it over at the ER,” the captain said. He glanced at Eddie. “What did Wolfe do? I heard Buck.”

“Had an opinion on the proper way to treat the victim.”

“Again?”

“Again.”

Bobby sighed and looked skyward; Eddie assumed he was praying for strength. It wasn’t the first time that Wolfe offered his unsolicited advice in regards to treating patients, although Eddie couldn’t decide if he thought he knew better than the paramedics, or if he thought he knew better than Hen. Either way, they were on an inevitable countdown to the moment someone finally lost their shit on him.

Eddie made a note to himself to make sure there was popcorn stocked at the station.

Hen radioed that the patient was ready for transport, and they stepped out into the hall in time to see the guy being wheeled out on the gurney, covered in a foil blanket. Buck and Wolfe were trailing behind, though they both paused to let the paramedics go on. Or rather, Buck paused and then stopped the probie from going on without him, probably out of fear that he’d say or do something else if he was without supervision.

“If you experience any dizziness or lightheadedness, get yourself to an ER,” Bobby instructed the accountant.

“I can’t,” the accountant said, already hurrying to his office. “I have to see what I can salvage tonight…”

“It’s like they say, Cap,” Chimney said as he walked past them to catch up with Hen and Albert. “Only two things in this life are inevitable.”

“Death and taxes.” Buck looked so damn proud of himself, and also very cute. It was a good thing Eddie had self-control.

“Sometimes they go together.” Bobby let out a sigh, and then turned on the probie, glaring at him. “Get to the engine.”

Even Buck cowered a bit at the man’s tone, a pavlovian response to the times he was the one getting scolded. Wolfe hunkered down in his turn-out coat and hurried for the stairs, Bobby on his heels. The captain was probably waiting to start the lecture until they were out of earshot of any civilians.

“How long are we stuck with him?” Eddie asked, keeping his voice low so neither of the men ahead of them could hear him. Although he already knew the answer. “Assuming he doesn’t miraculously stop being a douchebag.”

“Until Pops can make him somebody else’s problem,” Buck said, sounding exhausted at the prospect. “Or he does something that his uncle can’t protect him from.”

“Or until Hen kills him.”

“Or that.”

Eddie wasn’t his soulmate.

Buck had always been a romantic. This wasn’t a secret, anyone who saw him with Abby and then with Eddie - Ali had never been in Los Angeles often enough for it to really show - would be able to guess. It was true even when he was panting after anyone and everyone that would offer him even a shadow of the affection he craved. It was that sense of romance, of “you go, we go,” that led him to Los Angeles.

Back when he was in his Greek mythology phase, the same that just about every kid seemed to go through, he read about their take on soulmates. The tale of how, way back at the beginning, people had four arms and four legs and two heads. They were powerful and fearless, with the pride to match. 

Zeus, king of the gods, split them in half. He tore them asunder, so everyone had only one head and two arms and two legs. These new people yearned for their other halves, body and soul. They were forever in search of them because, when they did find their other half, they would know no greater joy.

Buck had always liked the idea of a soulmate. He liked the thought that somewhere out in the world, there was a person made just for him. Someone who would look at him and see him, all of him, and not be disgusted or disappointed. Someone who would accept him, warts and all. Someone that wouldn’t leave him behind as they searched for their own happiness.

And then he met Eddie, and the thought of a soulmate didn’t sit quite as comfortably with him.

It wasn’t because Buck didn’t think Eddie could be his soulmate. If pressed, he would cheerfully point out how Eddie fit all of those things up there. How Eddie looked at him and and saw him and didn’t turn away, how he accepted him even when he was at his worst, how he didn’t leave Buck behind in search of his own happiness because his happiness was with Buck. Eddie was every bit the other half his younger self yearned for.

The problem was Shannon. Not that Buck thought of her as a problem, far from it. He knew Eddie still loved her, even if it was twisted with bitterness and grief, and that was okay, because he also knew that his husband’s heart was big enough for the both of them. And that Chris still loved her went without saying, really.

The problem was Abby. Not that Buck thought of her as a problem, or even thought of her all that often these days, to be honest. For all that there was a scarred part of his own heart with her name on it, for all that their relationship died long before he admitted it, he loved her once. Or thought he did, perhaps even truly had, though that torch never burned as bright or as hot as the one he held for Eddie.

The problem was that, if Eddie was Buck’s soulmate, and if Buck was Eddie’s, then… Then that would mean that Shannon and Abby had been mistakes, wouldn’t it? If they were soulmates and therefore destined to be together, then Shannon and Abby were distractions, obstacles on their way to each other, and that did not sit well with Buck.

Even though the way Abby let it die was casually cruel, being with her had changed Buck for the better. Being with Abby had brought him Carla, who was such a godsend and almost worth the broken heart all on her own. Being with Abby had shown him what he’d really been chasing after with all of those that came before. And though he knew almost everyone around him, even Carla to an extent, held a grudge against the woman, he couldn’t quite bring himself to do the same.

Buck wouldn’t take it well if someone tried to tell him that Abby had been a mistake, and he knew that Eddie would be the same with Shannon. For all his opinions on the way she left her family, for all that he sympathized with her even as he still felt anger on his boys’ behalf at the thought of it, Buck could never bring himself to think of her as Eddie’s mistake. Not the mother of his child, not the woman who was his husband’s first love.

So Eddie wasn’t his soulmate, because Shannon wasn’t a mistake.

It was Monday, the day after a twenty-four hour shift that shouldn’t have been as exhausting as it turned out to be. It was Monday, after a shift Buck spent running around preventing or putting out all the metaphorical fires the new probie seemed intent on setting. It was Monday, and the reason that Buck was ruminating on soulmates and past relationships was simple: that afternoon, Eddie picked Chris up from school and took him to visit Shannon.

This was a regular occurrence, although there wasn’t a set schedule to it. Every so often, either because Chris wanted to or a certain day was coming up, Eddie packed a picnic basket and they went to the cemetery where she was buried. Buck never went with them, not because he wasn’t invited, but because he wasn’t hers - and she wasn’t his - in the way Eddie and Chris were. He never knew her, didn’t love her, the way they did. He opted instead to let them have that time together with her, while he did his own thing.

These days, “his own thing” usually translated into spending time with Abuela or trying to keep Gavin entertained. Today it was the latter, Buck stretched out on his stomach in Gavin’s room, pushing toy cars down the cartoon roads of the playmat. He was making over the top noises to go along with them, the toddler laughing at his antics, when he heard the front door open and close, followed by footsteps and the tapping of crutches.

Chris was always a bit more subdued after the graveside picnics, so Buck’s not terribly surprised when the boy popped his head in just long enough to say hello before disappearing into his room. There’s the sound of kitchen cabinets opening and closing, along with talking that Buck can’t quite make out, before Eddie came down the hall as well. 

Like Chris, Eddie was also subdued after the visit. Unlike their son, he walked into the room and sat down on the floor with them, grabbing a toy car and making it crash into the one Buck was currently playing with.

“Jerk,” Buck complained without any heat. “How was it?”

“It was fine,” Eddie said, giving his car up when Gavin made grabby hands at it. They’d have to start working on the tot’s manners, though Buck had no idea when or how to go about that. “He caught her up on all the school gossip.”

“I’ll have to ask him for it, I’m a couple weeks behind.” The PTO members gossiped just as much as the kids, but they only met once a month or so. “How did he do on that math test? Did he say?”

“He aced it.”

“That’s my boy.”

“Like you had anything to do with it, Mathematician,” Eddie said, shoving at his head. “We stopped and got stuff to make s’mores.”

“Isn’t that something he did with Shannon?” Buck asked. He watched his husband’s eyes go soft at the question.

“It was, mi sol,” he said. “How was it here?”

“Q-word.” He ignored his husband’s scoff; he was not going to risk it, damn it. “We ate a snack, though I think Hoover got most of it, and then we played. Abuela spent most of the time on the phone, chatting with… I think church people? Apparently someone got caught cheating on their wife.”

“Riveting.”

“I thought so.”

They fell into an easy silence for a bit, except for the sound of Gavin’s babbling and the over-the-top explosions Buck made when they crashed cars. It lasted for several moments, until the toddler got bored of what he was doing and got up to dig through the pile of stuffed animals in the corner. He pulled one out, dragged it over to show it off to them, before tossing it down and going for another.

“I miss her,” Eddie said.

“I know,” Buck said.

“I love you.”

“I know that, too,” he said, and then pushed himself up, letting out as melodramatic of a groan as possible. “Okay, little guy. Why don’t we put away our cars before pulling out anything else? Can we do that?”

Gavin looked him right in the eye and shook his head.

Eddie was too busy laughing to be of any help.

The Fun in Funeral

Chapter Summary

In which someone crashes a funeral. Or rather, in which someone crashes into a funeral.

“I cannot believe,” Eddie said, gathering plates off the dining table and taking them to the sink, for Buck to rinse, “that you and Maddie didn’t talk about it ahead of time.”

It was their next shift, and they were cleaning up after lunch. The ones assigned to that task, at least, which meant Hen and Albert were downstairs making sure the ambulances were stocked, and the probie was… Buck paused in scrubbing the plate he was working on to crane his neck around, relaxing only minutely when he caught sight of Wolfe on the other end of the station’s loft.

The probie was chatting to Jones, who was very clearly paying more attention to the book he was reading than to anything the kid was saying. Which was most definitely the best possible scenario, so Buck let it go.

Anyways, the current drama was this: Chimney and Maddie were being audited by the IRS. Apparently, so Chimney anxiously explained, he and Maddie filed their taxes separately and both did so as Head of Household. And they also both claimed Jee-Yun as a dependent. Which was apparently tax fraud. And now they were being summoned directly to the nearest IRS office to plead their case, or something.

Buck wasn’t entirely certain how it worked, even after looking it up.

“We did talk about it!” Chimney protested. “She said she was going to, and then I told her I already had. I mean, I- I pay plenty of the expenses, right?”

“You know, Eds and I don’t have this problem,” Buck couldn’t help but point out. “We filed our taxes together, which we can do because we’re married. If you just hurried up and made an honest woman of my sister…”

“Yeah, yeah, yeah,” the other man said, flapping a hand to shut him up. “It would literally be your dream come true, whatever. Not helping.”

“There’s apparently only a point-six percent chance of being audited at all,” Buck continued, because sometimes he was a little shit. “Still greater odds than being struck by lightning.”

“That is also not helping,” Chimney said, after a moment where they all stared at Buck.

“Neither are you,” Bobby pointed out, shoving a dirty plate into his hand. The paramedic had been standing there catastrophizing the entire time instead of helping clear the table, so it was a fair statement.

“Look, don’t beat yourself up, Chim,” Ravi tried to console as he deliberately stacked plates beside the sink, making sure none of them slipped into the water and splashed everywhere. “I’m not great at doing my taxes, either. No one is.”

“I am,” Buck was quick to correct.

“Right, sorry. No one is great at doing their taxes except for the Mathematician.”

Buck glared at him, and then flicked dirty water at Ravi’s face.

“I actually thought about using my newfound math skills to file our taxes by hand this year,” he said.

“So, did you?”

“No, I said I thought about it. Eddie and I hired a professional.”

“He was a great guy,” Eddie commented. “Very helpful.”

“Still not helping,” Chimney complained.

“Why are you and Maddie doing your own taxes, anyway?” Bobby asked him.

“We’re using a computer program instead of a real person,” the paramedic sheepishly admitted. “Because it’s cheaper.”

“Until they hit you with the fines because neither of you actually talked before filing,” Eddie pointed out. “A real person would have definitely noticed that you were both claiming Head of Household and Jee-Yun.”

“You guys think I’m a goner, don’t you?” Chimney asked, drooping a little. “Maddie and I are definitely going to debtor’s prison.”

“Georgia wasn’t too bad when I was there,” Buck told him. “You might like it.”

However much Chimney might like the state of Georgia, the man clearly did not like Buck’s contributions to the conversation. The station’s alarm went off before he could shoot back with anything, though.

“Remember,” Buck heard Bobby say as they headed for the trucks, “With the IRS, you can run, but you can’t hide.”

The call they were responding to was at a funeral home. Someone literally crashed a funeral, driving their car through the wall and into the mourners gathered. They were all expecting a mess when they got there, as was the case with mass casualty events, and they were not disappointed.

There was a car in the middle of the chapel, the driver, an older man, was dazed and already out, though he was still lingering by the driver’s seat. There was an older woman pinned beneath it, a pretty lady in black beside her. There was a trail of destruction leading from the car to a hole in the wall, and a semicircle of debris in front of it. The guests and well-wishers were crowded as far from the car as they could get, and most of them were covered in dust.

“We were just getting started when it crashed through,” the lady in black said, getting up and giving them room. “And then it landed on top of Marie.”

“She’s breathing,” Eddie announced, kneeling down and holding a gloved hand in front of the old woman’s - Marie’s - nose. Buck bent over, peering under the car for a look at what they were dealing with.

“Looks like this angel was literally watching over her,” he said, pointing out the statue that prevented the car from crushing Marie entirely. As it was, she was merely pinned.

“Alright, Hen, Albert, get the driver out of here,” Bobby ordered. “Buck, Eddie, I want airbags. Get this car lifted and stabilized. The rest of you, start triaging.”

There was a series of “yes, Cap”s from the others. Buck handed Eddie a c-collar from the bag, before getting to his feet and heading for the trucks. Hen and Albert were checking over the driver by the entrance - “I saw two parking spaces, then there was just the one…” - and Bobby was talking into his radio, requesting more RA units.

Unfortunately, Buck needed another pair of hands. Fortunately, he knew a pair that wasn’t as needed.

“Probie!” he hollered, and watched Wolfe jump in place before rushing to his side. While the probie was an EMT, just like the rest of them, he was the bottom of the food chain and someone they’d miss least. “Come on.”

They went back to the trucks, where Buck opened one of the compartments and started dragging out the two-by-fours they kept for this reason, along with chocks. He dumped most of them into the kid’s arms, before grabbing the pair of high-pressure airbags, along with the hoses and tank of air.

“Do we really need these?” Wolfe asked, hurrying along behind him as he went back inside. “Won’t the airbags keep it up?”

“Unless the bags slip and the car moves,” Buck told him. “Then the car falls back on the patient. And maybe one of us.”

They passed the driver being pulled along on a gurney by Albert and one of the others, on his way to an ambulance. Back inside, they found Hen had moved on to a woman whose leg was stretched out in front of her, with Ravi assisting. Eddie stood from where he’d been checking over the trapped Marie, and both he and Buck started getting the car ready for lift.

The chocks went behind and in front of the wheels, except for the one pinning their victim. Buck had Wolfe help him set up the lift stack from the two-by-fours, while Eddie and Bobby hooked the bags up to the air. When the lift stack was done and simply waiting for the airbags, they started on the crib stack right next to it, Buck talking as he did.

“This needs to go as close to the car’s framework as we can,” he explained. “We don’t want to lose any potential lift by building up with just the airbags. We use the cribbing for it.”

“Yeah, I know,” the probie said, almost snappish. Buck fixed him with a look, debating if it was worth pointing out how the kid’s earlier question implied that, no, he didn’t know.

“You need to be sure,” was what Buck settled with.

Eddie knelt beside them, sliding the two airbags onto the lift stack.

“You ready?” he asked as he stood back up, the control for the bags in hand. Buck nodded, holding up a two-by-four in a salute.

Lifting the car up with the airbags was a slow and steady process. They alternated between the bags, inflating the bottom one first and then the top, being sure to never inflate them too much, too fast. If they inflated them too much, as Buck explained to Wolfe, then they’d risk losing stability because of a pillow effect. And they wanted to leave one bag softer and the other firmer, so they bonded and didn’t slip out.

Buck wasn’t certain if the kid appreciated the impromptu lecture, but if he didn't, that was too bad for him.

Eddie paused in inflating the bags at regular intervals, allowing Buck to build the crib stack up. When they finally got the car high enough to safely pull the woman out, and Buck had the two-by-fours stacked high enough and stable enough to hold it there, Eddie slowly deflated the bags. They watched, holding their breath a little, as the car’s weight rested on the wood and then settled.

“Alright, let’s get her out,” Bobby instructed. Buck and Eddie moved to do just that, gripping her shoulders and arms tight. “On three. One, two…”

They got Marie out from under the car easily, pulling her far enough away that they didn’t have to worry about something happening with the car.

“Hen, I’m gonna need a hand over here,” Eddie called over his shoulder. “Buck?”

“Backboard, coming up.”

The nearest gurney was on the other end of the room, where one of the additional paramedics Bobby called for had pulled it in. Buck passed by Hen on the way to getting it, and when he was done dragging it - and more importantly, the backboard on top of it - back to Eddie, Marie was awake and answering Hen’s questions.

They rolled her onto her side, Wolfe keeping her neck and head stable, and Buck slid the backboard under her. They had just set her down, and were strapping her in, when he spotted the large framed picture, laying beside the rear wheel of the car. It showed an old woman, who looked… Well, identical to Marie.

“I’m sorry for your loss,” Buck told her. “I can’t imagine how hard it must have been to lose your twin.”

And then to have the funeral interrupted like this…

“My twin?” the old woman asked, confused at the statement. Buck was just about to ask Hen or Eddie if the old woman had a concussion, before Marie glanced over at the picture as well and clearly realized what he was talking about. “Oh, no, honey, that’s me.”

“That’s- What?” Buck met Eddie’s eyes; his husband was clearly as confused as he was. “This is your funeral?”

“I was sitting right over there,” Marie said, gesturing as best she could with her arms mostly immobilized by the straps. “What better place to be for your own funeral than the front row?”

“It’s called a living funeral.” The lady that was kneeling by Marie when they arrived had come back. There was a makeshift bandage wrapped around one of her hands.

“Natalia planned it for me,” Marie said. “She’s my death doula.”

“Death doula?” Hen repeated. “I’m only familiar with the other kind.”

“I prefer ‘end of life’ doula,” the lady, Natalia, said.

That was interesting. A death - end of life - doula sounded interesting. A living funeral sounded interesting. Attending his own funeral, seeing genuine proof of whether or not people would actually miss him if he was gone, was… Interesting.

“Okay, we need room to get her on the gurney,” Hen said, distracting Buck from his thoughts.

“Wolfe, could you take her over there?” he asked, and then started second-guessing it the moment he looked at him. The probie was staring wide-eyed at Natalia, and there was nothing good that could come from him having an expression like that. “Actually, nevermind, I can-”

“I got it,” Wolfe said, leaping to and nearly tripping over his feet. “Uh, let me take you over here…”

Buck watched the pair go, and decided it was absolutely not his problem.

“Try not to move, ma’am,” Hen instructed Marie, after they got the old woman onto the gurney. Eddie had his stethoscope on and was checking her vitals. “You may have dislocated your hip.”

“It happens all the time,” Marie said, very much not concerned. “A symptom of my everlasting fight with bone cancer.”

“Don’t lose hope, yet,” Eddie advised, draping the stethoscope around his neck.

“This isn’t about losing hope,” the old woman corrected. “You see all those people there? I haven’t seen any of them in years. I invited them out to this ceremony so that I didn’t have to see them from a casket.” She let out a little scoff. “Where’s the fun in that?”

“That’s one way of throwing a family reunion,” Hen quipped, but Buck thought she sounded a bit uneasy.

“We’re all gonna die alone,” Marie said. “Might as well spend time with our loved ones while we’re still here.”

Buck couldn’t decide if that was a morbid thought or a nice one. It might, perhaps, be a little bit of both. From the looks of how uncomfortable the other two appeared, he figured they couldn’t decide, either.

Eggs

Chapter Summary

In which there are eggs.

Chapter Notes

content warning: homophobic language

“You’ve been stuck in your head,” Eddie said, breaking the tranquil silence that had fallen over the house. “Again. Wanna tell me what’s up?”

Buck had been in his head since the middle of their shift yesterday. It wasn’t unusual, sometimes his brain was a twisted labyrinth and he got lost in it. Sometimes it was literally a twisted labyrinth and he literally got lost in it. But he figured the bupropion was supposed to help with that. Maybe a week was too early for it to really kick in, or maybe it never would. He hoped it would, because if he went through a week or more of his mouth turning into a desert and his throat feeling like sandpaper, accompanied by random bouts of dizziness, without anything to show for it?

Well, if that was going to be the case, something was getting set on fire.

Anyways, Buck was stuck in his head and he knew it. He just wasn’t certain if Eddie would be pleased if Buck admitted that he was thinking about Marie’s living funeral and Natalia the death doula. No, scratch that, he was absolutely certain that Eddie would not, in fact, be pleased.

They spent that morning and early afternoon keeping both Gavin and Jee-Yun entertained, after Maddie and Chimney dropped her off on their way to their meeting with the IRS. Watching them together wasn’t as easy as it used to be, ever since Gavin got his legs under him, but he and Eddie kept them trapped- Er, contained in Gavin’s room, so the hurricane of destruction wasn’t too bad. And after a few hours, they managed to wear themselves down into a well-earned nap.

Which was when Buck was ambushed by his own husband.

“What did your mom want this morning?” he asked, rather than answer Eddie’s own question.

Eddie had been on the phone with her as he and Chris rushed to get out the door that morning. Buck meant to ask him already, but then he got distracted and forgot until now. Which was fine, because now it was serving as a very good, if unsubtle, change in topics.

“She was wanting to see us again,” his husband said, and Buck did his best to clamp down on the reflexive grimace.

Helena and Ramon Diaz were better than his own (blood) parents, but that wasn’t really saying much. And Buck liked them, mostly, for all that he still didn’t quite trust them, but he liked them better when they were in a different timezone. Something that was reinforced during their visit for Gavin’s birthday, when Buck kept having to stave off their unsolicited parenting critiques.

(Honestly, it had mostly been Helena, and Buck just smiled and nodded and continued on as he was doing.)

“They were just up here last month,” he pointed out, trying to keep from just blurting out a blunt “No.” They were Eddie’s parents, he wasn’t going to tell him his parents couldn’t visit if he wanted to see them. Even if he was still salty about how Helena took him aside during the birthday party and asked if it was a good idea to have such an energetic dog around the boys, one that might knock them over while he jumped and ran around, because Gavin or Chris could be hurt.

“I told them our schedule was filled, so we’d have to wait and see about a good time for it.” Oh thank God. “But back to you. What’s going on up here?”

Eddie reached up and tapped Buck on the forehead. Buck reached up and smacked the offending finger away.

“Nothing, I swear, it’s…” he sighed, knowing that Eddie wasn’t believing him in the slightest. “It’s stupid. You won’t like it.”

“Try me.”

Buck sighed again. After making sure the monitor was on, in case Gavin or Jee-Yun woke up, he led Eddie into the privacy of their own bedroom. He didn’t think Eddie would be upset enough that there’d be a fight, but then again…

“Okay,” he started. “You remember that call yesterday? The funeral?”

“I do,” Eddie said, heading for the chair in the corner. He paused to make sure the monitor receiver was on, before sitting down. “Mi sol, have you been fixating on what the old woman, Marie said? Before you went off to find the probie?”

“No,” Buck said, fidgeting where he stood. “Well, a- a little.”

“Of course,” his husband sighed, which… Okay, that was fair. Buck did have a track record when it came to old dying people on calls and the way he spiraled over what they said. Thomas, Red, Lev; Eddie was right to be jumping to the conclusion that he was caught up in Marie’s statement about how everyone died alone.

Except that wasn’t the part he was fixated on this time. He’d get to that later, when his brain cycled back around to it. This time, he was focusing on the conversation he had after that one, the one with Natalia the death doula.

After they got Marie secured on the gurney, Buck had gone in search of Wolfe, to get started on the clean-up. He found the kid with Natalia, who had fresh gauze wrapped around her hand and was looking somewhat uncomfortable as he chatted her up. She perked up when she spotted Buck, which had Wolfe noticing his arrival.

“This was the guy I was telling you about,” the kid told her, which meant Buck was too curious now to just grab him and go.

“The one who was struck by lightning and died?” she asked, sounding genuinely excited about it.

“Uh, yeah, that’s me.”

“That is so freakin’ cool.”

“Uh.” That was not the reaction Buck was used to. It was kinda nice. “I don’t know. Cool?”

“Well, wasn’t it?” Natalia asked, stepping closer. It conveniently put her farther away from Wolfe, and Buck wasn’t sure how much of that was coincidental. “Come on, be honest.”

“I think it’s very cool,” the probie cut in, evidently tired of not being part of the conversation.

Buck had stammered a bit in response, unsure what to really say. Bobby thankfully called for them both at that point, rescuing him from the conversation. Physically, at least.

“Buck?” Eddie’s voice drew him back into the present. 

“Yeah, sorry, I…” He shook his head and did his best to focus his thoughts. “I haven’t been thinking about Marie, I was, uh. I was thinking about Natalia. The death doula.”

“What about her?” his husband carefully asked.

“Well, I mean… I was thinking of reaching out to her.”

“Why?”

“See how Marie’s doing, maybe? Or ask her more about her job, it sounded interesting,” Buck said, fidgeting nervously. “But she, uh… She thought it was neat. The, um. The lightning.”

As he watched, Eddie’s flat expression transformed into an angry scowl. His fingers dug into the arm of the chair he was sitting in, and he took in a sharp breath, and then another, before pushing himself up.

“She thought it was neat that you were struck by lightning?” he said. “That you died?”

“Yeah. She thought it was neat. And when I talked to her, I felt like she saw me-”

“She saw you?” Eddie spat the words out. “Like I don’t see you, all of you, every fucking-”

“She saw me and not my corpse!” Buck shouted at him, shutting his husband up. Eddie stared at him, eyes wide and lips parted. “She thinks it’s cool. And she’s right, it is cool!”

“How is it cool? You died! I had to lower your body to the ground, I had to drive the ambulance to the ER, I was the one that got your heart started back. I had to watch you lay in that bed for days, hooked to those machines, and…” Eddie dragged his hand through his hair. “That’s cool?”

“If it had happened to some other firefighter at some other station, you’d be thinking the same thing.”

“But it didn’t happen to some other firefighter, it happened to you . In front of me.”

“Yeah, I know, and it sucks,” Buck told him. “Maddie spent days popping in just to make sure I didn’t keel over behind her back, and she still sometimes looks at me like I might die again at any moment. Copeland wants to talk about how I feel, about how it’s affecting me. Whenever I make a joke about it at work, everyone stares at me like they’re appalled and want to start scolding me for it. And you…”

“And me?” Eddie prompted when he trailed off. “What am I doing that’s so bad?”

“Nothing, Eds. Nothing you’re doing, that anyone is doing, is bad. It’s just, it's not a trauma for her. She doesn’t know me, she wasn’t there. She just… She thought it was cool. Not horrifying, not traumatic. It’s just a neat story to her. Like I met a celebrity, or won the lottery, or rescued a puppy from a well.”

“Why didn’t you tell me?” Eddie asked, after a minute where they both silently looked everywhere but at one another. “If you’ve been feeling this way, why didn’t you say anything?”

“I didn’t realize how much it was bothering me, not until the way she reacted,” Buck told him. “But babe, is there any way I could have said it that didn’t have you feeling guilty? Because you can’t tell me you aren’t feeling guilty right now, even though you shouldn’t. It’s not your fault.”

“If you feel like you can’t talk to me…”

“I feel like I’m poking an open wound,” he said, stepping forward and cupping his husband’s face. “Because you had to lower my body to the ground, you had to get me to the ER. You had to come home and tell Chris, and I know, Eds. I know what that’s like.”

Because he did. Buck remembered far too well what it had been like to be in Eddie’s shoes. Except he didn’t have to wait days for news on whether or not Eddie would be okay, not like his family had to do with him.

“How did it even come up in conversation?” Eddie asked him, loosely wrapping his fingers around Buck’s wrists. “Did you just walk up and say, hey, I’ve got a story for you?”

“No, uh. I think the probie was using me to flirt with her?” Buck said, and his husband rolled his eyes so hard he was worried they were going to pop out. “Yeah, I know. Anyway, uh… Don’t you need to check my blood pressure? You haven’t done that today.”

Buck figured he knew why his husband was so anal about it, why he was so militant about him staying hydrated ever since he started complaining of a dry mouth. It was like Eddie had said, he’d been the one to lower him from the ladder, the one to sit by his bedside day after day. So he figured that, after a conversation like the one they just had, doing it might make him feel a bit better.

But-

“We can do it later,” Eddie said, surprising him.

“Really?”

“Yeah.” He gripped one of Buck’s wrists tighter, moved it so he could place a kiss in the middle of his palm. “Might be better if we wait for you to calm down.”

“If you’re sure…”

“I am,” Eddie said, stepping away without letting go of him. “We should probably be waking the tots up. If we let them sleep for too much longer, they’ll be awful to put to bed. I know Gavin will be.”

“What if,” Buck said, pulling him closer again. “What if we just wake up Gavin and let Jee sleep a bit longer?”

“And what, let Chimney and your sister deal with a grumpy kid tonight?”

“Not my problem.”

Eddie rolled his eyes again and started dragging him out of their bedroom.

Buck was working on dinner, when there was knocking at the door. It wasn’t unusual for them to have visitors, but they didn’t often have people just drop by. He paused in the middle of stirring tomato sauce into the beef he cooked, looking up in time to see Eddie get off the couch, where he’d been watching television with Abuela, and answer the door. 

“Hey, Eddie,” he heard May say as she came in. “Hola, Isabel. And hello, Gavin.”

Buck watched from the kitchen as she bent down to smother the toddler sitting on the floor in kisses.

“Hey, May,” Buck called out. “What’s brought you over?”

“Can’t a girl just come over and see her step-brother?” May asked, walking through the dining room to sit at the breakfast bar. Eddie shrugged at him, before sitting back down on the couch. “What are you making?”

“Bobby’s lasagna,” Buck answered. “You’re welcome to stay for dinner.”

“That’s tempting,” she said. “I didn’t see Chris in there.”

“He’s in his room. But really, what’s brought you over?”

“I was on my way to visit Mom and Bobby,” May answered, tugging at her hair. “Figured I’d stop by and say hello, you know? See how you were doing.”

“That makes sense,” he said, nodding. She nodded too, like a bobblehead, before finally breaking and coming around to wrap her arms around his middle. He recovered from the surprise of it quickly, putting everything down so he could return the hug. “Alright, who told you what?”

“Maddie,” she admitted, mostly into his shirt. “We talk sometimes, about… You know.”

“That makes sense,” he said, patting at her back.

“Do you want to talk about it?” May asked, pulling away. “Because I’m here if you want to. I am perfectly willing.”

“Thank you,” he said, trying to put the gratitude he felt into the two words. “Not right now, though.”

“That’s fine. But any time, I promise. Even if I’m studying for a test, or in the middle of class.”

“What about when you’re with Darius?” Buck teased. She rolled her eyes and smacked at him, before moving to sit back down at the breakfast bar. “How are things doing with him, anyway?”

“We’re doing fine,” she answered primly. “He’s taking me on a date this weekend.”

“Better be a good date,” he told her, going back to fixing the lasagna. “You know, if you wanted, you could bring him to dinner again.”

“No, thank you. I’m lucky your caveman routine didn’t have him running after the last dinner he was invited to..”

“That would have been a shame,” Buck said, making no effort at sounding sincere. He grinned when she flipped him off for it. “I meant what I said, earlier. You’re more than welcome to stay for dinner.”

“Maybe. Mom’s been-” She pressed her face into her elbow, just in time to smother a series of sneezes. “Ew, I did not think ahead.”

“We have Claritin,” Buck offered, grabbing some tissues and handing them over.

“Maybe, if I do stay.” She wiped at her nose and then her elbow, grimacing as she did. “Anyway, Mom’s been focused on that dead guy, though. The one that she helped arrest for embezzlement, and then he died in her backseat.”

“I thought he died at the hospital?”

“Whatever,” she said, waving the distinction away. “It’s bothering her a lot, it’s why I was heading over.”

“Yeah, Pops mentioned it…”

They talked a bit longer as Buck finished putting together the lasagna. Her sneezing got worse, until she decided to head on over to Bobby and Athena’s as he put dinner into the oven. She was getting up and waving off his offer to walk her to her car, when there was the sound of something smacking into the front window. It set off Hoover, who started barking up a storm, which in turn set off Gavin.

“What in the hell?” Buck asked no one in particular, rushing for the living room. The window was covered in- “Is that egg?”

“It looks it,” Eddie said, bouncing a hiccuping Gavin in his arms. “I don’t know what-”

There was a crack as another egg smacked into the glass, yellow yolk smearing everywhere. Two more quickly followed it. Buck headed for the door, to see what the hell was going on, and Eddie joined him after passing their kid off to Abuela.

Outside, on the sidewalk, was a pair of boys that Buck almost recognized. Both of them were around Chris’ age, one older and one younger, and they had an opened carton of eggs between them. They were in the middle of grabbing another one to throw when the sight of the adults startled them into freezing.

“What are you doing!?” Eddie shouted at them, following Buck down the porch steps.

“Fags!” the oldest shouted, before he and his brother turned tail and ran, dropping the carton as they did. They went for Edward and Shanna’s house, slamming the front door closed behind them.

“Eds, did… Did we just get hate crimed?” Buck asked, looking between the egg covered window and the carton laying on the sidewalk. “By children?”

Eddie did not answer, too busy marching his way across the street. He was visibly fuming, so much so that Buck half expected steam to be pouring out his husband’s ears. The man made a direct path of Edward and Shanna’s door, and he started pounding on it the moment he reached it.

Edward yanked it open, greeting them with a,

“What do you want?”

It was so curt, nothing like the friendliness he’d shown when Buck first met him and his wife.

“Your sons,” Eddie said, sounding very much like he wanted to shove past him and haul the brats out, “just threw eggs at our house and called us fags.”

“So?” Edward said.

“So?” Buck repeated. “What do you mean, so? We figured you’d want to know. They shouldn’t do that.”

Edward actually rolled his eyes and scoffed at him, before turning away and shouting into the house. Before the door slammed shut in their faces, Buck heard,

“Calvin, did you waste the eggs on the queers?”

It answered a couple lingering questions, at least.

Pride and Pettiness

Chapter Summary

In which Buck plans to fight fire with pettiness.

Eddie seethed through the rest of the night and into the morning, Buck could tell. He was angry as they stomped back to their house, and he was angry when he called the cops, and he was angry as he related to them what happened. The only person that maybe matched him was Athena, who May called while they were busy confronting the egg throwers and who showed up with Bobby before the on-duty officers did.

That anger followed like a cloud as they went in for their next shift, palpable enough that most of their coworkers stayed far, far away from Eddie where they could. Bobby hovered, just shy of being overbearing with his concern, which Buck knew the others noticed and probably had them curious. It was finally Hen that broke and asked, while they were relaxing after a call.

“Alright,” she said, sitting down in the chair next to the couch he and Eddie were curled up on, bent over Buck’s phone. They both looked up at her. “What happened that’s got that one-” She pointed at Eddie. “In a twist, and that one-” She pointed at Bobby this time, who was once again not very far away. “In mother hen mode?”

Buck glanced at Eddie before answering.

“You remember our new neighbors?” he asked.

“The ones with the names.”

“Yeah. And uh, remember that visit from Deidra we mentioned…?”

“The one that she had to do because someone called with a complaint about your lifestyle? Yeah, I remember-” Hen stopped in the middle of her sentence, stiffening in her seat, looking almost as angry as Eddie. “That was them?”

“We think so,” Buck said. Eddie scoffed. “We don’t know for sure! It was anonymous.”

“Their brats threw eggs at our house last night,” Eddie told Hen. “And when we went to tell them about it, Edward was more upset that they wasted the eggs than anything else.”

“So why do you think it was them who called?” Chimney asked, coming over and leaning against the back of Hen’s chair. Even Albert and Ravi, who’d been focused on their game of Mario Kart, paused the round to give them their attention.

“Well, one of the brats called us a slur,” Buck said. “And then Edward called us ‘the queers,’ so…”

“I am so sorry that happened to you.” Hen reached out to squeeze both his and Eddie’s knees. “Are you okay?”

“I am pissed.” Eddie told her, rather unnecessarily.

“I’m fine,” Buck said. “I mean, it’s not my first time being hate-crimed.” At that point, Bobby lost the pretense of not listening in, stomping over to join the others in staring at him. “Hey, I know I’m white and generally straight-passing, but that goes out the window when you’re caught making out with a guy in front of a gay bar.”

“Pretty sure those expressions were more horror than disbelief, mi sol,” Eddie pointed out, arm snaking around his waist. Buck just shrugged.

“So, what did you guys do?” Albert asked, setting down his controller. “What are you going to do?”

“Right now, we’re looking at pride flags we can hang up,” Buck answered, holding up his phone so they could see the screen. “Just to be extra obnoxious and rub it in their faces.”

“May was visiting them when it happened,” Bobby informed the others. “So she called Athena and I.”

“And then Pops and I had to keep Ma, Eddie, and Abuela from marching over there,” Buck said. “We called the cops, they’ll handle it. Hopefully.”

Though Buck wasn’t certain what the police would or even could do, legally speaking. He’d looked it up when they’d gone to bed that night, he and Eddie both too wound up to fall asleep easily. Egging a house was only a misdemeanor as long as the damages were less than $400 - Buck literally just wiped the window clean after one of the officers took pictures of it - and it was only legally a hate crime if they could prove that was the motivation. Being called a slur was maybe enough for that, but then again, it would just be their word against whatever the little shits and their parents said.
