“According to Athena, they’ll probably just have to pay a fine,” Bobby said, heading back to what he was doing before joining the conversation. If he’d actually been doing something, Buck wasn’t sure.

Eddie let out another scoff and shook his head in clear disgust, but he didn’t say anything.

“What flags are you looking at getting?” Hen asked, pushing out of her seat to come sit by Buck, shoving herself into his space to look at his phone.

“A rainbow one, obviously,” Buck said, leaning against Eddie to give her room.

“Obviously.”

“And a bisexual one, of course.” He clicked to add that flag to the cart and started scrolling down. Eddie’s finger tapping at the screen stopped him.

“That one, too,” his husband said. The flag he was pointing at had a black triangle on the left end, with three horizontal stripes in white, purple, and gray. Buck glanced at his face, before adding that flag, too.

“I was thinking of stopping by the store and getting a couple- Or, I guess three poles so we can have them all up,” he said, continuing on. “But I want to find one really, really big flag. Just, huge.”

“And do what?” Ravi asked. He and Albert had gone back to Mario Kart, so the question was thrown over his shoulder. “Let it hang down from your roof.”

“I hadn’t actually thought that far ahead,” Buck confessed. “But that is a good idea.”

“Oh hey, bunting.” Hen jabbed at the screen, pointing it out. “You can put that around your porch or fence.”

“You, Henrietta,” he said, eagerly adding a couple packages to the cart, as well, “are a brilliant woman.”

“I know.”

“What is that one?” Chimney asked. Buck startled a bit, because he hadn’t noticed him rounding the couch to stand behind them.

“Which one?”

“That one,” the paramedic repeated. “With the red bone.”

“Oh, uh… Hen, do you want to take this?”

“Absolutely not,” she said, shaking her head. “You do it.”

“I don’t know what that one is, either,” Eddie added, frowning. “Puppy play?”

With how fast Albert and Ravi both twisted their heads around to give them wide-eyed looks, Buck was surprised their necks didn’t break. On the screen, both of their cars went flying off the edge of Rainbow Road and were scooped up by the cloud guy.

“Yeah, no, I’m not explaining it. Especially not at work,” Buck told them both, hurriedly scrolling past it. “You can look it up later, Chim. Oh! This flag’s ten by fifteen, perfect.”

Eddie looked at it, saw the price, and yanked the phone out of Buck’s hand.

“¡Ni hablar! You are not spending that much on a flag.”

“But babe-”

“No.”

“Hey, Buck?” Chimney broke in. He sounded nervous. “Do you mind helping me with something on one of our next days off?”

“Sure. I mean, I should be able to.” Buck tried to mentally go through their calendar and then gave up, grabbing his phone back so he could pull up the actual thing. “Uh, we have our next Copeland appointment next week, and I’m taking Eddie to another Dodgers game…”

“Any day is fine, just tell me when,” he said. “Hen?”

“Oh, uh.” She blinked. “Sure?”

“Perfect!”

The paramedic turned on his heel and hurried away, leaving them to stare after him.

“Do we not get to know what he needs help with?” Eddie asked.

“Guess not,” Buck answered, pulling the store page back up. He stretched his arm to keep his phone out of Eddie’s reach when his husband went to grab it. “I’m not buying the flag, I want to see if there’s anything else on here.”

“Like what?”

“I dunno, gay lawn gnomes.”

“So when you said obnoxious…” Hen trailed off.

“I meant obnoxious,” Buck reaffirmed, though now that he thought about it… “Eds, you’re okay with it, right?”

“Yes,” the man simply said, nodding. “But I’ll take your phone again if you start buying something too expensive.”

Buck stuck his tongue out at him, before turning his attention on his phone. He and Hen went through the page, disappointed at the lack of tacky lawn decorations on offer, and went in search of another one after he paid for the flags and bunting. They unfortunately didn’t find the gay lawn gnomes he was hoping for, and Hen got sidetracked with teasing Eddie with the idea of Buck in the croptop they just scrolled past.

Bobby’s phone going off, with a ringtone that he knew meant it was Athena calling, quickly had Buck distracted. He turned to lean over the back of the couch, drawing his knees under him as he shamelessly eavesdropped. The captain pulled his phone out and answered with a soft,

“Hey honey… They’re both fine, what did you find out?... He isn’t dead?”

Buck watched him listen to whatever Athena was explaining, frowning heavily at her (inaudible to Buck) words. He started nodding and then walked over to the couch, his pressing the phone to his shoulder.

“Hen, what would happen if someone took an overdose of Lisinopril?” he asked.

“An overdose of Lisinopril?” Hen repeated as she thought about it. “It would bottom out their blood pressure for about twenty-minutes, depending on how much they took.”

“Thank you.” The captain put the phone back to his ear, relaying what she just told him. “So he could have come to in the ER, made the switch then… He was able to sneak out, but where did he go?”

With Bobby standing right by them, Buck could better hear the other end of the conversation. Enough to make out Athena’s voice, if not her words. At least until he distinctly heard her shout,

“The sneaky son of a bitch!”

Then she went silent. Bobby glanced at his phone, shrugged, and tucked it back into his pants.

“What was that about, Cap?” Eddie asked, looking up at him.

“The guy she arrested, who had a medical emergency in her custody and then died at the ER? He’s not actually dead,” Bobby answered. “I think Athena just figured out where he went, or where he’s going.”

“That’s good, right?” Buck asked. “May said it was bothering her, because it happened on her watch. This means that it’s not her fault. Right?”

“He took the Lisinopril while he was hiding in his bathroom, before they cuffed him,” Bobby told them. “So yes, that means it’s not her fault.”

The sound of a scuffle drew their attention to Ravi and Albert, who had once again forgotten their game. They were shoving lightly at each other, clearly arguing over something.

“You ask him,” Ravi said, which was followed up by Albert’s, “I’m not going to ask him, you ask him.”

“Children,” Bobby raised his voice. “If we can’t get along, I’ll take away video game privileges. And ask who, what?”

“Er.” Ravi looked at Albert; Albert looked at Ravi.

“Fine.” Albert twisted around to face them. “Cap, do you know what pup-?”

Buck wasn’t sure who moved faster to shut the younger man up, him or Hen.

The anger was a banked fire in Eddie’s belly. Before, when he and Buck rushed out of their front door to find those two boys throwing eggs at their house, when that little shit called them that word, the anger exploded through him and caught in his very marrow. It raged like a wildfire when the boys’ father simply didn’t care, when he showed more concern about the eggs being wasted than the act itself.

Eddie wasn’t a stranger to public displays of bigotry aimed in his direction. El Paso might have been right on the border, separated from Ciudad Juárez by just a river and some bridges, with people moving back and forth every day to work a job on the other side from where they lived, but that didn’t mean there weren’t racist assholes. It didn’t mean he didn’t encounter the occasional gringo that asked him things like if his parents got married for a green card.

But he had gotten comfortable here. Not because Los Angeles had a dearth of bigots, but because he’d managed to insulate himself and Christopher so well against them. Until the new probie, there hadn’t been anyone at work that caused problems in that vein, and Durand was a school that had tolerance and progressiveness as things it prided itself on.

So part of the anger had been from surprise, he knew, and from the feeling of comfort being ripped away from him. Because these were his neighbors, and this was his home, his family. He couldn’t just grit his teeth and ignore them until he was done with his job, or simply march past their stupidity. 

It fueled the fire even further to know that it was hurled at Buck, too. To see his husband cling to Gavin during and after Deidra’s visit, like he was afraid she would yank him away. To know that Buck seemed more surprised that they were hate crimed by children than that they were hate crimed at all. To see him look so utterly disappointed and dejected at the fact that the people he had thought were so friendly turned out to be anything but.

It was a different sort of anger from what he felt after Shannon, different from what raged in him through the lawsuit and the fighting. It burned brighter, perhaps, but not as steadily, faltering over the course of their shift as he focused on work and Buck’s plan for petty warfare. Though when they got home, he found himself glaring at Edward and Shanna’s house as they drove past, found himself constantly looking out the front window, ready to catch the members of the horrid brood in the act again.

Buck kept him distracted, though. A day in the park after Chris’ equine therapy, an evening on the couch spent watching melodramatic telenovelas. Chris asked to make s’mores in the oven, like he used to with Shannon, and that was what Eddie was doing now. He heard the timer go off in the kitchen and freed himself from Buck’s clutches long enough to go pull them out.

He nearly dropped the tray of s’mores - four of them: one each for the three of them, and then one for Shannon - when he turned around to find Abuela standing right behind him.

“Are you sure you don’t want one?” he asked her. She’d declined it when they were putting them together, but still. She might have changed her mind.

“I’m sure,” she said, shaking her head. “I just wanted to tell you good night, before going to bed.”

“Buenas noches, ‘Buela,” he told her, leaning in to give a peck at her cheek. He lifted up the tray. “I need to get these in there, before they come after me.”

“Oh, I don’t think that’ll be a problem,” Abuela told him, laughing, before shuffling down the hall. Hoover trailed after her, though he bypassed her door to settle down just inside of Gavin’s room.

Eddie, curious about what she meant, shrugged and carried the tray into the living room.

“Okay, guys, who’s ready for…” He trailed off when he spotted the way Buck and Chris laid against each other, both of their heads tilted in uncomfortable ways where they’d fallen asleep. It was only a matter of time before- Yup, there was Buck’s snoring.

With a sigh, he sat the tray down on the coffee table, and then grabbed the blanket off the back of the couch. Eddie debated, briefly, with carrying Chris to his room, but decided against it. Instead, he just gently took his glasses off, setting them by the tray. He did his best to adjust their necks, sticking pillows behind their heads, before easing himself back into his spot, lifting Chris’ legs so he could stretch his own under them and across Buck’s lap.

He threw the blanket over all three of them, and then he reached over to snag a still warm s’more as he settled in to watch the telenovela.

(The anger was a banked fire in Eddie’s belly.)

A Literal Dream Come True

Chapter Summary

In which there's therapy and a trip to the mall.

Copeland’s office hadn’t changed much since Buck was last in it. The walls were still pale yellow, the couch and armchairs still deep gold. There was still a mismatch of clashing throw pillows piled up on the couch, though Buck thought he spotted at least one new one. The only big change from the last time he’d been in the office was that Eddie was with him again.

“How did the appointment with Dr. Weaver go, Evan?” the therapist asked.

“It went fine,” Buck answered, tugging the furry pink pillow out of the pile and into his lap. “I have two new labels for how I’m defective.” He looked up in time to spot Copeland’s minute frown as she wrote something down. Eddie was scowling; not at him, but clearly because of him. “Sorry.”

“You don’t need to apologize for describing how you feel.”

“He put him on bupropion,” Eddie explained. Buck wasn’t sure if Copeland had known beforehand, couldn’t remember if he’d signed the disclosure so Weaver could keep her informed. Oh well.

“And how has it been so far?” she asked. “I know it’s too early to really tell its effectiveness, but have you noticed any changes…?”

“Not really,” Buck said. “My mouth’s been really dry, and I got dizzy at times. It’s gotten better, but Eddie’s still not letting me drive again, yet.”

“You haven’t been letting him drive?” Copeland’s question was directed at Eddie, but Buck was the one to answer again.

“He took over the driving since we didn’t know how bad the dizziness would be or when it would hit,” he said. “He’s also been making sure I’ve had water on me at all times, and taking my blood pressure every day.”

Copeland tilted her head a little at that as she absently moved her pen moved across her notes. The action had Eddie scowling again, craning his neck slightly in an attempt to see what she was writing down. He opened his mouth, probably to protest that he hadn’t been as bad as Buck was saying, but Copeland spoke up before he could.

“Has there been anything else?” she asked. “Any other changes, anything else that’s happened since we last spoke?”

Buck was about to tell her about the new probie, and how he’d been playing babysitter with the kid at work. Or maybe about the neighbors that he thought were nice and who then turned out to be homophobic assholes. But Eddie beat him to the punch with,

“We had an argument the other day.” His husband didn’t look at him as he said it. “Me and Buck.”

Aw, hell.

“Eds, we don’t-”

“No, I want to talk it over with her.”

“You two don’t typically fight, per se,” Copeland noted, watching them attentively. “What was this argument about?”

“We went to a call the other day at a living funeral,” Buck told her. Reluctantly, because he could probably guess which parts the good doctor was going to latch onto like a limpet. From the way she was eying him at the mention of a living funeral, he figured he was right. “It was for an old woman named Marie. Her death doula set it up.”

“So how did that cause you two to argue?”

“He was in his head afterwards,” Eddie told her. “And at first I thought he was fixating on Marie telling us that we were all going to die alone, but no. It turned out that he wanted to be friends with the woman’s death doula.”

“And that was the cause of the argument? Him wanting to be friends with this person?”

“The argument was because he wanted to be friends with someone who thought it was cool how he was struck by lightning and died.”

“Because it is!” Buck snapped. “Why are you bringing this up again, Eddie? I told you, if it had happened to anyone else, you’d think it was cool, too.”

“That’s not-” His husband cut himself off with a shake of his head. “You said she saw you and not your corpse.”

Oh.

“Eddie…”

“No. I need to know, Buck. Do you really feel like all I’m seeing when I look at you is your corpse?”

“Evan?” Copeland prompted, when he didn’t respond right away. “Is that how you feel?”

“I- I don’t know.” Buck focused on the pillow in his lap, tugging at the fake fur. “Maybe sometimes.”

“I’m sorry,” Eddie said. “I’m sorry that’s how I-... Yes, sometimes I can’t get the image of you dangling from that ladder out of my head, or how you looked on that hospital bed. And I’m sorry for that.”

“Hey, no, no.” Buck reached over, grabbed his knee. “You don’t have to apologize. I told you already, I understood.”

“Evan mentioned how you’ve been making sure he has water on hand at all times, now,” Copeland said. “And that you were monitoring his blood pressure daily. Is there a possibility that this caretaking behavior is in response to that trauma?”

The answer was a resounding yes, Buck knew, even though Eddie merely shrugged and avoided looking at either of them. The fight club phase had been a malformed attempt at taking back control, after so many events where people Eddie cared about got hurt while he couldn’t do anything. Buck hadn’t been surprised by the way that need for control reemerged after the lightning strike and everything that followed it, and he hadn’t fought it very hard - or much at all, honestly - because he knew it made his husband feel better.

“The last time Evan was here,” Copeland continued, leaning back in her armchair. “We discussed support systems. I’m well aware that yours and his have considerable overlap, Eddie, so I want to make sure that you’re getting what you need. It’s not unusual for people to focus on who they perceive to be the main victim of the trauma, so to speak, while overlooking the way it has a splash zone of sorts.”

It was a point she had hammered on when they talked about the ladder truck bombing and the absolute shitstorm that ended up following it a few months later. The way she’d sat him down - metaphorically, they were still doing remote sessions then - and helped him understand how Bobby’s actions might have been a response to his own trauma from everything. It was a point that Buck himself brought up with Eddie after the shooting, when talking about getting Chris back into therapy.

“I’ve vented to Stanley some, and the others in my veterans group,” Eddie said with another shrug, one that looked purposefully casual. “Cap ambushed me at my performance review to ask about how I was handling everything, and told me Athena would be willing to listen if it was something I didn’t think I could talk to him about.”

“Did you take him up on that offer?”

“She came over the other day,” he said, glancing Buck’s way. “We talked some. I talked, she listened.”

That was probably the day Bobby had the kids. Abuela had been very insistent about keeping him in the kitchen, even though what they were making hadn’t been very complicated. A lot of what she had him slicing didn’t even get used in what they ended up eating for lunch.

“I’m glad to hear that,” Copeland said. She gave a little nod. “Pivoting back to Evan, then. Why do you want to be friends with someone who sees a traumatic event in your life as ‘cool’?”

“It- It’s like I told Eddie, it’s not a trauma with her,” Buck defended himself, clutching the pillow tighter. “She doesn’t know me, she wasn’t there. She just thought it was cool.”

He watched Copeland tap her pen for a moment.

“Is it possible,” she said, “you’re hoping that because it’s not a trauma for her, when you talk to her about it, it won’t be for you, either?”

“I…” Was he hoping for that? It’d simply felt nice to talk to a person, even for a scant few minutes, that didn’t treat him like a china doll someone just glued back together. Or someone he didn’t have to worry about rubbing salt into a wound while he talked about it. “I don’t know.”

“He complained that he thought everyone else got offended, whenever he made a joke about the lightning,” Eddie tattled. “That talking to me about it felt like poking an open wound.”

“You do like to make light of heavy subject matters,” Copeland commented. “Sometimes people simply don’t feel comfortable with jokes about certain events, no matter who’s making them.”

“We’ve made jokes about awful things before,” Buck complained. “When Chimney came back from his accident, the cake was in the shape of his head with licorice sticking out of it.”

“Perhaps, then, you’re simply assuming they’re offended by your remarks when they’re actually not,” she said. “Have you talked to any of them about this?”

“No,” Eddie answered for him. “No, he hasn’t.”

“Then I suppose you know what you should do.”

“Ugh, pass,” Buck said. “Can we talk about our homophobic neighbors, instead?”

“It’s your time.”

“Alright, so, they moved in across the road a couple days after our poker date-”

“He can do math now,” Eddie said.

“I can do math now. But the first time I met them, they seemed nice…”

Buck’s phone started ringing with a video call as he was sitting on the couch with Eddie, waiting for Chimney to pick him up for whatever it was the man needed his help doing. He didn’t think much about it when he saw that it was TK calling, swiping his finger along the screen to answer it.

“Hey, TK, I- Oh, wow.”

“What’s-” Eddie leaned over, eyebrows shooting up when he saw the paramedic on the screen. “Oh. Wow.”

“I knew it was bad,” TK said, reaching up to rub at the bruise starting to form around his eye. Buck figured he was on shift, since he was in uniform and it looked like he was somewhere at the 126. “I asked the others but they just gasped in horror before lying to me.”

“You’re still very pretty,” Buck assured him.

“Aw, thanks, Sunshine. That actually helps.”

“What happened?” Eddie asked, sitting back some.

“We had an EV fire earlier, there was a guy unresponsive in the backseat,” TK explained. “Cap thought he’d just had a kidney transplant and was still coming out of anesthesia. But I was in the back with the guy while she went to find out where the trauma team was, when the guy woke up and just, wham!”

“Are you telling us that you lost a fight to a barely conscious trauma victim?” Buck was trying (and failing) to keep from laughing.

“He sucker punched me! You, of all people, should understand!”

“You know what, that’s fair. Though, Chimney wasn’t anesthetized when he laid me out.”

“You said Vega thought he’d just had a kidney transplant?” Eddie asked, leaning back in. “What was he doing in the backseat of a car?”

“Hell if I know! We didn’t get to ask, the guy’s in the wind,” TK said. “But that’s not the problem. The problem is that we’re having lunch with Dad’s brother tonight. Paul told me to put concealer on it.”

  “Paul is a wise man.”

“But why is it a problem?” Buck asked him. “What, does your uncle get insulted by bruises?”

“He’s my uncle, who I have never met. I just want to make a good first impression, you know?”

“He met Owen and still decided to go all the way to Austin to visit. I don’t think he’s going to be bothered by a black eye.”

“Why did you say it like that?” TK asked him.

“Uh.” Buck turned to Eddie for help, only to get absolutely none. Thankfully, however, salvation came in the form of a text notification popping up. “Oh, hey, Chimney’s here.”

“Have fun,” Eddie instructed, leaning up to kiss him when he got to his feet and started for the door.

“Where are you going?”

“I actually don’t know,” Buck replied, stepping outside. “Chim said he wanted my help with something.”

“I should probably be hanging up anyway, actually do my job,” TK said, sounding oh-so-excited about it. “Bye, Sunshine.”

Buck tucked his phone away after the other man hung up. He was heading down the driveway to Chimney’s waiting car when he spotted Shanna standing outside, clutching to little Clara. She was either glaring at him, or at the row of flags they’d hung on the side of their house. Or maybe at the rainbow bunting that was draped along the front of their fence.

They’d already gotten compliments from a couple of their neighbors about the new decorations. One even asked where they got the bunting, and he’d been happy to tell them.

Buck waved to her, as cheerfully as he could. She turned and stomped inside, slamming the door loud enough that the whole street heard it.

“Was that Shanna?” Hen asked when he got to the car. She was sitting in the front, so he had to fold himself into the back with Jee-Yun’s seat.

“That was Shanna,” he confirmed, buckling in as Chimney started driving. “Where are we going? Has he said yet?”

“You’re helping me pick out a ring,” Chimney said. Buck lunged between the front seats as far as he could go with the seatbelt on.

“A ring!?”

“Sit back,” Hen scolded, pushing at him. Buck ignored her, focused on the man driving.

“Yes, a ring,” Chimney said. “An engagement ring.”

Buck stared at the side of the man’s head for a moment, thinking, before he asked,

“Are you marrying my sister to make it easier to do your taxes?”

“No!”

The jewelry store they went to was at one of Los Angeles’ many malls, this one in downtown. Chimney nervously led the way inside with Hen at his shoulder and Buck excitedly bounding along behind. The sales lady at the counter greeted them cordially at first, and then her eyes lit up the moment Chimney asked to see the engagement rings. She was quick to unlock one of the cases and pull out a tray holding a dazzling variety of diamond rings that, much like everything else in the store, had no price tags.

“It’s kind of an odd shape,” Chimney commented, eyeing one of the rings.

“Do you know what shape you’re interested in?” the sales lady asked.

“So many options. I don’t know which one feels most like Maddie.” He suddenly spun to face Hen and Buck. “That’s bad, right? That’s something I should know.”

“You should relax,” Hen told him. “They’re all nice, if a little traditional. It doesn’t have to be a diamond.”

“Well,” the sales lady said. “The saying isn’t, ‘opals are forever.’”

“That’s because opals break easily,” Buck said, going to Chimney’s other side so he could better look at the rings. The options offered hadn’t been nearly as vast when he was shopping for his and Eddie’s wedding bands. “Maybe you could get her an amethyst one? It’s her birthstone. I think. And it’ll be unique.”

The sales lady was glaring at him when he looked back up.

“I can’t do this,” Chimney suddenly gasped out, backing away from the counter. The sales lady’s glare, if possible, actually got even harsher, like she was blaming Buck for the other man’s sudden change of heart. “I can’t believe I was crazy enough to do this. What am I doing?”

“Chim, what’s going on?” Hen asked, chasing after and catching the man just inside the exit. Buck gave the sales lady an awkward smile before going over as well. “You wanted to come here and now you’re freaking out?”

“Is this a mistake?” Chimney asked.

“No,” Buck blurted out.

“You should have asked yourself that question before we got here,” Hen said, side-eying Buck.

“I’m supposed to be buying a ring for Maddie, but I keep thinking about Tatiana,” Chimney said. “Do you remember how terribly that went? Why would I put myself in this situation again?”

“First of all,” Hen said, “Maddie is not Tatiana.”

“Yeah, and you haven’t been lying to her about who you are for your entire relationship,” Buck added. He squinted at the shorter man. “Have you?”

“No!”

“You asked Tatiana to marry you for the wrong reasons,” Hen said, stomping on Buck’s foot. It actually hurt quite a lot. “You were looking for something special-”

“You don’t find it, you make it,” Buck chimed in, the words etched into his memory. Hen tried stomping on his foot again, but he shuffled out of the way before she could.

“What if this isn’t something she even wants?” Chimney asked.

“Then she says no. It won’t be the end of the world, just don’t get your head impaled again.”

“Buck!” Hen snapped, glaring at him. He threw up his hands and stepped away, because he was clearly not wanted here. She turned back to her friend. “You two haven’t ever talked about it?”

“Not really,” Chimney said. “It’s just never really come up unless someone -” He gave Buck a pointed look. “Brought it up.”

“Okay, crazy question,” she said. “How come you haven’t asked her before now?”

“Yeah!” Buck cut in. “I- I’d like to know that, too.”

“I think I always meant to ask,” Chimney said, and Buck figured it would be rude to voice his doubts about that. “When she was pregnant, I didn’t want her to think that was the only reason-”

“That’s why Eddie and Shannon got married, so that’s understandable.”

“And then,” he said, ignoring Buck’s contribution. “There was everything after Jee was born, so that wasn’t a good time. I guess life just kept getting in the way.”

“And now?” Hen prompted. “You’re in your own way?”

“Maybe,” he said. “But if I don’t ask now, I’m scared I never will.

“Chim, listen,” Hen said. “If you’re not sure, you don’t have to do this. You don’t have to decide anything right now.”

“But if it helps your decision any,” Buck told him, “Ma has a gun and she knows how to use it.”

He wasn’t fast enough to avoid getting his foot stomped on this time, and he ended up hopping away in minor agony.

“We should have left you at home,” Hen said.

“You know what? I love Maddie, we have a family together. That’s what I want,” Chimney said. “I’m doing it. I’m gonna do it.”

He marched past the both of them, back towards the counter.

“Excuse me,” he said, getting the sales lady's attention. He peered at the tray, before gingerly plucking one out and holding it up. “This one. I’ll take this one.”

“The trinity engagement ring,” the sales lady purred, taking it from him. She looked positively gleeful. “An excellent choice, I’ll box it up for you.”

Buck couldn’t help the way he bowled into Chimney for a bear hug.

All You Have Is Your Fire

Chapter Summary

In which we see things from another's perspective.

Chapter Notes

I would apologize for the gap between chapters, but I spent that time in the Keys and so I'm not sorry at all.

Fair warning: this chapter is from the Douchebag's perspective. His views are not my views. Throw rocks at him, not me.

Dalton wanted to be a firefighter from the moment he first visited his uncle’s station as a kid and got to watch them fight a house fire. If it had been up to him, he would have gone to the fire academy the moment he graduated from high school. Except his mom put her foot down, saying he needed to have something to fall back on, and Uncle Randolf supported her like he always did. So off to Los Angeles City College he went for the easiest two-year degree he could find.

Uncle Randolf was his favorite uncle, which wasn’t saying much because the man was his only uncle, and married to his mom’s sister, Aunt Susan. After his dad left and never came back, his aunt and uncle stepped in as best they could while his mom found her feet again, so Uncle Randolf was around a lot as he grew up.

He’d been very excited once he got into the fire academy, and that excitement stayed even as he was disappointed to find out just how much of it was classroom work again. The instructors were cool, except for the one who clearly didn’t know what she was talking about half the time. And he liked his fellow trainees, even the ones who couldn’t take a joke or who were just rude.

That excitement waned a bit as he graduated and got assigned to the 133. They were called to far fewer fires than he was expecting, and most of them were either minor enough to just need an extinguisher or simply false alarms. There was only one actual fire, and he’d been stuck as the man behind for it. Though the lack of firefighting at his firefighter job wasn’t the only problem with the station he was assigned to.

That rudeness, that inability to take jokes, wasn’t just contained to his fellow trainees at the academy. It started off okay, with everyone being welcoming enough, but that changed pretty quickly. It didn’t help that Captain Mehta was a stick in the mud, or that Captain Shore was just a bitch. Dalton was almost thankful when he got transferred to another station.

The 118 was better, maybe? Nash seemed a better captain than Mehta, at least, and definitely better than Shore. He was happy to see peer instructor Panikkar - sorry, Ravi - again, and Buck was a fun guy to work with. Weird, but fun. He also talked a lot. Like a lot, a lot. Get him talking about something and he’d not shut up, even turning it into a lecture sometimes, that kind of a lot. And he also hovered.

Dalton understood that Buck was the guy assigned to him for mentoring or whatever, but he didn’t think it required that much hovering. Maybe Buck had a crush on him? It hadn’t taken long to figure out that the two Diazes were married, and not much longer to learn that Ravi and Albert were a thing, too.

Was it something in the station’s water? Should he start bringing in his own water to drink?

Anyways, Buck was a cool guy and he knew a lot, and he hopefully didn’t have a crush on Dalton because he did not swing that way, thanks. The biggest problems with Buck were that sometimes it sounded like he thought Dalton was an idiot who didn’t know anything about the job - which he wasn’t, he’d been listening to his Uncle Randolf’s stories since he could listen - and sometimes he’d just get mean. Usually when Dalton was trying to be helpful. Nash did the same, scolding him like a child and acting like he was somehow in the wrong.

What was he supposed to do if he saw someone making a mistake? Or if he thought they could do something better? Not say something?

Overall, though, he liked the 118 so far. Yeah, some of them were just as standoffish and rude as those at the 133 - looking at you, Wilson - and it was starting to look like Ravi and his boyfriend were both trying to avoid him, but still. Hopefully Buck’s crush on him would go away and he wouldn’t feel the need to be in his space all the time.

And hopefully they could have an actual fire to respond to, and not all these boring medical calls or traffic accidents.

The others, the core little group of the captain’s favorites, were gathered upstairs around the kitchen island when Dalton arrived for his shift. Most of them at least; Chimney hadn’t shown up yet. Buck, he couldn’t help but notice, was spazzier than usual, perpetually in motion even as he stood in one spot. Didn’t they put him on meds for that?

“How are the neighbors?” Nash asked, nodding at Dalton as he passed by for the coffee machine.

“As unpleasant as ever,” Eddie answered, beside Buck as always, though he was perched on a stool. He didn’t seem bothered by the other man’s constant movement, even when it invaded his personal space. “This morning we came out to them putting up a flag of their own. Three guesses which flag, and the first two don’t count.”

Everyone screwed up their faces at that news.

“What’s wrong with your neighbors?” Dalton broke in to ask. Eddie glanced at him, but it was Buck who answered with,

“Turned out they’re homophobic douchebags.”

“Oh. That sucks.”

The entire group turned their heads to look at him, obvious surprise on most of their faces, especially Wilson’s. It had Dalton bristling. What, did they think he was some kind of bigot or something? But before he could ask them that, Chimney finally came up the stairs to join them. Buck started literally bouncing at the sight of him, and Dalton understood why the others occasionally called him the station’s dog.

Chimney looked at the excitable man and let out a long and drawn out sigh, before taking a ring box out of his pocket and setting it, opened, on the counter top. Everyone crowded in to look. Even Dalton got up to peer at the ring over the others’ heads.

“Is that what you needed Buck’s help for?” Eddie asked, picking the box up to get a better look. “Makes sense why he’s been so excited.”

“I can’t believe you took Buck ring shopping and not me,” Albert whined, leaning around Ravi to see. “You never even said anything to me! I’m your brother, man!”

“That is a serious rock,” Ravi said, snagging the box out of Eddie’s hand for a closer look before passing it on. “Or, rocks. Should you be keeping it here?”

“Are you planning on robbing me?” Chimney asked. “I didn’t want to leave it at home and risk Maddie finding it.”

“It’s beautiful, Chim,” Nash said, taking his own turn at oo-ing and aw-ing over the piece of jewelry.

“Maddie is going to lose it when she sees it,” Eddie commented, and Wilson nodded from the other side of Buck.

“So’s Jee-Yun,” she said. “When she realizes she’s gonna have to get a job slinging lattes to make up for the dent you made in her college fund.”

“Ha. Ha. I didn’t overspend too much,” Chimney defended himself, snagging the ring back. “Not really.”

“If there was ever an occasion to splurge,” Nash said, “it’s buying an engagement ring.”

“Come here, brother-in-law.” Buck bounded over and picked the smaller man up in a bear hug. Dalton thought that Chimney didn’t look particularly excited at the manhandling. “Hey, maybe Pops can officiate the wedding!”

“Oh, that’d be great,” Chimney said, prying himself free from Buck. “But we haven’t really even thought about it…”

“I can check with Athena,” Nash said. Wait, was he Pops? Dalton looked between the two men and could almost see it. “We could host in the backyard again, if you wanted something smaller. Or maybe just the rehearsal dinner or whatever.”

“Are you gonna do any traditional Korean wedding customs?” Ravi cut in. “When my cousin got married, we basically had two very different, very elaborate ceremonies.”

What was a traditional Korean wedding? Dalton tried imagining it and ended up picturing a woman in one of those rice paddy hats, a veil hanging from the brim with her face caked in white make-up. For that matter, what was a traditional Indian one? Did they wear those big feather hats and dance around a fire?

“Elaborate? I- I don’t know…” Chimney stuttered.

“Don’t worry,” Albert said, moving to sling his arm around his brother’s shoulders. “We won’t make you pick your best man just yet.”

“Is there even really a question?” Wilson asked. Albert scowled at her.

“He’s my brother!”

“You two can fight it out over that,” Buck said. “I’m going to be the maid of honor.” He frowned and then corrected himself. “Man of honor.”

“Okay, I think you guys are all jumping the gun a bit,” Chimney said, practically sprinting from the group to give himself space. “And it’s stressing me out. I still have to pop the actual question.”

Dalton watched as Buck and Eddie smirked at each other before turning to face the paramedic.

“So what’s the plan?” Eddie asked, leaning back against the island.

“C’mon, it’s gonna be some kind of grand romantic gesture,” Buck said. “Right? Like that guy who proposed while skydiving. Remember him?”

“I remember he broke both femurs and cracked three ribs,” Wilson said.

“I’ll keep it simple,” Chimney said. “After dinner, we put Jee to bed, and boom! I get down on one knee and ask her.”

There was a pause as they all shared a look.

“That’s kind of basic,” Ravi told him, quickly following it with, “No offense.”

“What he means,” Eddie said, standing and stepping forward while Buck pulled Ravi back. “Is that proposals are kind of a thing these days. But hey, you do what you want to do, okay? I’m just saying, it might be an opportunity.”

“A huge opportunity,” Buck agreed. “Maddie deserves something she’ll remember forever. You both do. No, no, you- You cannot propose without a plan.”

“Copy that.” Chimney sounded and looked overwhelmed. “Got to have a plan.”

“Are you sure that’s a good idea?” Dalton asked, watching Chimney stuff the ring box into his medical bag.

They were on their way to yet another boring, non-fire related call. Albert and Ravi were in the ambulance, having sprinted for it the moment the bell rang. Dalton wasn’t sure if that was normal, especially with the way that Wilson and Chimney scowled at them for it. But it was probably for the best, because the engine’s cab got a bit cramped with five people in it. Six would have been pushing it.

At least Buck was in the pull-down seat on the other side, so Dalton could spread his legs out in the space in front of him. Even if Wilson glared at him for it.

“I didn’t have time to put it in my locker,” Chimney explained, zipping the bag closed. “Maddie was right there. I’m worried she’ll start suspecting something.”

Chimney’s mother - step-mother? Adoptive mother? His something, Dalton knew that much - had shown up with his daughter, leaving her at the station until Maddie could pick her up. Dalton wouldn’t have known the woman was Buck’s sister if he hadn’t picked it up from conversation. She didn’t really look like Buck, nor did she look like Nash. There was also a very obvious age gap, so Dalton figured she was a half-sister or something.

“She’s fine,” Nash said from the captain’s seat. “I ran interference for you.”

“Yeah, that wasn’t awkward at all,” Chimney said, before mouthing to the others what Dalton thought was, ‘yes it was.’

“So,” Eddie said. “What about a nice romantic hot air balloon?”

It was another suggestion for a proposal. They’d been offering them all shift so far.

“No.” Buck, sitting across from him with their legs tangled together, shot it down quickly. “Never ends well.”

“I could charter a boat at the marina,” Chimney said. “Take her on a sunset cruise.”

“You guys do realize you’re just pitching potential calls?” Nash asked, twisting around to look at them. “Right?”

“What about a flash mob?” was Buck’s suggestion. Eddie clearly agreed with it, pointing and nodding. “That’s fun, she’ll never see it coming.”

“That’s too public,” Chimney countered. “Your sister would hate that.”

“I’m with her,” Wilson said. “Why would you want to invite a bunch of strangers into your moment?”

 “What about you and Karen?” Eddie asked. “Who asked who?”

“We both asked.” Oh, Karen was Wilson’s wife. “We went on a weekend getaway, spur of the moment. I had a ring, and it turned out she did, too. We were both planning to ask the other that weekend.”

“Couldn’t ask for a clearer sign that you two were meant to be together,” Nash said.

“I had a pretty clear sign, too,” Eddie said, sighing loud enough to be heard over their headsets. “Both times. When Shannon told me she was pregnant, I just said we should get married. Barely even asked. And then this one-” He kicked Buck’s leg. “Came in with his stupid, ‘It would have been better if I was the one shot.’ I was so angry, I blurted it out.”

“You said I got to have a plan,” Chimney protested. “That proposals are a whole thing.”

“So’s riling you up.”

Jones, in the driver’s seat, let out a loud laugh. It startled Dalton, because he’d almost forgotten that the man even existed.

“I don’t know,” Buck broke in. “I thought it was very romantic. His eyes all glazed over with morphine, a little bit of dry drool on the corner of his mouth.”

“I’d say it was the most shocked I’d ever seen him,” Eddie said, keeping his eyes on his husband. “But…”

Buck blinked at him a couple times before breaking out in a wide grin.

“Chim,” Nash said, “just remember what I told you. No matter what you decide to do, she’ll love it and she’ll say yes.”

“Exactly,” Wilson told him. “It doesn’t have to be a big over-the-top production. Don’t overthink it, and don’t listen to Eddie.”

Chimney, in Dalton’s eyes, did not look very settled by the advice.

You May Be Thinking That I Was Had

Chapter Summary

In which Hen voices her doubts.

Buck should have known, after he and Eddie walked out of their front door to find Edward hanging a Trump 2024 flag up across the street, that the day was not going to be as good as he assumed it might be. He ignored that omen, though, too excited over the thought of Chimney sharing the good news with everyone else at the station. Buck hadn’t told Eddie about the ring, even when he and Abuela and Chris all asked what had him so bouncy, because he didn’t want anything to take away from his future brother-in-law’s moment.

Turned out, he was underestimating Chimney’s ability to do it on his own.

He wasn’t sure when it happened, or how, or why, but at some point between them offering him ideas for how to propose to Maddie and the man coming back from a medical call with Hen and Bobby, Chimney suddenly changed his mind. He wasn’t going to propose, he was going to return the ring and get his money back. And sure, Buck had noticed he looked maybe a mite overwhelmed by all the advice and suggestions, maybe a bit nervous, but he hadn’t thought it was to the point that he was backing out.

It was Hen that pointed it out, after they got back to the station. Or rather, it was Hen that pointed Buck in the older man’s direction with a simple, “Chim’s spiraling.” Which was very much an understatement. Chimney was not spiraling, Chimney was straight-up crashing.

Buck found him sitting on the bench in the locker room, head in his hands. The paramedic looked up when he sat down next to him, rolling his eyes.

“Hen sent you?”

“She might have,” Buck said. “So… You’ve changed your mind?”

“I have.”

“Why?”

“Because it was a stupid idea,” the other man said. “A stupid, awful idea. So I’m just not going to do it. I’m taking the ring back tomorrow.”

“Come on, Chim. It’s- It’s not a stupid idea.”

“You’re only saying that because it’s what you want.”

“Hey! That’s not-”

“Everyone’s been telling me that she’ll say yes, but she has every reason to say no,” Chimney said, getting to his feet.  “And I just can’t risk hearing it. So, I’m not even gonna ask.”

“Chim-” Buck tried, but the other man wasn’t hearing it.

“It’s not happening!” Chimney snapped at him, storming out of the locker room.

Well, fuck. This was going to require teamwork.

He found Hen when he climbed the stairs to the station’s loft. She was at one of the tables, playing solitaire.

“Was I wrong?” she asked when she spotted him. “He’s spiraling, right?”

“Fighter jets spiral when they lose control at Mach One,” he said, pulling out the seat across from her and sitting down. “Chimney’s headed straight for a wall at Mach Ten. Y- You’ve gotta help me talk him down.”

“I don’t know,” Hen said, shaking her head as she focused back on the card game. “Maybe it’s better if we stay out of it.”

What the hell?

“Since when?” he demanded. “Come on, no, we just… We just have to make him realize he’s putting too much pressure on himself. You know how he is.”

“Which is why,” she said, “I’m suggesting we try the hands-off approach. Let him come to his decision on his own.”

“Uh, there’s- there’s only one right decision,” he pointed out to her. “He proposes, she says yes, it becomes one of the best decisions they’ve made.”

“If he gets there. We’ll just have to wait and see how it plays out.”

“How it plays out?” he repeated, confused. This was not like Hen. At all. Wait. Wait, wait, wait… “You don’t want to get involved ‘cause you don’t want him to marry Maddie.”

It didn’t make sense, but it was also the only thing that made sense. Hen looked up at him, barely managing to meet his eyes before her own flickered back down to the cards splayed out on the table.

“How can you- After everything? Do you remember where they were a year and a half ago?”

“I do,” she said. “Maddie was gone, and Chimney was raising Jee-Yun alone.”

“She was sick,” Buck gritted out, doing his best to keep from biting his friend’s head off.

“I know,” Hen said. “And I know that it was terrifying and overwhelming for her, but the choice she made gutted Chimney.”

“First of all,” he snapped, losing the fight with his temper. Hen’s eyes went wide as she leaned back some. “I didn’t let our mother talk about her like this, I’m not going to let you. Secondly, the choices they both made gutted Chimney. He enabled her, and you enabled him.”

Buck had not forgotten how Hen had known, before any of the rest of them, before Maddie had even taken flight, that his sister was sick. Had known, and stood by Maddie and Chimney’s decision to keep it a secret from everyone else. Up until that decision had already bitten everyone in the ass and them knowing no longer mattered, anyway.

“The both of them decided to isolate themselves. The both of them decided not to lean on anyone else for support. Chimney was the one who decided to take off across the country with an infant and no destination, instead of staying where there was a village willing to help him. And you encouraged him,” Buck reminded her. “But he loves her, and she deserves her happily ever after.”

“Why is this always about what Maddie deserves?” Hen demanded. “What about what Chimney deserves?”

“They both deserve to be happy.”

Hen let out a sigh and shook her head, going back to her cards.

“You’re right,” she said, utterly unconvincing. “You’re absolutely right.”

“No, don’t do that. You’re still blaming Maddie.”

“I’m not blaming Maddie,” she denied. “All I’m saying is that their relationship has been one trauma after another from the beginning. Maybe they both need to heal from that before jumping into marriage.”

“Jumping into marriage?” he repeated. “Hen, they have a two year old daughter and bought a house together. This isn’t “jumping” into anything. And they have both been in therapy for the past year and a half. Together and separately. What do you think that’s been, if not healing?”

“Then why is Chimney freaking out the way he is?” Hen asked him. “I know that we love to give him a hard time because it’s fun to watch him spiral, but in this case? I think it’s coming from a very real place of fear. And he may not be able to voice it, but I am. Because I don’t want to see him get hurt again.”

“Chimney is afraid because for some stupid reason, he’s convinced there’s a chance Maddie’s gonna say no,” Buck said, shoving himself away from the table and standing. And it was stupid, to think that Maddie might say no. Not after all the effort she put into fixing the rot in their foundation since she came back from Boston. “But this? This is you holding a grudge against my sister, against Chimney’s girlfriend.”

“Buck,” Hen tried, but he shook his head and stomped away.

There were too many other things he wanted to say to his friend, few of them polite and none of them kind, for him to stick around any longer.

There were certain places in Los Angeles that were the absolute worst to be called out to. And near the top of that list would be the city landfill. They were called out because a pair of idiots went scaling a literal mountain of trash in search of… Eddie wasn’t sure. Whatever it was, they never found it before the whole thing nearly buried them. They found one of the pair with his arm nearly sliced to ribbons by some wire, and he and Ravi had to rescue the other from a fire that was eating its way through everything.

At the very least, Buck was happier on his way back to the station than he had been leaving it. The victim that nearly lost his arm utterly refused to take “no” as an answer, vowing to come back and keep searching. And Chimney took that as a sign that the proposal should still go on.

All Eddie got out of it was a stench that would never go away.

“I can still smell it,” he complained as he jumped out of the engine’s cab. A cab that would need to be disinfected and sprayed down, along with their turn-outs, because he just knew the stench was seeping into everything. “Like it’s burnt in my nose.”

“It’s called phantosmia,” Ravi said. “The brain recreates smells even when they’re not present.”

Eddie couldn’t help pausing on his way to the lockers to stare at him, nor apparently could the others.

“What?” the younger man demanded. “Buck isn’t the only one that can know random trivia!”

“Well, I have another name for it,” Bobby commented, prompting them to move on.

“Because a garbage dump is exactly what I want to smell like when I pop the question,” Chimney complained, heading straight for the bench to dump the medical bag he’d carried with him. He glanced up to find them all staring at him, now. “That’s right. I’m not gonna wait a minute longer. I don’t need all the hoopla, just Maddie and Jee. That’s all.”

“That’s great!” Ravi exclaimed, reaching over to smack him on the shoulder. Hen was on the other side of him, and Eddie couldn’t help but think that she did not look overly joyous at the announcement.

(“She doesn’t want them to get married,” Buck whispered harshly. Eddie had caught him angrily marching away from Hen and immediately dragged him to a private corner to figure out what was wrong. “She’s blaming Maddie for everything that happened.”

“I’m sure that’s not what she meant, mi sol,” he tried. His husband shook his head.

“It sure sounded like it.”)

“Hey, when she sees that ring,” Eddie said, ignoring Hen and the smug look Buck shot her, “she’ll forget about how you smell.”

“Here’s hoping,” Chimney said, glaring at his younger brother when Albert hit him with his body spray. He reached into the bag and pulled out the ring box, opening it up.

The man froze, face dropping.

“What’s wrong?” Eddie asked, something unpleasant curdling in his stomach, worse than the stench he was trying to cover up with Buck’s expensive deodorant.

“The ring,” Chimney said, looking up. “It’s gone.”

Ay, maldito.

After a moment of horror-filled silence that draped across the group, they snapped into motion. Bobby and Buck were quick to gather the other half of their shift, setting everyone loose onto the station to search for the missing ring. Soon, everything that could be pulled out and searched through, was. The station bay became a mess of gear spread out and strewn along the floor.

(Hopefully Bobby thought of taking them offline. There was no way they’d be capable of responding to a call in a timely manner with this mess.)

“Chim,” Bobby said, striding quickly back into the locker room, where Eddie and Chimney had been searching. Ravi and Albert weren’t far behind, nor was Wolfe. “It’s not in my office.”

“I couldn’t find it in the laundry,” Wolfe said.

“And it’s not upstairs,” Ravi said. “Al and I looked everywhere.”

“This is crazy,” Chimney said, sounding like he was about to lose the last vestiges of whatever patience and calm he was clinging to. “The last place I had it was in your office, and I’ve had it the whole time after that!”

“I did ask if that was a good idea,” Wolfe pointed out, which was very much not helpful.

“Not the time!” Eddie turned around to snap. The youngest of them put up his hands, like he was trying to shield himself.

“That’s right,” Chimney said, and Eddie did not like the way his voice sounded. Like he was having an epiphany of some sort. The paramedic pointed a wagging finger at the probie. “You did ask that. And wouldn’t it be funny to teach me a lesson?”

“What?” Wolfe looked taken aback.

“Or was it my brother and his boyfriend?” Chimney said, moving onto them. “I bet the two of you took it!”

“Why would we take your ring?” Albert asked, expression a blur of confusion and insult. 

“Yeah, yeah, okay.” Eddie put himself between them and Chimney. “I think you’ve officially lost it.”

“I haven’t lost anything! You people stole it.” Chimney glared at him. 

“Take a breath,” Bobby ordered, stepping in to play mediator. “We would never do that to you.”

“I know you wouldn’t, Cap,” the shorter man said. “But these guys? They probably planned this whole little prank and left you out of the loop. We do it all the time.”

There was an awkward moment where it dawned on Chimney just what, precisely, he had just admitted. Bobby turned to each of them, his expression one of paternal disappointment that he typically only used on Buck.

“Good to know,” the captain said.

Eddie sighed and shook his head, turning slightly to look out over the bay. He spotted Buck almost immediately, his husband standing in front of the open ambulance doors with Hen. The two of them looked to be having another argument.

He realized he wasn’t the only one who saw them when Chimney dashed out of the locker room towards the pair, leaving the rest of them to chase after him.

“What’s this?” Chimney demanded, voice carrying across the bay. “Looks to me like two criminals conspiring.”

“He thinks we took his ring and hid it,” Eddie explained when the group got closer.

“Wasn’t me,” Buck denied, turning to scowl down at Hen. “I didn’t have anything to do with it.”

“I believe you,” Chimney said after a moment, stepping forward to pat him on the chest. “You want it to happen too much. Which leaves only Hen.”

She scoffed.

“I didn’t take that ring,” she said. “None of us did.”

“Oh? Let’s look at the evidence,” Chimney said. “I left the firehouse in the engine with the ring, and I put it in that bag-” He pointed back at the locker room, where the bag was still sitting on the bench, visible through the glass panes. “Which you were all witness to.”

“Uh, not me and Rav,” Al interrupted to point out. “We weren’t there.”

“The same bag,” Chimney continued, speaking over his brother as he paced, “that you all had access to. But no one had more access than my partner. Because it is always the ones closest to you.”

He stopped pacing to stand in front of an unimpressed Hen, hands clasped behind his back. 

“It was you, Fredo,” he declared.

“You sound like an insane person,” Hen said, and Eddie couldn’t disagree. The man’s mental stability really did seem to be cracking around the edges.

“Do I? Because it all adds up,” Chimney shot back. “You were trying to talk me out of it at the jewelry store.”

“No I wasn’t!” she said. “I was asking you the question that any actual friend-” Was Eddie imagining the side-eye she gave Buck at that? “-would ask. “Are you sure?” And you have been flip-flopping ever since. You’re all over the place.”

“There is a lot at stake here,” Chimney said. “I have every right to be nervous.”

“Maybe your nerves are trying to tell you something?”

“Like what?”

“That maybe you shouldn’t be getting married!”

There was a pregnant pause that followed Hen’s outburst, one that filled the entire bay because everyone had stopped to listen to them. It was broken by Wolfe’s half-whispered,

“Definitely better than my last station.”

“Guys,” Bobby said, reaching out to snag the probie’s shirt sleeve, tugging him back. “Let’s give these two a minute.”

Buck glared at Hen the entire time Eddie dragged him away,

“I don’t know what’s worse,” Chimney said. “The fact that you feel this way, or that you felt it and didn’t tell me.”

He and Hen were perched on the back of the ambulance, the bay silent around them and devoid of anyone else. Bobby had managed to herd all of the others away to give them privacy, though Chimney wasn’t sure where to. All of his coworkers could be huddled outside in the parking lot, for all he knew.

“I didn’t say anything because I didn’t want to hurt you,” she said.

“I’d say that was a fail.”

“Chim, listen, I- I’m not telling you not to propose-”

“Sure sounds like it.”

“I’ll support whatever decision you make,” Hen told him.”But it’s the in decision that worries me. How can you expect me to be fully on board when you’re not even sure?”

“You’re supposed to be on my side,” he said, and it sounded petulant even to himself.

“I am,” she said. “When Maddie left, I watched how you struggled with so much guilt. You blamed yourself when she was the one that left. She didn’t trust you enough to fully share what she was going through. Can you trust that she won’t do that again?”

Chimney let out a heavy sigh.

“I blamed myself because I had half the blame,” he said. “At least.”

“Chimney-”

“No. It’s true. She was sick, and I knew she was sick. I knew that she was struggling to find the right medication, the right treatment,” he said. “There are so many things I wish I could go back and do differently, do better. She said I enabled her and she wasn’t wrong, because I did. She wanted to hide how bad things were from everyone else, and I let her, I helped her. And when the blackout happened, I… There is not a single doubt in my mind that if I had gone to Bobby, if I had told him what was going on, he would have done everything he could to let me be home with her, instead of here. Or helped me figure something else out so that she wasn’t left to struggle alone.

“I can’t predict the future. But the two of us have spent so much time together, trying to fix what broke on both sides. We’re in a better place now. Maddie is in a better place, I’m in a better place.”

“I’m glad,” Hen said. “Truly, I- I am.”

“Good. Because you, of all people, should really understand,” he said. She looked at him, confused, so he spelled it out. “When you cheated on Karen, how long did it take for her to trust you again?”

“A long time I suppose,” she said, letting out a sigh and looking away.

“But she forgave you.”

“She did.”

“And you moved forward.”

“We did.”

“That’s all I want, that’s marriage,” Chimney told her. “We fuck up and we apologize and we do better. That doesn’t mean we shouldn’t be together.”

“You’re right,” she said, and he hated how choked up she sounded, and he hated the way her eyes were shiny with tears that weren’t quite falling yet. “I’m sorry, I’m a terrible friend.”

“No, no, no, you are my best friend.” He grabbed her wrist, gave it a squeeze. “My very opinionated, sometimes wrong, best friend. Who was just trying to protect me, and I love you for that.”

“I overstepped.”

“And I forgive you. See how that works?”

“Come on,” Hen said, standing up. She sniffled and wiped at her eyes. “Let’s go find that ring.”

“We’ve looked everywhere,” Chimney reminded her, getting up as well.

“No, not everywhere.”

Retracing their steps, going back to the scenes they were called to that shift, sounded like a good idea on paper. In practice? Well…

Athena was not very happy, when she found the pair of them digging through the landfill in the middle of the night. And it was very, very awkward to sit in the back of her cruiser as she scolded them.

Even worse, they still didn’t manage to find the ring. At this point, Chimney was just going to accept it as a sign from the universe.

Close to the Flame

Chapter Summary

In which Taylor Kelly is gonna be Taylor Kelly...

Taylor Kelly was Buck’s friend. That was an important distinction for Eddie, even and especially as his husband’s other friends became his friends, too, and vice versa. TK and Carlos? Their friends. Myra, Buck’s former dancing teacher? Their friend, even with all the awkwardness that surrounded him meeting her and her husband, Gabe, the first time. Nik and Gerty from Eddie’s veterans’ group, whom he occasionally hung out with afterwards? It had taken a while for the streams to cross, but they’d hosted each other for dinners and now they texted Buck to check in on them both whenever Eddie missed a week.

But Taylor Kelly wasn’t their friend, she was Buck’s, and Eddie would not hesitate to make that clear to anyone and everyone that thought otherwise. And he had absolutely no doubt that Taylor would do the same, because the dislike was most assuredly mutual. Hell, the woman showed up to the wedding dressed for a funeral. They’d almost come close to something resembling friendship after Chimney punched Buck and they were united in their mutual anger, but the common ground crumbled beneath their feet with Jonah Greenway and that mess.

Eddie tolerated her presence for Buck’s sake, grumbling and complaining and insulting her the entire time. He made a point of changing the channel whenever she was reporting something and he had the remote. Her number had been blocked on his phone ever since her actions negatively impacted his husband’s career. And if he thought he could get away with it, he would have done the same on Buck’s phone.

Eddie was not friends with Taylor Kelly. Eddie did not like Taylor Kelly. If he had to choose between saving a dog or the reporter, he would first ask which was which before rescuing the mutt. If they were running from a hungry mountain lion together, he would trip her without remorse or hesitation. If she came to their door suffering from a curse that would kill her unless Eddie said something nice about her, he’d set her up with Natalia the death doula and wish her a speedy trip Down South.

And yet despite all that, or perhaps because of it, Eddie ended up buying her book.

He hadn’t gone out with the intent of buying her book. Honestly, he hadn’t even known she’d written a book until he saw it sitting in a rack by the check-out lanes. After spending an afternoon fixing a leaking pipe for Pepa, he stopped at the store on his way home to pick up a couple things. Standing in line, waiting to pay with a basket in one hand and a small bouquet in the other - because it had been too long since he’d gotten Buck flowers - he spotted Taylor Kelly’s face.

Close to the Flame: My Adventures With the LAFD, was the title. The cover was a photoshopped still from one of her reports. A still image of Taylor Kelly holding a microphone stared out from it, and Eddie couldn’t bring himself to look away. He reached out with barely a thought, adjusting the basket and the bouquet and snagging the book as the line in front of him moved and he could finally check out.

There wasn’t really any reason for why he bought the book, except perhaps for morbid curiosity. That was a flaw that typically belonged to his husband, but Eddie could never claim to be completely innocent of it. As far as he was aware, their station was the only one that Taylor plagued, which meant the book had to be about them. At the very least, Buck was most definitely in the pages somewhere. Probably more than once.

The book’s presence was a constant, nagging thing in his mind the entire drive home. It wasn’t a large book, it couldn’t be a large book. Despite her bragging, she didn’t actually have very many “adventures” with them. There were very few options when it came to what she could have shared with the world, which meant…

Well, Eddie couldn’t imagine it meant anything good, considering what those options entailed.

Buck was sitting on the floor of the living room, playing with Gavin, when Eddie got home. His face bloomed into a smile as bright as the sun as he was handed the flowers, and he got up to fill up a vase with water as Eddie busied himself putting away the gains of the grocery store side trip. That smile dropped when he sat the vase down in the middle of the dining table and his husband placed the book next to it.

“Taylor wrote a book?” Buck asked with a frown as he picked it up.

“She wrote a book,” Eddie confirmed, sitting in one of the chairs and patting Hoover on the head. The dog had come trotting in from the backyard to greet him.

“Is this… Is this about us?” The frown intensified as he flipped the book over to scan the back, spreading to envelope his entire face. “Eds, I- She never- I didn’t know about this. I swear, I-”

“I know, mi sol,” Eddie interrupted. “I haven’t looked through it. I just saw it and grabbed it.”

“I can’t believe- No.” Buck cut himself off, shaking his head, as he flipped through the book. “No, I can believe it. I can absolutely believe that she would do this without telling us.”

Of course he could believe it; she’d done just about the same, not quite a full year before.

“What do you want to do?” he asked, crossing his arms and leaning back in his chair. Personally, he wanted to read it and see what the reporter had to say about him and his, fuel the the righteous fury he already felt surging to the fore. But if his husband wanted to burn the book without another glance and pretend it didn’t exist, then that was what they would do.

Not that he believed for a moment that would be what the other man chose.

“I want to read it,” Buck blurted, glancing hesitantly at him. Like Eddie would be mad about that or something, like Eddie hadn’t been the one to impulse buy the damned thing. “She wrote a book about us, babe. I- I want to see what she said.”

Gavin made a shrill cry in the living room and there was the sound of something toppling to the floor, followed by giggling.

“Later,” Eddie said, getting up.

“Later,” Buck agreed. “Uh, do you think the others know about this?”

He raised the book up, as if Eddie somehow didn’t know he was talking about it.

“If they did, they would have said something about it,” he pointed out.

“We should… We should tell them, right?” His husband sounded unsure. “We should absolutely tell them about this.”

“Maybe we should wait until after we’ve read it,” Eddie suggested. “So that we know how, uh…”

“How bitchy it gets?”

“You said it, not me.”

“The people that know me, for a given factor of knowing me, refer to me as Taylor Kelly, the Bitch,” Buck read aloud. “And I have earned that epithet.”

“Damn right, she has,” Eddie said, leaning into his side.

It was later, hours later, and they were curled up in their bed with the book. Gavin had been put to bed, refusing to sleep until they read him two whole stories and sang him three lullabies. Chris, honestly, didn’t go to bed much easier; he was undoubtedly still on his computer, and they’d check in a bit to make sure he wasn’t up too late. But now they had the privacy to read Taylor’s book, which had been taunting them from where Buck had tossed it on their bed before seeing what new mess Gavin made for them.

Buck was more than a bit nervous about starting the book, because Taylor didn’t have a filter when it came to telling a story. Everything needed to be out there, the whole truth as she knew it, and it didn’t matter how much damage it caused. In some ways, after the mess with his parents and the revelation about Daniel, he appreciated that philosophy. In other ways, he hated it, because he knew that she had a fondness for sensationalism that she wouldn’t admit to herself, and that her definition of a truth that needed to be told did not leave much, if any, room for privacy.

He’d broken out the wine, ignoring Eddie’s protests as he did so, because he was definitely going to need the fortitude.

The start of the book was an introduction to Taylor herself. It wasn’t anything new to Buck, stuff he already knew, but he noticed that it was the shallow version. There was nothing about her parents, no mention of how her father was in prison for - supposedly, the man still denied it - murdering her mother, no mention of her actual last name of Martin. Just the pretty, puffed up tale of a woman who wanted to be a reporter since she was a kid, who put herself through college and pulled herself up to where she was now.

That Taylor Kelly had a problem with hypocrisy was not breaking news for Buck.

“I struggled with the question of whether or not I should change the names of the people featured in this book,” Buck read, fingers clutching tight enough that the pages crinkled and crumpled. “I found myself asking, would there even be a point? Some of the experiences I recall here were highly publicized at the time.” What? There was nothing he could think of, absolutely nothing, that was “highly publicized” and which also involved her. What could she possibly be referring to? 

Judging from the deepening furrow of Eddie’s brow, Buck figured his husband was wondering the same thing.

“But besides that, my first actual news story was about these firefighters,” he continued, pausing to take a swig of his wine. “I did interviews with them. That segment is on the internet, publicly available for viewing. But for the sake of- Oh, thank God, she changed the names to protect the innocent.”

“If you’re still innocent,” Eddie said, “then I clearly haven’t been fulfilling my husbandly duties. Also, when the hell did you watch Dragnet?”

“The hell is Dragnet?”

Buck wondered, a little, where she got the names from. Did she simply look up a random generator and use that? Bobby became Captain Krause, Hen and Chimney became Paramedics Hinds and Choi, while Eddie and himself became Firefighters Guzman and Stark. Not that she included a conversion chart with the book, seeing as that would defeat the purpose, but it was simple enough to figure out.

Taylor also changed their station number to 911, which was not very subtle at all.

She started the actual narrative much as it started in real life: the helicopter rescue. Buck remembered it, remembered how the pilot radioed about mechanical trouble and tried to set it down in a baseball field, only to miss and hit the stands, instead. No one had been in any actual, mortal danger, not really. But from the way Taylor told it, the helicopter death spiraled before crashing into the stands, with the pilot - but not the reporter - panicking the entire time.

“I don’t like the way she’s spending so many words talking about being in your arms,” Eddie complained, when they got to the part of the actual rescue.

“Yeah, the description here of me lifting her out is, uh… I think I’ve read it in one of Pepa’s books.”

“I can’t believe you actually read those things,” his husband said, reaching over him to snag his half-full wine glass off the bedside table.

“Hey!”

“Ay, chitón. I’m lowering your risk of seizures.”

Buck rolled his eyes, grabbed the glass out of his hand to take a swig of his own, and started reading again.

“I didn’t get to properly meet the firefighters I would grow to know, some better than others-” He paused to roll his eyes at Eddie’s annoyed scoff. “-until later, when I went to do a feature on the people who rescued me. Most of them were welcoming, especially Stark, who… Who was the closest thing to a station dog they had.”

There was something bordering on insulting in that sentence, despite the fact that it wasn’t the first time he’d heard it. Instead of focusing on it, he took another sip of his wine and continued.

“That was not how everyone reacted to my presence, however. I’m still convinced Captain Krause was a moment away from throwing myself and my cameraman out of the station, if the fire chief hadn’t called when he did. And Guzman, perhaps it isn’t fair to say he was unwelcoming, but from the moment he muscled his way into the conversation when he spotted me and Stark talking, I got the feeling that the man did not like me.”

“The way she phrased that makes it sound like I was jealous,” Eddie said.

“Were you?”

“I don’t think so,” his husband answered. “I just knew that I didn’t like her talking to you.”

The book continued on to describe the days Taylor spent at the station, following them around with her cameraman and interviewing them. She described the eating contest, with the monitor lizard and the live crickets; she described the bodybuilder competition with the man  suffering from extreme hyponatremia. And she also described…

“Something they did when I first met them, but which they have since stopped doing, was accept tokens of appreciation from the people they served. Especially baked goods.” Buck did not have a good feeling about what was about to follow. “The reason for why they stopped accepting these gifts was because of a delivery of seemingly innocent brownies. Unbeknownst to anyone at the time, the anonymous baker had laced them with LSD. The first sign I noticed that something was off came after half the crew responded to a brawl at a children’s beauty pageant, with Krause staying behind. When I- When I tried interviewing the captain…”

“She isn’t,” Eddie whispered, sounding horrified. “She’s awful, but- She wouldn’t.”

“When I tried interviewing the captain,” he continued, unable to stop reading, unable to tear his eyes away, “it was obvious that he was under the influence of the drugs. At first, it was simple ramblings about every crumb being a universe, before he decided to do a basic physics demonstration. At that point, he had begun hallucinating that I-”

The book thudded against the wall. Buck hadn’t consciously made the choice to throw it, had simply wanted the awful thing as far away from him as possible, as quickly as possible.

“That bitch,” he whispered, horror struck and angry.

“What is it?” Eddie asked, sitting up now. “Buck? What did she write?”

At that point, he had begun hallucinating that I was his late daughter, River…

“She told me,” Buck said. “She told me that she wanted to use all of the footage, that she got overruled by her bosses because the department’s lawyers were scarier. She told me, but she apologized to Pops after the treasure hunt, and I- I just thought…”

“What Taylor does isn’t your fault.”

He shook his head.

“I keep expecting better from her, even when I know the kind of person she is.”

“What do you want to do?” Eddie asked. “We could throw it on the grill and light it on fire, if you want.”

That was tempting. That was very, very tempting. But…

“I want to know what else she put in there,” Buck said, climbing off the mattress to pick the book up off the floor. “We need to know what else she wrote.”

“Maybe that’s the worst of it,” Eddie tried, even though his doubt was clear, and Buck loved him for it.

“I hope so…”

It wasn’t. It really, really wasn’t.

I was waiting outside the hospital doors when Stark came out. He clearly made an effort to clean himself up and make himself presentable for the greater public. But there were still bloodstains on his pants, something he didn’t seem to notice until I pointed it out to him. He wasn’t entirely present, stammering his way through a disjointed explanation of events and more focused on getting home to Guzman’s son.

Watching Guzman get shot, I think, broke him more than being crushed by the ladder truck.

Buck was the one who drove them to Bobby and Athena’s the next morning, now that enough time had passed for his body to adjust to the bupropion and the dizziness wasn’t as much a concern. Normally, he would have celebrated, except his morning was spent on the phone with Taylor. It hadn’t been a pleasant conversation, with the only thing keeping him from yelling at the reporter being the knowledge that it might upset Gavin.

Not that there hadn’t been any yelling. There simply hadn’t been any from his end.

Despite having a key, they always knocked and waited for Bobby or Athena to answer the door. May had learned the lesson for them, and both knew the opportunities that an empty house presented. But Buck was much too agitated to wait this time and so he let himself in and stomped down the stairs, Eddie hurrying behind him with Gavin in his arms.

“Buck, Eddie,” Bobby greeted them in surprise. He and Athena were both seated at their dining table, the remnants of breakfast sitting in front of them. “And you brought a grandkid with you. Hand him here.”

“What brings the two of you over this morning?” Athena asked as Eddie handed off Gavin.

Buck’s answer was to simply toss Taylor’s book onto the table. Her face stared up at everyone.

“Oh,” Bobby said, adjusting the toddler in his arms. “How bad is it?”

“Bad,” Eddie stated. “Buck threw it at a wall, at one point.”

“She wrote about the brownies,” Buck said, hyper aware of his son’s presence as he kept his temper at bay. “And Eddie. She- She wrote about…”

The captain closed his eyes and breathed.

“Did she at least change your names?” Athena asked, lip curling as she glared at the book.

“For all the good it’ll do,” Eddie told her. “But she practically encouraged the reader to look up that piece she did on us.”

“I’ll let the chief know,” Bobby said, opening his eyes again. “The department can handle it.”

“I didn’t know she was gonna do this,” Buck said, frantic for them to believe him. “She didn’t say anything to me, not a word. I swear, if I had, I would have said something, I-”

“Buck,” the captain interrupted. “I know.”

“Why don’t the both of you take a seat,” Athena suggested, gesturing at the other chairs. Both of them knew better than to refuse a “suggestion” from the sergeant, and were quick to follow it. “Tell us what’s in this…”

“Piece of trash?” Eddie filled in for her, when she trailed off.

“Pretty much what you’d expect,” Buck told her and Bobby, crossing his arms on the table and laying his head on them. As they spent most of the night powering through the book, neither of them had gotten much sleep. “She talked about the brownies, she talked about the treasure hunt, and… Other things. In between all that, she implied that Eddie didn’t like her because he was jealous, called me broken, and bragged about having sex with a firefighter.”

“I’m not jealous,” Eddie insisted. “I’ve never been jealous of her.”

Bobby eyed him, clearly remembering all the hickies that showed up on Buck in the days and weeks following Taylor’s reappearance in their lives.
