“She called Chris, his son,” Buck said. It was a line that angered him as much as everything else, even as it was tucked away in the voyeuristic retelling of a day he still had occasional nightmares about. “Not our son, his son.”

“She didn’t write about Chris, did she?” Bobby asked, looking very much like he would be perfectly willing to grab Athena’s gun if she had.

“Not by name or with any detail,” Eddie said. “It was just “Guzman’s son.” That’s the fake name she gave me.”

“Enough about this.” Athena reached out and nudged the book farther away from her. “Let’s talk about something pleasant.”

“What about the temperature that paper burns at?” Bobby managed to make it sound like an idle idea. “We could do a practical demonstration.”

“Tempting…”

Get Dragged

Chapter Summary

In which a proposal goes wrong and then right, but actually it stays wrong.

Chapter Notes

It's my birthday. I am Old.
Warning for some minor biphobia below.

“What the hell is this?” Hen demanded as she and Chimney stared at the offending item sitting on the table, where Buck had tossed it down none-too-gently after he and Eddie joined the others in the station’s loft. “Is that Taylor Kelly?”

“Close to the Flame,” Albert read aloud, leaning forward in his seat. “My Adventures With… Is this a book about us?”

“Afraid so,” Eddie answered for him, popping up at Buck’s side with a cup of coffee for him. He accepted it gratefully. “It’s utter trash.”

“So why is it here?” Hen asked, lip curling a little. 

“We figured you’d want to know what it says,” Buck told her. “I- I don’t really know when she started writing it, but uh, but the last thing she talks about is Jonah.”

That news had Hen‘s face twisting unpleasantly.

“Am I in it?” Ravi asked him, reaching forward to pull the book closer so he could start to flip through it.

“Uh, I don’t- Oh! I think you’re just a nameless probie who got lucky with finding where the treasure was buried.”

“Lucky?” Ravi snapped his head up, looking absolutely insulted. “Lucky?”

“Does she talk about the time she left you standing in a parking lot with your pants undone?” Chimney joked. At Buck’s embarrassed silence, and Eddie’s accompanying scowl, the humor dropped. “Oh my God, she does?”

Albert let out a quiet, “Awkward.”

“So how bad is it?” Hen asked them. “Really?”

“She talks about me,” Bobby said, joining them. “And the brownies.”

“Brownies…?” Ravi asked, glancing between them all.

“Special brownies,” Chimney answered. “Fudge with nuts and a secret ingredient. Which I was spared from eating because all of you- ” He pointed an accusing finger at the ones who had been there at the time. “-are gluttonous pigs.”

“Don’t worry though, I, uh- I took care of that part,” Buck said, scratching at the back of his neck. Ravi thumbed through the book, quickly finding the remnants of the torn out page towards the beginning. “It- It really wasn’t that interesting of a read.”

“You can read through it during our downtime, if you’d like,” Bobby said. “I already told the chief about it, the brass’ll decide what the department’s official statement is going to be.”

And hopefully, Buck thought, they wouldn’t take it out on him this time.

The group broke up to get started on their chores for the day, leaving the book laying on the table. Chimney looked worried, Buck noticed, and wasn’t very surprised when the man grabbed him before he could wander too far.

“In the book,” the paramedic said, sounding unsure. “Did she, uh… Did she say anything about…?”

“The punch did get mentioned, yeah.” It was nice to know that someone was holding a grudge on his behalf, even if he himself had forgiven it.

“Shit. B- But that’s not-” Chimney shook his head. “Did she say why? Did she talk about…”

“Oh! N- No, Maddie’s not… No, not a word.” Buck wasn’t certain what he would have done, if Taylor had put his sister’s struggles with her mental health into that cash grab of a book. It was bad enough that Taylor talked about the shooting, where she framed herself as someone he leaned on for emotional support. “She gets mentioned as my sister and your girlfriend, and that’s about it.”

“Good.” Chimney relaxed a little, relieved. “That’s… That’s good.”

“Alright,” Bobby said over the headset. “We’re responding to a proposal gone wrong. And also a bank robbery.”

“Uh, what?” Albert asked, leaning forward a bit in his jumpseat. Chimney and Hen were the ones riding the ambulance this time. “Was he proposing in the bank?”

“In front of the bank,” the captain explained. “He leaped off a building, and his parachute got caught on the getaway car.”

“How far was he dragged?” Ravi asked.

“Turn here,” Bobby told Jones, eyes fixed on the tablet in his hand. “And he’s still being dragged. Sergeant Grant’s in pursuit.”

Buck shared a look of horror with Eddie. Once, while working on that dude ranch in Montana, one of the horses got spooked and threw their rider off. That would have been bad enough, except the ride got their ankle caught in the stirrup and was dragged for several yards before they could stop the frightened animal. He’d been a mess and a half, afterwards, and that was just over grass and dirt, at less than thirty miles per hour. 

Being dragged behind a car during a high speed chase? Over blacktop? Buck shuddered to imagine, and shuddered at the knowledge that he soon wouldn’t have to imagine.

“Eddie, you’re assisting the paramedics when we get there.”

“Got it, Cap.”

When they caught up to their victim, and the would-be bank robber, the police chase was over. The truck that the robber was driving was smashed into the side of a dump truck, Athena and several other cops surrounding and closing in with their weapons drawn. Buck grabbed the Jaws without being told as Eddie hurried to join the paramedics in a huddle around the victim, who’d left a trail of smeared blood down the road.

There was blue ink splattered on the truck windows, and the driver’s side was blocked by the dump truck. At the captain’s urging, Ravi hurried to check on whoever was in that vehicle.

 Buck and Bobby waited a few feet away while Athena whipped out her baton, smashing the passenger-side window. A barked order at one of the other officers had them opening the door for her. The sergeant was quick to step in close, gun fixed on the driver. She reached in, picking up and passing off a gun, before dragging out a duffel bag that she dropped carelessly onto the pavement.

“Get him out of here so I can arrest his sorry ass,” Athena ordered.

“Yes, ma’am,” Buck said, hurrying to do as she bid. The driver, dressed in black with a ski mask on his head, was just as splattered as the car. The airbag had gone off, and the door and console were crushed inward so that he couldn’t get out, but he didn’t look injured. “It looks like someone microwaved a Smurf in here.”

“Buck.” Bobby did not sound impressed with his comment, so he ducked his head and jammed the Jaws into place.

“This is going to be loud,” he warned the guy. The sound of hydraulics working and metal crunching filled the air, not quite managing to cover up the pained cries of the victim as the paramedics worked on him. “Alright, he’s free.”

Buck stepped back and out of the car. Bobby stepped forward, reaching in and grabbing the bank robber, manhandling him out and pushing him against the truck. Athena had him cuffed and unmasked in no time, quickly marching him to her patrol car.

“He was in a crash,” Buck pointed out, watching them go. “Shouldn’t someone check him over?”

Afterall, just because he seemed fine, didn’t mean there wasn’t something wrong. Sure, the guy was an idiot and a criminal and definitely responsible for someone else being horribly maimed, but Athena herself once got after him for putting his own morals ahead of the job.

“I’ll make sure she doesn’t take off with him before he’s looked at,” Bobby said, following after his wife.

The sound of a woman crying out, “Griffin!” had Buck’s attention. It was a woman running down the street for them, dressed in a business suit and wearing heels. If she ran all the way from the bank, then Buck was impressed.

The vic - Griffin, he assumed - was disconnected from the parachute and loaded up onto a gurney by the time the woman caught up.

“I’m here, I’m here,” she was saying, beelining for his side, the paramedics pausing on their way to the ambulance.

“Blair,” Griffin said, holding up his hand. The light land just right to sparkle off the ring he was holding. “Will you…?”

“Yes,” she said instantly, taking it from him and slipping it on. She kissed him on the head and they continued on.

Blair lingered, though, staring down at the ring on her finger with a frown.

“You can ride with him if you want, ma’am,” Eddie said, having stopped with Chimney when they noticed she wasn’t following.

“Do I have to?” she blurted out. Buck watched Eddie and Chimney exchange glances before walking back to her. He approached as well, hefting the jaws onto his shoulder. “I had to say yes, right? He jumped off a building for me.”

“You were gonna say no?” Chimney asked, sounding rather distraught about it for someone who wasn’t in the relationship. He was probably projecting; the man was as bad about it as Buck was.

“People change when you put a ring on it,” she said. “I liked us how we were.”

“So, what do you wanna do?” Eddie asked, gesturing back at the ambulance.

“I guess I’ll wait a few months and then break it off,” Blair said, not answering the question that Eddie was actually asking. “I heard a proposal gone wrong can scar a guy for life.”

She stepped past them, either heading for the ambulance or planning to walk back to the bank for her car, Buck wasn’t sure. There was a dumbfounded silence that Chimney broke with a snide,

“Yeah. I heard that, too.”

“Oh, give me a break,” Eddie said, scowling at the paramedic’s back as he walked off.

“For what it’s worth,” Buck said, stepping up beside him, “I didn’t say yes just because you were shot.”

“I actually wasn’t worried about that until this moment,” his husband said. “Thanks for that.”

“You’re welcome.” He gestured at Athena’s patrol car with his free hand. The sergeant was standing by the open backdoor, talking to Bobby. “Go check the bank robber over, make sure Ma doesn’t actually have someone die in her custody.”

“If you insist,” Eddie sighed, sounding so very put upon.

Buck laughed and shoved at his shoulder, before leaving him to put away the Jaws.

The copy of Taylor’s book that Buck brought into the station did not go ignored by the rest of their coworkers. Most, like Ortiz and Clarke, scoffed as they read a few pages before dropping it back where they found it. Jones simply used it for a coaster. And then there was Dalton Wolfe, who picked it up and only put it down when he absolutely had to.

It was probably something to be worried about. Buck knew that it was something to be worried about. But he spent so much of his time worrying about the new ways the probie would come up with to be offensive, so much of his time hovering to keep the probie from telling Clarke and Hen how to do their jobs, that he was simply too thankful for the break to really be concerned. So of course, it bit him in the ass.

They were on their way to what would, hopefully, be their last call that shift. It was him and the probie in the truck, along with Eddie and Hen and Chimney, and of course Bobby and Jones. Ravi and Albert, the lucky bastards, were riding the ambulance. They were talking, joking, teasing Buck because the call they were responding to involved a horse-drawn carriage and they weren’t letting him forget about the one from last summer, when Wolfe spoke up.

“Hey, Buck?” the probie said.

“Yeah?” he said, ever so innocent at the time.

“In that book, you’re Stark, right?”

“Right…” He said, sharing a look with the others. Somewhere near the front of the book, last he looked, someone had actually scribbled that conversion table down. He wasn’t sure who it was, but figured it was one of the ones who typically rode the ladder.

“So when did you turn gay?”

“Uh.” Buck’s thoughts were the exact sound a car made, just before it crashed into a wall. Or perhaps that of a computer as it threw up error message after error message. “I…”

“He isn’t,” Hen answered for him, the words sharp and offended on his behalf.

“Oh.” Wolfe scrunched his face up in confusion. “But that reporter lady talks about you and her…?” 

“I’m bi,” Buck said “I’ve- I’ve always been bi.”

“Oh. So you chose a side when you got married, then?”

Hen looked about ready to undo her seat belt and lunge for the probie, who wasn’t paying her any attention. 

“That isn’t how it works, probie,” Eddie said, glaring at the younger man.

Buck twisted in his seat, silently beseeching the captain for help.

“That’s enough.” Bobby’s voice was firm. In the rearview mirror, Buck could see Jones scowling heavily. “We’re here.”

“Oh, thank God,” Chimney said, hurrying to get out.

They ended up rescuing an old woman on her way to her fairytale wedding, whose horse-drawn carriage suffered a bit of dry rot and collapse beneath her. Bobby, ever the romantic, agreed to take her to the church without much prompting or begging. Buck, desperate to not be stuck in the engine with Wolfe again, bullied his way onto the ambulance and left the others to suffer.

Thank God they could go home after this.

 “I am so looking forward to this time off,” Hen said, changed into her civvies and heading out of the lockers. “Denny’s on a sleepover with Nathaniel. Karen and I are going to have ourselves a staycation.”

“What’s the plan?” Buck asked, him and Eddie following behind her with Albert.

“Curl up on the couch and watch nothing but television that’s bad for us,” she answered. “Before our house gets invaded by children, again.”

Buck paused, sharing a frown with the other two. And with Bobby, who was already in the bay.

“Children?” the captain prompted.

“Oh! I haven’t told you?” Hen’s gaze bounced between them, apparently seeing the answer on their faces. “We’re fostering again.”

“That’s great!” Eddie clapped  her on the arm. “We’ll have to see about bringing Gavin and Chris around, one day.”

“Hey, guys,” Ravi called out from across the bay. He and Chimney were still in uniform and headed their way, both of them clutching sheafs of paper. “Before you leave, city wants us to get new parking permits.”

“Annoying, but it’s procedure,” Chimney said.

“Alright,” Bobby said as they all grabbed a copy of the form. “FIll them out, leave them on my desk. I’ll expedite them.”

“Gonna work another double, Chim?” Buck asked, barely glancing at the form.

“I live here now.”

“We love having Chim on the B-side,” Ravi told them. “Or, as we like to call it, the A+ side.”

“We don’t call it that,” Julie Rosen cut in, walking by, Crawford walking beside her as they headed for the stairs. The other B-shift paramedic shook their head in agreement.

“Stop trying to make fetch happen, Panikkar,” they said.

“I’ll get you to start saying it one day!” Ravi called after them both.

“Not a chance!”

“As soon as I cover the deductible of this insurance claim I’m putting in, I’m donezo,” Chimney said.

“Chim…” Hen actually sounded a little sad about it.

“No, no.” The shorter paramedic shook his head. “The ring is gone. I’m sad about it, but I’ll be even sadder if I have to eat the cost of a missing diamond.”

“So get the insurance money,” Eddie suggested.

“Then buy a new ring and propose,” Buck added.

“Really no reason why you shouldn’t,” Bobby agreed.

“Hell, you don’t even need a ring,” Buck said. “I didn’t.”

“I appreciate what you’re all trying to do,” Chimney told them, starting to back away. “But the ring is gone. It’s a sign, from God or from Sauron, and I am heeding the warning. It’s time to move on.”

“Well that was depressing,” Albert said as they watched Chimney climb the stairs to the loft. “And we know why he’s working a double. But why are you?”

He eyed his boyfriend, who smiled.

“It’s a surprise,” Ravi said. Albert rolled his eyes.

“Fine,” he huffed, going for Bobby’s office. He paused to kiss him on the cheek along the way. “Then I’ll see you at home. Where I’ll be all alone. In bed. By myself. Without my boyfriend…”

“He doesn’t play fair,” Ravi complained, letting out a besotted sigh. He turned back around from where he was gazing after Albert, frowning at their expressions. “What?”

“You’re not saving up for a ring, are you?” Hen asked.

“No!” he was quick to say. Too quick.

“What, is Al-?” Buck started, feeling defensive for Albert, but his husband grabbed his arm and started pulling him away.

“Yeah, yeah,” Eddie said. “Let’s go fill out and drop off these forms, mi sol. You can defend Albert’s honor later.”

“Fine…”

The Rest is Confetti

Chapter Summary

In which Buck sticks his foot in his mouth, Grandpops returns, and Maddie talks to Hen.

“So, I have a hypothetical scenario for you,” TK said when Buck answered the phone.

“Hypothetically speaking, Carlos is right,” he said, pushing himself off the floor. 

He, and Eddie, had been helping Gavin build up and knock down blocks in the living room. The television was on, playing a rerun of a telenovela that Abuela wasn’t actually watching as she napped in her chair. Chris was sitting at the dining table, laptop in front of him as he worked on a project for school.

“What? Carlos?” TK sputtered. “Carlos isn’t even in this hypothetical!”

“Still, he’s right.”

“You’re supposed to be my friend.”

“I am. That’s why I’m saying he’s right.”

Buck could almost hear the other man glowering at him through the phone.

“Anyway,” TK gritted out. “Hypothetically speaking-”

“Carlos is still right.”

“Anyway! Say that Judd was acting captain and-”

“Wait,” Buck interrupted. “Why is Judd acting captain?”

“Dad drove Uncle Robert back to LA.” TK sounded odd as he said it. “I think he’ll be there for a few days. Anyway, say Judd’s acting captain and there was a fire at a paper mill. And at this paper mill fire, there was a guy with his arm stuck in a roller press.”

“Ow.”

“Exactly. But - hypothetically - the guy is stuck and Judd gives the order to evacuate the building because things are about to explode, and Captain Vega decides to stay behind to keep working on him. Who’s in the right? And remember, there’s no wrong answers. Unless you say it’s Judd.”

Buck rolled his eyes.

“Judd was IC?”

“Yes…? Damn it, Buck, no. You’re supposed to be on our side.”

“And I am. I would totally stay behind to help the guy, no matter how big the explosion,” Buck told him, shaking his head and mouthing ‘no’ to a worried looking Eddie. “But if Judd was IC…”

“You’re totally disappointing me right now, Sunshine,” his friend complained. “I’m pretty sure he called HR on her.”

“That’s a di-” He caught himself and glanced at Gavin, who giggled happily as he knocked over the block tower Eddie just finished stacking. “That wasn’t a friendship building moment.”

“...I hope you know, I am totally going to make fun of you for saying that in that tone.”

“I expect nothing less.”

Someone rapped on the door, setting Hoover off and startling Abuela awake. Buck said his goodbyes to TK and got to his feet, patted the dog on the head and went to answer. It was his sister standing there, looking utterly done and still dressed for work.

“Oh, Maddie, this is a-” The rest of the sentence died in his throat when she raised her left hand, showing off the engagement ring glittering on her finger. “Chimney popped the question?”

“N-” she started to see, but was interrupted by Abuela. The older woman let out an excited cry and leaped to her feet in a show of youthful spyness that he hadn’t ever seen from her.

“¿Preguntó?” Abuela hurried to them so she could grab Maddie’s hand and start cooing over the ring. “¡Felicidades!”

“Hi, Aunt Maddie,” Chris called from the dining room, waving from his spot in the dining room.

“Hi, Chris. And Howie, uh. Howie hasn’t asked yet,” Maddie said, flustered. “I found this in Jee’s dollhouse.”

“Ay. Bah.” Abuela dropped her hand, tsking and shaking her head as she went back to her chair.

“How did it end up in Jee’s dollhouse?” Eddie asked, getting to his feet, too. Buck let his sister the rest of the way in, closing the door behind her. “We thought he lost it in the landfill.”

“In the what?” Maddie sounded as confused as Buck felt.

“Back up,” he said. “If he didn’t propose, why are you wearing it?”

“Because I found it,” his sister said, all petulance. “And I was looking at it. And it was shiny, and pretty. And, y’know, I tried it on.”

“I don’t think you should do that,” he couldn’t help pointing out. “Pretty sure it’s bad luck. Not a word, Diaz.”

“I wasn’t going to say anything,” Eddie said, in a tone that told Buck he was absolutely going to say something.

“That’s probably why it’s stuck,” Maddie said, tugging on the ring in demonstration. “I’ve tried everything. Olive oil, taking a shower, anything I could think of. Could you help me?”

Everything, huh? Well, Chimney was already planning on filing an insurance claim for it.

“Sure. Babe, where’s the ring cutter?”

“Pretty sure it’s in the drawer on your side of the bed,” Eddie answered, a bit dubious.

“Wait,” Maddie said, stopping him before he could go fetch it. “You have one of those at home? For what?”

“Uh.” Buck gave a purposefully casual shrug. “Other stuff.”

Don’t look at Eddie. Don’t look at Eddie. Don’t look at Eddie.

“What other stuff?” his sister asked. He wasn’t sure if it was the expression he was making or the way that Abuela cackled in her chair, but her own face scrunched up in disgust. “Nevermind. Forget it. I’m not gonna cut it off, ‘cause it’s my engagement ring.”

“You said he hadn’t asked,” Eddie pointed out. “If he hasn’t asked, and you haven’t said yes…”

“That’s my next question,” Maddie told them. “Why didn’t he ask?”

“He lost the ring.”

“And then he lost his mind,” Buck continued. Gavin toddled up to him, a pair of brightly colored blocks clutched in his hand, and so Buck’s attention was split as he entertained his kid. “We went on a call, came back, ring was gone. He was convinced we were hazing him. Things got kind of tense and I got into it with Hen-”

“Uh, Buck,” Eddie tried.

“- He got into it with Hen-”

“Mi sol-”

“Got into what?” Maddie asked.

“Whether or not- Oh, you want me to stack them for you?” Buck took one of the blocks, setting them on top of the misshapen pile. “Whether or not to propose. We tore up the whole firehouse trying to find it, but nothing. Because apparently Jee had it.”

“Hen doesn’t think we should get married?”

Buck’s head shot up at the question, finally focusing on the words that just left his mouth.

Oh. Oh shit.

“It- It wasn’t like that,” he stammered out. When he turned to Eddie for help, his traitorous husband raised his hands and shook his head.

“Then what was it like?” his sister demanded.

“She just- She was concerned-” Fuck, that wasn’t any better to say, because now she was headed for the door. “Maddie-”

“It’s fine, I’ve got to go.”

“Maddie!”

The door clicked shut behind his sister and Buck slumped in place.

“How does your foot taste?” Eddie asked.

“Shut up,” he grumbled. “Awful.”

“Dad, Pops,” Chris tentatively called out. “I’m done.”

“Alright, Superman, let’s- Oh.”

“That’s a lot of design work,” Eddie said, joining him and leaning over Chris’ shoulder to look at it. On the screen was an isometric view of Mission Santa Barbara. “I’m not sure this mission thing’s supposed to be this involved.”

“It’s got to be good,” their son replied.

“Yeah, Dad,” Buck said, grinning at his glower.

“What’s this area right here?” Eddie asked, pointing at what Buck was fairly certain was a portion of the Santa Ynez Mountains. Because their child was just that extra.

“That’s where the indigenous people live,” Christopher explained, stumbling over the word a little and reminding Buck that he and Eddie should probably take a peek at what their kid was being taught. Just to be safe and make sure things weren’t being whitewashed, because he could and would raise hell at the next Parent-Teacher Organization meeting.

“Right,” Eddie said, nodding as he straightened up again. “I think we’re gonna need some professional help.

The two men shared a look. 

Buck pulled his phone back out.

“I’ll call Pops.”

Bobby was, perhaps, a bit too eager to drop everything after Buck’s call. To be fair to him, Athena was still on shift and May was busy with school and friends, so it wasn’t like he really had much of anything to drop, honestly. Still, there probably should have been a bit more time between answering the phone and getting into the car, if only for the sake of his dignity.

Then again, this was for his grandkid, so his dignity didn’t really get much of a say.

Apparently, Chris was building a diorama of the mission in Santa Barbara. Bobby was fairly certain he remembered something about the schools no longer doing that project a few years ago, not long after he moved to Los Angeles. He was also fairly certain that when they did do it, it was a couple grades below the one Chris was in now. And that they made them out of popsicle sticks and sugar cubes.

Chris had no intention of making his diorama out of popsicle sticks and sugar cubes, that much was clear. The kid had a list of supplies that they needed and was leading the way down the aisles of the hardware store, directing Bobby on what to grab. It reminded him very much of a certain person with a clipboard, but that was an observation he’d save for later.

“They have pre-fab models,” he said, because he should at least make the effort.

“I know,” Chris said, so dismissively that his opinions on that topic were clear. He frowned at his list and then up at what was on offer, before pointing. “That one, please.”

“Better manners than your pops had when I first met him,” Bobby remarked, reaching up and grabbing what was requested. He dropped it into the cart with the rest. “I hope your brother takes after you. Where to next?”

“Uh.” The kid peered around, twirling in place a little. “There, plywood. For the base.”

He let Chris lead the way to an aisle down  and across from where they were. At the end was the plywood, and Bobby grabbed a panel that seemed to be the right size. He also grabbed a second, just in case.

“You’ll need epoxy, too, I think,” Bobby said, mostly to himself. He was pretty sure they passed it. Turning the cart around, he asked, “So your dads mentioned you were really getting into this.”

“I guess,” Chris said, shrugging a little. “I want it to be good.”

Bobby hummed to himself. There was a memory of Eddie complaining that Buck spent far too much on Legos, one Christmas or birthday, that was nagging at his brain.

“Is this something you want to do when you’re older?” he asked. “Designing things? Building things?”

“Maybe. I don’t know.”

“You don’t have to know,” Bobby assured him. “My father was a firefighter, and so was my grandfather, so that’s what I did. But your pops did just about everything else before settling on it. And Athena was going to school to be a lawyer before she decided to be a cop.”

“You mean, ‘Nana,’” the kid said, grinning at him.

“If you want to call her that, you go right ahead. She won’t shoot you,” he said, making Chris laugh. “Alright, here’s the epoxy. Now, your dads didn’t say when to have you back, so what do you want to do after we check out?”

The kid thought about it for a moment, before settling on,

“McDonalds?”

“I can take you home and make you burgers,” Bobby pointed out.

“I know. But I want McDonalds,” Chris said, looking up at him all wide-eyed, his bottom lip jutting out just a little. “Please?”

Bobby let out a sigh.

“Fine,” he said, shaking his head as his oldest grandkid let out a cheer. Maybe he’d call Athena, if she wasn’t busy, and see if she’d had anything to eat yet. Even if the meal he was offering was disgustingly unhealthy fast food.

The good thing about her brother living across the city from the Wilsons is that, by the time Maddie pulled up, she wasn’t quite so upset. Not to imply that she wasn’t upset at all, because she was, but also much more clear headed. It helped, too, that she took a meandering route that added to the travel time.

Regardless, she still marched up and rapped on the door with perhaps a bit more force than necessary.

“Maddie? Hey,” Hen greeted her when she opened the door, letting her in. Her face must have been showing her emotions, because the next thing she asked was, “Is something wrong?”

“Yeah, I think there might be,” Maddie said. “I found the ring.”

“Where? How?” were the first questions, followed by, “Did you tell Chim?”

“I got the feeling I wasn’t supposed to know he’d bought it,” she said. “I also got the feeling that you don’t think he should’ve bought it.”

Hen’s face dropped and she took a breath, visibly steeling herself for the conversation.

“I don’t… I don’t know what Buck told you,” she said, and Maddie supposed it was fair to make that (correct) assumption. It still annoyed her, though. “But that’s not what I said.”

“Then what did you say?”

“That you’ve both been through so much-”

“You mean that I put him through so much.”

“That isn’t-”

“I left him and Jee for a reason.”

“Yes,” Hen said, nodding. “To protect them. So you have to understand that that’s why I said what I did to Chimney. I was just trying to protect him, too.”

“From me.”

“No, from himself.” She meant protect him from making what Hen thought was a bad choice, but Maddie bit her tongue. “He was so stressed about whether or not he should propose, I just… Wanted him to take a breath. Maybe you should, too.”

Hen gestured to her couch, obviously offering Maddie a seat.

“He’s your best friend,” she said, making no move to sit down. “You love him. And he loves you, too. Your opinion matters to him, even more than Bobby’s. You probably could have kept him from being stressed at all.”

The other woman frowned at that, but she didn’t argue. Instead, Hen asked,

“Why haven’t you told Chim you found the ring?”

Maddie didn’t need to ask how Hen knew she hadn’t, yet, because they both knew the man would have been blowing up Hen’s phone. Because Hen was his best friend, and he loved her, and she would have been the first he told.

“You think Howie is the only one stressing about this?” Maddie asked in return, holding up her hand to show the ring. “This is more than just words and a piece of paper, there’s a power that we give away. But it’s everything I want, just like I thought it was before.”

“You aren’t the same person that you were back then,” Hen said.

“I’m not even the same person now that I was when I broke. But the thing is, this?” She tugged on the ring a bit. “This doesn’t change anything, not really. We’ll go to sleep in the same bed and wake up in the same bed, in the house we bought together and where we’re raising a child together. It doesn’t make things more real, it just makes them more official. It’s the icing on the cake, and it’s the confetti, and it’s the neat little bow on top.

“But it also makes things safer. Safer for me, safer for Jee-Yun. Safer for Howie.”

“You’ve been thinking about this, too.”

“My baby brother died and woke up to tell me he saw it in his dream,” Maddie pointed out. “Of course I’ve been thinking about it.”

“That boy.” Hen laughed as she said it, shaking her head a little. “I don’t think I’ve ever seen a person so excited for someone else’s proposal.”

“All he has ever wanted was for me to be happy. And he knows that Howie makes me happy.”

“I’m sorry,” Hen said, and Maddie was quick to shake her head.

“No, don’t apologize. I’m not- Okay, I was upset,” she admitted. “But he’s your best friend and you love him just as much as I do, if not more. Why would I ever want you to apologize for putting him before me?”

“He took Buck and I to help him pick out the ring,” Hen told her.

“It is a very pretty ring,” Maddie said, holding her hand out to show it off. “It’s also very stuck.”

“Do you want me to help you get it off?”

“Not on your life.”

There was the beginnings of a plot starting to sprout in Maddie’s head. She just needed to get it set up before Howie got home in the morning…

Buck’s phone chimed as he walked through the door, him and Chris getting home from equine therapy. He couldn’t check it yet, with his arms full of flailing toddler, so it had to wait until he sat Gavin down and took off his shoes. Gavin, of course, was off like a rocket the moment he was free, with Hoover following along loyally.

The message on his phone, when Buck finally pulled it out to look, had him grinning widely: a picture of Maddie and Chimney’s hands side-by-side on their patio table. He recognized the engagement ring his sister was wearing, the one she’d been wearing since yesterday, apparently. But he also recognized the clunky plastic ring that Chimney was wearing, because Jee-Yun liked making him wear it on occasion when he was over.

There was definitely a story there, but that could wait, because,

“Abuela!” he called out, stepping into the backyard. The woman in question sat up from where she was tending to her garden. “One of them finally asked the question. How would you feel about helping me plan an engagement party?”

Staring Into Open Flame

Chapter Summary

In which there's a celebration and a fire.

Buck was a people person, this much was never in doubt. He was in his element any time he could be sociable, which was probably why the other shifts mistakenly called his moderately less social husband ‘the quiet one’ in comparison. Because Eddie wasn’t really quiet, especially if you got him on a tangent, he was simply not as loud.

But Buck was a people person, and he was currently in heaven.

The backyard was filled. There was a fold-out table with refreshments sitting out. There were hastily-bought decorations tacked onto the back wall of the house and stretched along the privacy fence, with fairy lights twisting around trees and poles. Buck’s record player was dragged out and plugged in and playing music. And there were people, so many people. Perhaps too many people.

Besides their little core group, Buck had invited the Lees, along with Sue and Josh and Linda from dispatch, and whoever else they passed the invitation on to. There was the other half of their shift and some of their families. There were a lot of people, and when he opened the door to let Ravi and Albert in and spotted all the cars lining the street, he was very thankful Abuela had him go around to his neighbors with pre-apology baked goods.

“You’re late,” Buck accused. “To your brother’s engagement party.”

“Our building has a pool, we lost track of time,” Albert said. “‘Sides, we aren’t the last ones here.”

He pointed and Buck followed, spotting Wolfe unfolding himself from the front seat of a bright yellow mini cooper across the street. It was impressive, because Buck didn’t think there was any way he could fit into that car, and the probie had a couple inches on him in height.

“Don’t give me that look,” he said, spotting the scowl on Ravi’s face. “He was standing right there when I invited everyone! What was I supposed to do, dis invite him?”

Ravi grumbled something that sounded suspiciously like “Yes” before saying, “Get out of the way and let us in, I can smell Bobby’s food.”

“And Abuela’s!” Buck happily added, shuffling to the side so they could pass him. He lingered at the door, waiting on Wolfe to make his way up the driveway. He frowned when he saw the probie was still standing by his car, gaze focused on Edward and Shanna’s house. It was the only one with a Trump flag and beset on all sides by rainbow decorations.

Finally, just as Buck was about to shout for his attention, the young man turned and headed his way. There was a plastic clamshell of cookies gripped in his hand.

“You didn’t have to bring anything,” Buck said when he got to the door.

“Mama wouldn’t let me leave the house without it,” Wolfe said. Buck shrugged, because that was more than fair.

“You can just toss it on the table with everything else. C’mon.”

The backyard was just as crowded as when he left it. He led Wolfe to the refreshments table while stepping around Denny and Chris, who were scampering off to hide in the latter’s room with a bag of chips they’d liberated. After, when the cookies were deposited and the probie wandered off to (probably) offend people in various ways, Buck grabbed a plastic flute of champagne and started making the rounds.

There was Chimney, showing off the plastic costume jewelry that both Maddie and Jee-Yun surprised him with when he got home from his shift, where they had breakfast set up and proceeded to propose to him. Julie Rosen was nodding along to the words as he said,

“So cute, right? I promised I’d wear it all day.”

“Might be a problem on shift,” Rosen pointed out, sipping at her own bubbly drink.

“It’s been fine so far. Hasn’t it, Buck?”

“It’s been perfectly fine,” Buck answered dutifully, shaking his head the moment his future brother-in-law wasn’t looking. “He didn’t actually wear it all shift, but he did have Al take a picture so he could lie and say that he did.”

“Buck!” Chimney hissed, elbowing him. He glanced around. “Not where Jee can hear!”

“Sorry!” he said, ducking away and moving on. He passed by Bobby, talking to Anne and John Lee, and then Athena, chatting away with Sue from Dispatch. Maddie was with Josh, and Buck bounded to the pair so he could pull her into a one-armed hug.

“How many of those have you had?” Josh asked, nodding at his drink as Maddie wiggled her way to freedom.

“This is my first one.” Buck glanced down at it; it was almost full. “Why?”

“Nothing, you’re just playing up the Golden Retriever resemblance a bit,” the dispatcher said, which had him frowning, because that reminded him of- “I noticed Taylor’s not here.”

“Good riddance,” Maddie said, mostly into her own drink.

“Taylor and I aren’t really… Talking, at the moment,” Buck said. The last time he’d spoken to her, the morning after he read her book, some very not nice things had been said on both sides. 

“So I’ve heard,” Josh said, side-eyeing him a bit. “Are you going to be the Best Man at the wedding?”

“I’m leaving that up to Hen and Al to fight over. I’m gonna be the Man of Honor.”

“I didn’t agree to that!” Maddie complained, smacking him in the stomach. “I’m the bride, I get to choose my Maid of Honor.”

“Well who’d you choose instead?” he asked. She scowled at him, before grumbling out,

“I’ll make you wear the ugliest dress I can find, I swear. And you’ll have to wear it, because it’ll be my wedding.”

“S’long as I can wear flats. Heels and I do not mix.”

“Know that from personal experience?” Josh asked, giving him a once-over. Buck fluttered his eyelashes.

“I might.”

“Go bother someone else,” Maddie ordered. He allowed himself to be shoved away, laughing as he bounced off to entertain another grouping of guests.

Buck did circuit after circuit of the backyard, having the time of his life as he greeted and talked and entertained. He paused to heckle Ravi and Albert, went out of his way to embarrass May just because he could, and only had to step in thrice when he overheard Wolfe quickly approaching the event horizon between insensitivity and actual offense. It was really only a matter of time before the kid got punched, but Buck did not want it to happen tonight, thanks. And the champaign that the kid was drinking probably wasn’t helping.

Wait, shit, was he even old enough to drink yet?

Buck swooped in a fourth time to casually give him some fruit punch, just to be safe.

After his last circuit, he made his way to the patio. Eddie was on his feet, talking to Karen while he rocked an increasingly drowsy looking Gavin in his arms. Jee-Yun was fast asleep in a patio chair.

“There you are,” Karen said when he joined them.

“Here I am. Not that I’ve been anywhere else, really.”

“And yet it feels like this is the first time I’ve seen you all night,” Eddie said, smirking at him. “Enjoying yourself?”

“I am,” Buck told him, grinning widely. His husband smiled in turn, adjusting Gavin so he could reach up and run his thumb along the corner of Buck’s eye. “What?”

“Nothing,” Eddie said, even as he kept it up.

“Doesn’t seem like nothing when you’re groping my face,” Buck said, reaching up to grab his hand, tangling their fingers together.

“It’s a nice face,” the other man said. “And I like seeing you like this.”

“Like what?”

Eddie gave a soft little hum.

“Eres mi sol,” he said. “Iluminas mi vida, y te amo tanto por eso.”

And then he pulled his hand free and headed inside with Gavin, presumably to put him to bed.

“H- Hey! You can’t just say that and leave! Get back here!” Buck called after him, but Eddie only laughed as he let the door close behind him. He turned to Karen. “Can you believe that man!?”

“Yes, yes, whatever he said was very sweet,” she said, patting his cheek. “Has Hen told you about our new foster kids?”

“She has. But uh, actually, I’m gonna put this one inside, too.”

He set down his drink, scooped up Jee-Yun, and followed after his husband to put his slumbering niece somewhere better than a patio chair. And if he happened to run into Eddie while he did so, and if they happened to take a detour into their bedroom for a bit, well. The party could handle itself without him for a short while, surely.

Karen’s laughter followed him the entire way inside.

(The teasing he got from his sister the moment she spotted the fresh marks just under his shirt collar, marks that hadn’t been there earlier in the evening, was absolutely worth it.)

“You have the plane tickets, right?” TK asked.

“We have the tickets,” Buck assured him, settling against the arm of the couch and tugging Eddie into his side. It was the evening after the party, and most of the day had been spent cleaning up what they put off the night before. “We’ll be there next week, barring any horrible disasters.”

“Not that there haven't been enough disasters already. And now Carlos’ dad wants to wear a cowboy hat with his suit.”

“It’s Texas,” Buck said, the fingers of his free hand running through his husband’s hair. “Aren’t cowboy hats part of the dress code?”

“Absolutely,” Eddie cut in, voice partially muffled with his face pressed into Buck’s shoulder. “You’ve got the fancy dress party cowboy hat, and then the casual dress one, and the one you break out for a funeral. It’s black.”

“I didn’t hear everything your boy just said, but tell him I said to go to hell.”

“Teek says he loves you-”

“That is not-”

“- And that he would elope with you right now if it wasn’t for me and Carlos.”

“You are such an ass. He wishes he was good enough for me.”

Eddie lifted his head enough to blink and frown at him, before laying it back on his shoulder.

“Too much hassle,” he said. “Easier to just keep the one I’m stuck with.”

“Stuck with!?” Buck squawked, tugging at his hair. “The complete and utter disrespect. You’re just lucky Chris is such a cool kid, and that your Abuela and Tia are so wonderful…”

“I always knew you married me for my kid.”

“And your abuela’s cooking.”

“Is this flirting?” TK asked, reminding Buck that he was on the phone. “Should I put it on speaker so Carlos can hear and this can get really weird?”

“We’re married with kids, one of whom is a toddler,” Buck said. “Our flirting is usually asking the other, ‘hey, you wanna?’ And then taking off our pants.”

“Nothing but the best romance for my guy,” Eddie said, stretching out along the couch.

“...is it too late to return the rings?”

They talked for a bit longer, before his friend hopped off because Carlos needed something. Buck set his phone down on the end table and manhandled a grumbling Eddie so the man was on top of him and he could stretch his legs along the couch as well. After a moment, he reached out and turned off the lamp that was on.

There was an orange glow, flickering along the wall and the television.

There was an orange glow, one that Buck knew far too intimately, and he just about shoved Eddie to the ground when he jumped up and looked out their front window.

Across the street, the side of one of the houses - the side of Edward and Shanna’s house - was ablaze.

“Fuck,” Buck swore, running for the door. He grabbed his shoes and tugged them on as he flung the door open. The smell of smoke hit him in the face, and he heard Eddie’s own swearing right behind him.

He sprinted across the street, barely missing being hit by the yellow car that sped past him.

“This is off-duty firefighter Eddie Diaz,” his husband was saying, on the line with 9-1-1 and telling them what was happening. Which was good, because Buck hadn’t thought to grab his own phone.

Edward and Shanna’s house was fully involved, flames covering the right side of the building with dark plumes of smoke billowing into the air. There was absolutely no reason for it to be this big already, not without someone noticing. How did it get this bad without anyone calling for help already? Shit, where was-

Just as he had that thought, there was a flurry of activity at the front door. It burst open, a teenaged girl hurrying out with the boys that threw the eggs, Edward right behind them.

“Over here!” Buck said, intercepting them. He and Eddie led them to the sidewalk, the pair of them falling into work mode and checking them over. “Are any of you hurt?”

“N- No,” the girl said, the boys shaking their heads. Actually, their everything was shaking. They were absolutely terrified, clutching to each other and to their sister, the three of them dressed for bed.

“Sh- Shanna,” Edward gasped out, looking around. “Wh- Where’s Shanna?”

“She was inside?” Eddie asked, shooting a look at Buck.

“She was getting Clara,” the other man said, already turning back for the house. “She- Shanna!”

“No, no, you can’t go in there,” Eddie wrapped his arms around him, stopping him from running back into the blaze. “You can’t- Buck, no!”

Buck was, of course, already half-way across the paved-over yard before he even heard his husband’s angry and worried shouting. He bounded up the porch steps, stepping around the flag that was already starting to catch, and reached the still open front door.

And then he stopped, stumbling back from the heat and the smoke, eyes going wide as he spotted the inferno devouring the inside. He thought he could hear them, Shanna shouting and Clara crying, but there were flames crawling along the floor and stretching for the ceiling, there was smoke rolling out like the sea. They were in there, and every fiber of his being was telling him to rush in, to do what he could, but…

He glanced back.

“Shanna!” Edward was shouting, struggling against Eddie’s grip. Eddie was staring at him, eyes wide and begging. “Shanna!”

Buck looked to the fire, took a hesitant step forward-

And then he turned again and ran back to Eddie.

“C’mon, over here you guys,” Buck said, herding the kids across the street as the sound of sirens got closer. Their neighbors had finally stumbled out, drawn by the shouting and gawking at the scene. “Ov- Over here, where it’s safer.”

“Shanna!” Edward was shouting, struggling so much to get to her that Buck had to help Eddie hold him back. “Clara! No, no, let me go! Shanna! Clara!”

“It’s too dangerous,” Eddie said, but the other man wasn't listening, didn’t care, kept struggling to get to his wife.

It felt like forever before the trucks arrived.

“I know it doesn’t help,” Eddie whispered, hours later. They were curled around each other in their bed, both of them smelling like smoke simply from proximity. “But I’m proud of you.”

Buck’s derisive snort expressed his feelings better than any words could.

“I’m serious,” Eddie continued. “That fire. If you had gone in…”

If he had gone in without the gear they wore for those exact situations, Eddie didn’t say, then he wouldn’t have come back out. Not in one piece, at least. And the thing was, Buck knew that. He knew it the moment he gazed into that burning hell, and he knew that the moment he took that step forward, and he knew that the moment he turned tail.

But…

“I heard her,” Buck whispered back. “She was calling for help, and I could hear her, and I-”

“There wasn’t anything you could have done,” Eddie said. “You would have just needed rescuing, too.”

It was the truth, and Buck knew it was the truth.

But that didn’t help a single bit.

Breaking

Chapter Summary

In which things break.

Chapter Notes

See the end of the chapter for notes

The day was not going to be a good one.

Neither Buck nor Eddie slept very much or very well. They had both stayed outside to keep Edward and his kids, bratty as they were, out of the way of the firefighters that were trying to save what they could. Eventually, they managed to hand them off to paramedics from both the 134 and the 94, but they didn’t go inside right away. At one point, Abuela came out with a sniffling Gavin, woken by the noise, and Buck eagerly took him from her arms.

After the blaze had been contained enough for them to go in, the firefighters carried out a shape that no longer looked quite like a person, along with a bundle that easily fit in their arms. Buck and Eddie went inside then, because they didn’t want to watch Edward break all over again.

Neither of them slept very much nor very well, and they were slow to pull themselves out of bed when their alarm went off. They went through the motions of rousing Chris for school, of getting Gavin changed, of greeting Carla when she arrived. They allowed Abuela to share what she knew, when Carla asked about the blackened skeleton of a building that now stood across the street.

A blackened skeleton that Buck had a hard time looking away from when they first stepped outside and hopped into the jeep.

“I already know the answer,” he said, when it was no longer visible in their rearview mirror. “But how are you?”

“I’m fine,” Eddie lied. Buck took advantage of a stop sign to give him his best expression of disbelief, receiving a sigh in return. “I had a nightmare about Shannon.”

“I figured you would,” Buck said, reaching over to grab and squeeze his husband’s hand. 

There was no way that Eddie wasn’t going to have a nightmare about his late wife, not after having to listen to a man’s pain-filled wailing for a woman whose name was just short of being the same.

“I’ll talk to Copeland. But I really am fine,” Eddie insisted. “And I am proud of you, for not going in there. I know how hard it had to have been to turn back.”

Buck let out a hum, not really feeling proud of himself for it. With the heat and the smoke that blasted him in the face through that front door, he knew there was no way he would’ve gotten in and out without his gear. Still, though, it galled that he didn’t do anything.

“Maybe… Maybe it’s the meds working?” he suggested, fingers tapping against the steering wheel as he drove. “Keeping me from pulling dumb shit.”

“Maybe.” Eddie didn’t sound like he quite agreed, though.

The route they took, La Brea and then Washington, kept them off the mess of traffic that was the Santa Monica Freeway. It meant that, despite having a slow start to their morning, they managed to make it to the station before they were considered late. Only barely, though.

“Cutting it close, Diazes,” Bobby remarked with a glance at his watch when they stomped up the stairs to the loft. The rest of their shift was already gathered for that morning’s briefing.

“Sorry Cap, long night,” Buck said, taking a spot to lean against the railing as Eddie went on to get them both coffee. “Neighbors’ house caught fire.”

He watched Bobby’s eyes widen in concern.

“Was anyone…?” he started to ask, but clearly Buck’s expression was enough of an answer for him to let the question die. “Well, you’re just in time.”

“Which neighbors?” Albert asked. The captain rolled his eyes at the interruption.

“The neighbors,” Eddie answered as he waited for the coffee machine to do its magic.

From the murmuring of the others, they seemed to be torn between sympathy and schadenfreude. Buck would probably have been the same, except he’d been there, he’d seen and he’d heard. It didn’t matter how much he disliked them, it didn’t matter how much their (actual and assumed) actions hurt him and his family. Last night had still been horrible, and he wouldn’t wish it upon anyone.

“Alright, we have some outstanding tasks from our last shift,” Bobby said, trying to get their attention back on track. It was like herding cats, at times. “And an important announcement about our upcoming weather conditions.”

Eddie carefully made his way around their coworkers to Buck’s side with the mugs of coffee, nodding along to the captain’s words as he went.

“Thanks,” Buck whispered, taking his and letting out a happy hum when he sipped at it.

“Buck!”

“Yeah, Cap?”

“The two of you,” the captain said, gesturing at him and Eddie, “and Wolfe are helping Jones and Ortiz with apparatus maintenance today. Oil and brake fluid changes.”

“Got it, Cap.”

“Chimney, Al, and…” Bobby trailed off with a scowl as he looked around the gathered crowd. Buck did the same and noticed that Hen wasn’t in yet. “Chimney and Al, they got hit pretty hard with medical calls last night. First thing today, make sure both ambos are fully stocked.”

“On it, Cap,” Chimney said, his brother nodding beside him.

“Ravi, Hendrix, go ahead and do inventory for the-”

“Hey!” Buck cried out, having turned to look over the station bay now that he’d gotten his marching orders for the day. He felt the captain’s glare on his back at yet another interruption, but he ignored it. “Look who finally showed up!”

Below them, obviously trying for a stealthy entrance, was Hen. She awkwardly waved up at them as she stood in the locker room doorway.

“Sorry I’m late!” Hen shouted up at them. Bobby, having moved to stand just beside Buck so he could see what grabbed his attention, let out a grunt.

“Let’s get cracking,” he said, dismissing them all. “Chim, tell Hen what you’re doing.”

“Will do,” the paramedic said as he headed for the stairs. “Wait, what was the important weather update?”

“There’s a moderate marine layer.”

“The May Gray,” Jones sighed, already halfway down with Ortiz. Wolfe was right below them, though he kept glancing back up. Buck got the impression the kid was looking at him and Eddie, but he wasn’t sure.

“How are you?” Bobby asked, and as out of the blue the question seemed, it wasn’t really a surprise.

“We’re fine,” Buck and Eddie said together, sharing a look immediately after. Bobby snorted out a laugh.

“How bad was it?” he asked. “Really?”

“Two-alarm,” Eddie answered. “Took them hours to get it under control. It was half-gone before we even ran out of the house.”

“I still don’t know how it got so bad without anyone noticing,” Buck said. “I think it went fast.”

“You didn’t answer my question earlier,” Bobby pointed out. “Was anyone…?”

“Shanna apparently went to grab their youngest,” Eddie said, a comforting heat pressed along Buck’s side. “And then couldn’t get out.”

Bobby let out a sigh. It was a full-body sigh, the heavy sort that a person only let out when they had an intimate knowledge of all the details hidden by such an innocuous statement. He clasped his hand on Buck’s shoulder.

“Both of you are here,” he said, “and I wasn’t woken up in the middle of the night by a phone call. I assume neither of you did anything stupid, then?”

“No foolish heroics from us,” Buck said, raising his coffee up in a cheerless toast.

“We both stayed outside,” Eddie added. “Made sure Edward didn’t run back in and that the kids were fine.”

“Good,” Bobby said. “I’m-”

“Will you shut the fuck up!”

It was a shout that filled the entire station, echoing off the rafters and silencing all other noise. Eddie had a reputation with the other shifts for being ‘the quiet one’ only because he was being compared to Buck. Jones was actually the quiet one, so quiet he could be and often was forgotten, even as he drove them to scenes and back. Buck wasn’t sure he’d ever heard the man raise his voice, not once.

Not until now.

“What the hell?” Buck whispered, staring wide-eyed down at the tableau below them. The driver-engineer was stomping away from a visibly recoiling Wolfe, Ortiz lingering only long enough to shake his head in agitation before following after him. “One of us should probably find out what that was about, right?”

As one, he and Eddie turned their heads to look expectantly at the captain, who let out another, less weighty sigh.

“Go help Ortiz with the ladder’s maintenance,” he ordered, and then called out, “Jones! My-”

The alarm sounded then, dispatch giving details overhead about a crash on the 710. Bobby glared upward, as if either the alarm, God, or both had personally offended him.

“Nevermind. Ortiz, grab one of the others to help with the ladder!” he barked out, jogging down the stairs. Buck drained the rest of his coffee and hurried behind him with Eddie. “Al, stay here and make sure that second ambo is stocked! Ravi, close that compartment and get your gear on! Hen, you too! Hurry up!”

It was a mad scramble to get into their turn-outs and clamber into either the engine or an ambulance. The drive there was made in mostly awkward silence except for Bobby giving Jones directions and giving them the general overview of what details he had. Wolfe was not very talkative, hunkered down into his coat like a turtle retreating into its shell. Jones positively simmered in the driver’s seat, scowling straight ahead and not once looking in the rearview mirror at them.

It was with much relief that they leaped out when they arrived and pulled to a stop on the overpass, the pressures of the job a fine refuge from all of that.  

“Hen, Chim, check the driver of this camper van,” Bobby ordered, gesturing at the vehicle that was on its side. There was another large orange van parked a bit aways from it, very obviously the cause of the first being flipped judging from the smashed-in nose. There was also another pair of cars, ones that were in - or so Bobby said - a minor fender bender first, causing the camper to brake and only just missing being crushed when it was rolled.

“Copy that,” Chimney replied, him and Hen already on their way with their medical bags.

“Buck, Edie, Ravi,” the captain continued. “Check these surrounding vehicles for injuries.”

“Got it, Cap,” they chorused, heading for the civilians that were either standing around or perched on their cars.

Their injuries were minor. A bumped head here, a tweaked shoulder there. People that they could take care of easily and then suggest a trip to the hospital later, just to be safe. It wasn’t a bad accident, whatever happened between the first two cars, it was just the domino effect that followed it.

Not that they had much of a chance to look any of them over beyond that, because-

“Cap!” Chimney shouted from the front of the camper. “Second victim in the back! No seatbelt!”

As one, Buck and Eddie and Ravi looked at the camper, smashed and on its side. And they cringed. Buck jogged over to look things over with the captain, but he could already tell it wasn’t good. It was obvious where the orange van struck it, with the back doors bent and warped and twisted.

“These back doors are obliterated,” Bobby said, agreeing with his unspoken observations. “We’ll have to pull that windshield.”

“On it,” Buck said, scrambling over to the engine. “Wolfe, Jones! Halligans!”

Because of how often they responded to traffic accidents on the Santa Monica Freeway, they had on hand a pair of heavy duty vacuum suction cups. They were perfect for grabbing hold of large sheets of glass, such as a windshield, that would otherwise be difficult or awkward to carry. They also made excellent handles to pull it out by.

Using the halligans as levers, they popped the partially cracked windshield out of the camper. Buck used the suction cups to get it out of the way, giving Hen and Chimney the room they needed to get in and assess the young lady that was just on the other side.

“Alright, what’s your name?” he heard Hen asked, followed by a frightened reply of,

“Jo.”

“Okay, Jo, can you move?”

“I- I don’t know, I think my leg is broken. B- But, please help her!”

The “her” being the second patient, the one they were most worried about.

“I need to get back there, so we’re gonna pull you out,” Chimney explained to her. “You might feel a little pain, okay?”

“O- Okay.”

“On three,” Buck said, kneeling down and wrapping his arms around her. “One, two…”

The young lady, Jo, let out a cry of pain as he lifted her up, her leg dangling awkwardly beneath her. He moved quickly, setting her down on the gurney that Ravi pulled over. Hen followed to check her over while Chimney crawled into the camper to properly assess the woman in the back, the one that hadn’t been belted in when the vehicle was rolled.

“Cap, we’re going to need an air ambulance,” he reported over the radio. “Patient is unresponsive, breathing is weak, and she’s lost bladder control. Could be a spinal.”

“Copy that.”

“Spinal injury?” Jo asked, no longer quite so distracted by her own pain. “Is she okay?”

“We won’t know until we get her to the hospital,” was Hen’s answer, and while it was the truth, it didn’t actually do much to settle Jo’s mind.

“Please don’t take me now,” she begged, eyes tracking Eddie’s movement as he carried a backboard to the camper. “I need to see her.”

“Hey, hey,” Buck said, trying to calm her before she injured herself more. There was no telling what other injuries she had. It was bad enough that they had to move her like they did so Chimney could access the more critical patient. “You’ll be able to see her before they airlift her. Promise, okay?”

They weren’t supposed to make promises, but it wasn’t like he was telling her that everything was going to be okay. Jo didn’t seem to be critical, so there was no reason to not let her have the chance to see her… Mother? Before she was loaded up onto the incoming helicopter.

“So, going on a trip with your mother?” he asked, trying to keep her distracted from the general anxiety and panic inducing situation she was currently in, and therefore calm enough for Hen to do her job.

“Mallory’s not my mom,” Jo said. “She was my mom’s best friend. We were doing this trip for her…”

“For her?”

“She’s dead.”

“Oh.” Awkward. “I’m sorry.”

Buck was spared the further tasting of his own foot by the sound of the incoming helicopter. They all looked up, spotting it quickly coming towards them.

“Jones, Wolfe!” Bobby called out, marching past them towards the traffic jam that had built up around them. “We need a hundred feet of clearance! Get these cars moving!”

“Buck,” Eddie radioed. “Come help.”

Buck hurried to the camper, already moving at just the sound of his name. The second patient, Mallory, was strapped to a backboard. He reached in and grabbed an end, easing it out between the front seats and through the gap where the windshield used to be. Together, he and Eddie carried her to where the air rescue was about to land. They had to pass Jo, who was being loaded up into the ambulance.

“See?” Buck said as they briefly paused. “Like I promised.”

“Mallory, I’m so sorry,” Jo said, reaching to grab her hand. “I can’t lose you, too.”

“I’m not going anywhere,” the older woman assured her. “We still have a lot of ground to cover.”

“Next time, we’ll take the train!” the younger said, raising her voice as they moved on.

It was a quick hand-off to the air rescue paramedics. Buck gave them a warning about the possible spinal injury, and then Mallory was in the helicopter and lifting away. It was quick, it was easy, it was efficient.

“Hey, Cap,” Eddie called over. “I think there was a propane stove in the van. I’m gonna check it out.”

“Good thinking,” Bobby called back.

Buck patted him on the shoulder as they split ways, him going to see if there was anywhere he was needed, his husband off to clamber back into the camper. Hen was in the driver’s seat of the ambulance, with Jo and Chimney in the back. Jones had the same idea Buck did, while Ravi and Wolfe were on the other side of the median, by the engine.

And then the overpass shook.

There is a man. His name is Hector. Hector is a truck driver, hauling freight of all sorts. The company he works for doesn’t matter, the freight he hauls doesn’t matter. What matters is that Hector has been awake for a very, very long time.

He has tried everything to stay awake for just a bit longer, to get to his destination just that bit sooner. Time is money, or rather, time spent sleeping is money he doesn’t get. At this moment, right now, Hector is throwing back his third energy drink of the morning.

It isn’t helping, not really.

Hector’s eyes are heavy.

His entire body is heavy.

There is an overpass up ahead, a bridge leading to an interchange. He sees it in the distance.

A blink, and it is closer.

Another blink, and it is closer still.

He blinks again, and this time, his eyes stay closed.

He does not see the construction workers waving for him to stop, nor does he hear them. He doesn’t hear the cars blaring their horns, nor does he see them careen to get out of the way.

Hector has finally fallen asleep.

He stays asleep, even as his truck drifts to the side and slams, full speed, into the support.

His truck is very heavy, and the freight makes it heavier still.

The bridge shakes and cracks and crumbles.

Buck has no idea what’s happening. The bridge shook, and now it’s cracking beneath him. Literally, he can see the cracks forming in the pavement. Everyone around him is in a panic, shouting and yelling, and that panic gets worse as the lane they are on starts to angle downwards.

Buck was behind the ambulance when it started, and that is not a good place to be. Because now the ambulance is sliding, sliding, backwards. He was knocked to the ground at some point, shaken off his feet, and now he scrambles to get out of the way.

The vehicle goes by, missing him by a hair’s breadth, so close he can feel it brushing his gear. So close that if he was properly on his feet, he would have been brained by the side-view mirror going over him.

The bridge is still cracking, pieces are still falling, and Buck has nothing to grab hold of.

Buck falls, too.

Chapter End Notes

"Where's Lucy?" a hypothetical person might ask. "Is she still at the 147? Was she one of the air rescue people and they simply didn't interact?"
And the answer, dear non-existent reader, is that it doesn't matter. Lucy doesn't matter. Everything, every single action she took in the entire show, does not matter. There was a rant here about it, but I deleted it because that's not what any of you, hypothetical readers, are interested in.
Also everything about this bridge call broke me. Like, it's in Long Beach!?!? It's in Long Beach! They responded to a call forty minutes away! Or perhaps more than an hour, if traffic was bad! And then they have Maddie radioing for help from stations in Downtown LA, like-
It broke me. It just broke me.

Breathe In, Breathe Out, Move On

Chapter Summary

In which Buck spends an entire chapter delaying a panic attack.

Buck did not black out, however much he might have wished that he had. There was no loss of time, no lapse of consciousness, not even a moment. Although, there was an advantage gained from his “adventurous” childhood in that he could tell, just from how everything hurt, that he didn’t have any broken bones.

There was no missing time, no scratchy jump in his memories, which told him that the ache in his head was simply that and nothing more. He remembered them responding to the crash on an overpass on the 710, he remembered one of the patients was air-lifted out while the other was loaded onto the ambulance. He remembered Eddie checking the camper for the propane stove he thought he saw, he remembered heading to lend a hand wherever it was needed.

He remembered, too, how they’d all fallen as part of the overpass collapsed.

He had landed face first on the ambulance’s windshield, which was now spider-webbed with cracks and yet still held. The bottom half of his face was wet and he tasted pennies in his mouth and the glass was smeared red with what he knew was his blood. He could see Hen in the driver’s seat, unconscious and unresponsive no matter how much he tapped on the windshield; there was a bloody laceration on her temple.

Oh God, he thought, pushing himself up and looking around. Oh God, oh God, oh-

He took a breath in.

He let the breath out.

He wiped at the wetness on his face and focused on what he could do, instead of how bad things might be. First on the list,

“One Eighteen, report in,” Buck ordered into the radio, doing his best to keep his voice from breaking. “I need a headcount.”

“Wolfe and I are grabbing the ropes,” Ravi radioed.

“I’m in the van,” Eddie responded. Buck couldn’t lie and say that he didn’t let out a small sob of relief at the sound of his husband’s voice, even with the undercurrents of stress and pain. “Pretty sure I broke a couple ribs. But this van is about to get pancaked.”

It was, Buck could see it when he stretched his neck out. The ambulance had landed on it and the camper van was in turn wedged against something else, the only thing keeping both vehicles from falling the rest of the way.

“I’ll get you out soon,” he promised. “Han, what’s your status?”

A silent pause.

“Han?” he tried again. “Han, come in.”

“Han here,” Chimney finally responded. Weak, groggy. Status: Not Good. Instead of dwelling on that, on something he couldn’t do anything about right now, Buck settled for being thankful that the man responded at all and moved on.

“Jones, Captain Nash, I haven’t heard from either of you.”

No response.

“Nash, Jones, come in.”

Still no response, and now Buck could feel the panic really building up, could feel it start to claw its way up his spine and into his skull.

He took a breath in.

He let the breath out.

“Dispatch, One Eighteen,” he reported into the radio. “Requesting immediate assistance to our location. We’ve had a partial bridge collapse with firefighters down and an unknown number of civilian casualties.”

Buck couldn’t see how many, if any, of the other cars on the bridge had fallen with them. And while it was in the middle of the morning, and so therefore not as busy as it would be at a different time of day, this was still Los Angeles and these were still busy roads. It would require a miracle for there to not be anyone trapped in the debris below. But that was yet another problem he could do nothing about right now and so it was shelved for later, right beside the imminent panic attack over why neither Bobby nor Jones were answering their radios.

“Copy that, One Eighteen.” It was Maddie who responded, which went a long way towards calming him. His sister would get them the help they needed.

Okay, moving on to the next item on the list of Things He Can Actually Do Right Now, which was distressingly short. Hen was still unconscious in the driver’s seat of the ambulance, right below him. Chimney and the patient, Jo, were both in the back. He could get in there, properly assess Hen and try to get a visual on the other two, figure out how Not Good his future brother-in-law’s status currently was.

But how could he get in?

Answer: the passenger door had its window down, excellent.

Gingerly crawling across the windshield, Buck got to the edge of the ambulance’s hood. He used the side view mirror, the one that nearly took his head off, as a foothold and then twisted his other leg around onto the door and through the window. It was an awkward shimmy into the seat after that, where he immediately froze and held his breath when things shifted.

“Chim? You still back there?” He tried to get a look at him through the window in the cab’s back wall, but the paramedic was in the far corner and mostly blocked from view by the gurney. At least Jo was strapped in, and he could make out her head turning left and right as she frantically looked around her. Which was a tally for the positives, however limited they currently were.

“I’m alive,” was Chimney’s low energy answer. “Not great.”

Buck focused his attention on the person in front of him, on Hen. Unconscious, bleeding from a head wound, but breathing. Possible concussion. He didn’t have a c-collar on hand, and everything about their current situation was tenuous. She needed to be moved, and he needed to know if she could manage that on her own or not. He also couldn’t risk her waking up in the middle of a rescue operation and accidentally make things worse because of her disorientation.

So he stuck the salts under Hen’s nose until her face twisted and her eyes fluttered open.

“Don’t move too much,” Buck warned. He should be holding her head and neck still until they got a collar on her, but they didn’t have any hands to spare. Aggravating a possible neck injury was unfortunately something they’d have to risk at the moment.

“What happened?” she mumbled.

“Bridge collapse,” he answered. “You with me?”

“I’m good, I…” Hen squinted up at him. Or maybe it was at his face, because she pulled a wipe out of the mess around them and started pawing at the blood he knew was smeared around his nose and mouth. “Are you ‘kay?”

“I’m okay,” Buck said, but let her do it. Her hands were mostly steady, but he could swear that, once or twice, she reached up and missed him entirely. Vision problems, maybe?

“Where is everybody?”

“Ravi is up top with the probie, Eddie’s stuck in the van, and Chimney and the patient are both in the back,” he listed off for her. “I don’t know how bad off Chim is right now, but I know it’s not good. I’m gonna go up top and get the ambulance secured so we can start getting you all out.”

Hen blinked a few times. To Buck, it seemed like she was having trouble staying present. So that concussion was not only possible, it was past probable and heading towards definite.

“Where’s Cap?” she asked. “Where’s Bobby?”

“He’s MIA, so’s Jones,” he told her. “Are- Are you sure you’re good?”

“Yeah, yeah, I’m… Just get that rope so we can get out of here.”

“Okay…” Buck stuck his head out the window and shouted up. “Ravi! Where’s that line!?”

It was like a magic trick. He asked and he received, the end of the rope falling to rest just a little bit away from the window. Getting out and grabbing it took as much careful maneuvering as climbing in did, his still-forming bruises protesting at the way stretched. Afterwards, it was a matter of keeping his eyes dead ahead while he pulled himself up.

Ravi was there to greet him, grabbing hold of his arm to pull him over the edge and onto solid ground.

“I have the ropes hooked up to the winch and the truck,” he said, passing him the helmet and harness, and then lending a hand in getting it on.

“Awesome.”

“So, wh- what’s the plan?” Wolfe asked, though he wasn’t looking at them; his gaze was focused on the sight below them.

“The plan is that I’ll go back down to anchor the ambulance so it doesn’t fall the rest of the way, and then I get Hen out,” Buck said. He finished strapping everything on and grabbed the younger man’s shoulder, pulling so he faced him. “You will do everything Ravi says. If he tells you to grab something, you grab it. If he tells you to do something, you do it. No arguing, no questions unless it’s to clarify. Understand?”

“But I-”

“Yes or no, probie.”

The younger man scowled, but nodded and gave a slightly petulant sounding,

“Yes, sir.”

“Good.” Buck tossed the anchor line over the edge so it fell to the ambulance. He shoulder the bag containing rope gear and threw his leg back over the edge.

“One Eighteen, LAPD on scene at the lower level and ready to assist,” Athena radioed. He hadn’t even noticed her sirens arriving, distracted as he was. “One-Eighteen? Captain?”

Bobby wasn’t responding to anyone, he didn’t know how bad off Chimney actually was, and Hen had a concussion of unknown severity. Which meant…

Shit.

“LAPD, this is Buck. Captain Nash is unaccounted for,” he responded. “Clear the area and start setting up somewhere nearby for triage. We have wounded that are about to come down and vehicles that are not secured.”

It felt unnatural to be giving Athena Grant orders. It felt even more unnatural when, not even a moment later, she simply said,

“Copy that.”

Trusting that the sergeant was doing as he asked, Buck abseiled down to the ambulance. The bag was tossed onto the hood and then immediately slid to hit the windshield. He grabbed the anchor rope, pulling until he had the hook, which he then attached to the hard point just below and behind the bumper.

“Ravi!” he called up. “Tension!”

There was a whir as the extra slack was tugged back until the line was taut. Buck gave the ambulance a test shove; it creaked but did not shift, which was good enough. Climbing back up onto it, he crawled again to that open window and threw the bag onto the seat beside Hen.

“Okay, there’s a harness and a capture strap in there,” he told her. “I need you to get Chimney and the patient down while I handle the van. Can you do that?”

What he should do, considering Hen’s head wound, was take her down himself and then leave her there until she could get checked over. Except the only other first responders to arrive so far was LAPD. Except he didn’t know how bad off Chimney was. Except the van wasn’t secured and could fall the rest of the way at any moment. Except they didn’t have enough hands to spare, and they didn’t have the time to spare.

Buck couldn’t tell how much of Hen’s squinty glare was indignation and how much was the concussion.

“I’ll see you at the bottom,” he told her once she nodded, and then slid off the ambulance to head for the van’s back doors.

“Sending down the saw!” Ravi called down, not even having to be asked because he truly was the best. And it was a good thing, too, because the doors had been unusable when they first arrived. Now, well..

Buck rapped on the door. Once, twice, three times.

“I’m here, Eddie, okay?” he said, hoping his husband could hear him. “I’m about to get this open and then we’ll get you out, just shield your eyes.”

He grabbed the saw when it reached him and was poised to start cutting away at the obstruction that dared to separate the two of them. And then Athena radioed again.

“One Eighteen,” she said, and the way she said it immediately had his stomach dropping. “We’ve located one of yours.”

“Understood.” He didn’t ask for more details, he didn’t need to. Her tone filled in all the blanks for him. And the fact that the world wasn’t ending to the sounds of her grief told him exactly who she was talking about.

He took a breath in.

He let the breath out.

The saw cut through the metal like it was nothing, sparks flying as Buck pressed the tool down without mercy. There was the sound of metal creaking again, coming from up above. Probably Hen working to get Chimney and Jo. But he ignored that, just like he was ignoring everything else, and focused on what was right in front of him.

When he could finally manhandle the doors open, he nearly let out a sob of relief. There was Eddie: face screwed up in a grimace of pain, pinned between a fridge and a couch, clutching at the chain with his St. Christopher medallion and wedding ring. There was Eddie, love of his life, hurting but alive.

“Hey, baby.”

“Evan,” his husband gasped. He held out his hand, and Buck took it in his. “How are we doing this? Is the van secured?”
