“The van isn’t, but I am.” Buck kicked a random piece of debris out of the way before stepping farther into the vehicle, which rocked concerningly. Eddie glared at him. “Don’t give me that look, what did you think we were going to do? Just yank you out? That’s how you get punctured lungs.”

It killed him to let go of the other man’s hand, but he had to do it because he needed both for the task ahead. 

Buck put a lot of work into his body, and while vanity was a large part, it wasn’t the entire reason why. He built up his muscles so he could run up and down multi-story buildings in full turn-out gear. He built up his muscles so he could pick up his kids whenever they wanted.

He built up his muscles so he could do this, so he could lift up the fridge pinning his husband and allow the man to pull himself to freedom. He held it up until Eddie was entirely clear and then let it drop back down with a heavy thud.

And then Buck grabbed Eddie’s face and pulled him in and kissed him as hard as he could.

“Let’s get you out of here,” Buck said. He wrapped his arms around Eddie and half-pulled, half-carried him out of the van.

Thanks to the way things had fallen, the debris made something of a set of stairs leading to the ground below. Buck guided Eddie down them, each and every pained grunt the other man let out acting as a knife to the heart. Other stations had arrived in the meanwhile, including the rest of their own. He spotted Meyers and Price helping Jo off the truck she and Hen were standing on, before carrying her off to wherever triage had been set up.

“Buck.” Eddie’s voice caught his attention, as it always did. He turned his head to look at him, and then turned again to follow his gaze.

Jones was laying on the pavement just a foot or two away, still and limp, his helmet lying near-by. His eyes were closed, maybe Athena’s doing. Despite already knowing what he would find, Buck unhooked himself from the line and knelt to check for a pulse anyway.

Nothing.

He took a breath in.

He let the breath out.

And then, before he had any chance to decide what to do next, there was the sound of violent gagging and something splattering. Hen had collapsed on the vehicle she’d stopped on, head hanging over the edge as she vomited. Buck and Eddie rushed over.

“I’m okay,” she lied to them. “I’m okay. P- Pretty sure I’m just concussed.”

“You are definitely concussed,” Buck said, climbing up a bit so he could get to her. And it wasn’t just anything, with all the symptoms she’d been showing. “You’re getting down and then you and Eddie are going to get checked over.”

“No! We need to get Chimney!”

“And I will get him,” he said. “I promise, just come on down.”

It took more coaxing, because she really was concussed but also because her best friend was up there. While Buck wasn’t sure how bad off Chimney was, he knew it must have been bad for Hen to be this worried. It took more coaxing, but they finally managed to get her to come down. Buck had to take a lot of her weight to manage it, but it got done.

“Come on, let’s get you both to the paramedics,” he said, reaching behind Hen to unhook her from the line. “I’ll come back and get Chimney, I promise.”

There was the sound of metal creaking. Loud, ominous, creaking that started up as they were walking away. Looking up, Buck saw the ambulance shift and then, below it, the unsecured camper van starting to roll again.

“Get back!” he cried out in warning to everyone around them, hurrying Eddie and Hen. “Get back!”

The van toppled, bouncing off debris to crash down right where they’d been mere moments before. Buck had the briefest of moments to be thankful that he managed to get Eddie out of that before it finally went, and only the briefest of moments. Because, as things are wont to do in their lives, it got worse.

The back doors to the ambulance burst open, gravity sending the gurney hurtling out like a rocket. Chimney came with it, only just managed to grab hold of something before he fell out entirely. His legs, his entire body, dangled in the open air.

“Chim!” Hen screamed, while at the same time he heard Albert somewhere behind them, crying, “Howie!”

There wasn’t really any time to think, not really. Buck saw the problem, he knew the solution, he just needed to follow through. He sprinted back to the debris, to the pile of cars and concrete, and started climbing. It wasn’t all that different from rock climbing, really, just a matter of finding the right hand- and footholds. Right hand, left foot; left hand, right foot; repeat. It got him most of the way, and then Hen’s still dangling rope got him the rest.

He pulled himself up and took short hops until he was able to reach Chimney, wrapping an arm around him and taking his weight. He was pale, far too pale, and there was something warm and wet where Buck was holding him.

“Okay, grab the rope with me,” he instructed. “We’ll get you down. How bad is it?”

“That gurney that fell out?” Chimney waited for him to nod. “It stabbed me.”

“Oh.” Fuck, that would explain the wetness he was feeling. “Okay, so we’re gonna go a bit faster, then.”

They made the trip down as quickly as possible, but that wasn’t as quick as Buck would like. It wasn’t an easy task to do, gripping the rope with one hand and holding onto Chimney with the other as they abseiled down. Except it wasn’t really abseiling, since the rope wasn’t attached to them.

It wasn’t just Hen and Eddie waiting for them when they got to the ground below. Albert was there too, having apparently sprinted over. The younger Han was quick to help get his brother onto the gurney he brought with him.

“The One Thirty-three are just arriving, but our ambulance-” He paused, glanced back at the one still dangling off the overpass. “Our other ambulance is over here. Clarke! Come give us a hand!”

“I think we’re gonna need a new gurney,” Chimney grunted out, wincing when Albert pressed a bunch of gauze to his wound. It had been bleeding constantly the entire way down, and probably would’ve bled more if Buck hadn’t been (unintentionally) keeping pressure on it. “Buck, would you…?”

“I’ll tell Maddie as soon as-” They tell him which hospital they were headed for, was how Buck was intending to finish that sentence. But he got interrupted by,

“This is Sergeant Athena Grant to anyone on scene.” The sergeant had been curiously absent, but Buck hadn’t the time to focus dwell on it. Now he was starting to regret that. “I have Captain Nash of the One Eighteen and a civilian. We’re trapped in a container at the bottom of the collapse. He’s pinned. We’re in need of assistance.”

Bobby was alive. Bobby was alive and he was trapped. With Athena. Bobby was alive and he was pinned and he was trapped with Athena and-

He took a breath in.

He let the breath out.

“Get him loaded up and go,” Buck ordered, already marching for that container. “Hen, go with them.”

“What, no-”

“You have a concussion,” he snapped, not having the time for this. “You were unconscious, you are disoriented, you vomited. You will go with them.”

He didn’t give her time to argue further. Others from the 118, led by Ortiz, were already converging on their location, and so he waved them along on his way back to the collapse site. It was easy to find the container, big and bright yellow as it was, and also partially covered by part of the downed bridge. There was a slab of concrete sticking out at an angle from the top, which Buck assumed was how Bobby got in there to be trapped. But the camper van falling had shifted things and now that hole was blocked, as were the doors that Athena and the civilian used for access.

“That door is completely blocked,” Eddie commented, echoing his silent observations.

Wait.

“Your ribs are broken. Why are you-? Nevermind.” Buck could yell at his husband later, there wasn’t time for that now. He grabbed his radio and thumbed the switch. “Ravi? Can you see where we are?”

“Yeah.”

“I need that saw again.”

“Rescue saw, coming down.”

“Go get halligans and anything else we can use for leverage,” he ordered the others, and they hurried back to the ladder truck. Except for Eddie; Buck grabbed his coat before he could, and then pointed at a block of cement that was out of the way. “Sit there.”

“Buck-”

“Broken ribs,” he pointed out again. “You can’t help lift anything right now.”

His husband scowled and glowered but he sat down exactly where he was told.

“Buck,” Ravi radioed again. “The saw’s hooked on some rebar to your right and above. Do you see it?”

“I see it, getting it now,” Buck replied, hurrying over and clambering up so he could grab it.

The doors to the shipping container were blocked by debris they had no hope of moving without access to a crane. The hole that Bobby fell through was similarly sealed. But he knew it wasn’t hopeless, and he knew it wasn’t impossible, because he was a firefighter. And he learned a long time ago that there was not a locked room anywhere they couldn’t break into with the right tool.

And sometimes the right tool was a heavy duty rescue saw that could, would, and often did chew through metal like it was paper mache.

“Sergeant, bang on the sides, I need to know where you are,” Buck requested. A moment later, there was a nearly muffled thump followed by another, near the blocked entrance. “Step back, cover your eyes.”

The entrances that were there already were all unavailable to them.

So he simply made a new one.

By the time the others returned, he’d made them a doorway and he used the halligan that Ortiz passed him to peel it open like a sardine can. The inside was a mess of cardboard boxes and pieces of concrete, with Athena and the civilian kneeling by Bobby’s head. The captain was pinned by a small chunk of the bridge, but he was awake and looking at them.

They swarmed him, the 118 that were there. They stuck their tools under what was pinning him and lifted as a single unit. Athena and the civilian grabbed him by the collar of his coat and hauled him out from under it, allowing them to drop it back down. There were paramedics from the 133 there at the ready, and they quickly got the captain situated on their gurney.

The entire trip to where the ambulances were waiting, Bobby was asking about everyone else and downplaying his own injuries. Athena hadn’t told him about Jones while they were stuck, apparently, and so Buck got to see first-hand the devastation on his face at the news. And unfortunately he couldn’t stay and mourn with him, because there wasn’t time. 

He went to go make sure Hen was actually getting checked over, which she was. Albert apparently stuck her in the ambulance with Chimney, and they were all on their way to the hospital. So after that, he had to make sure Maddie was informed of where they were going. Then he needed to hunt down Eddie, stop him from making his already broken ribs worse. And then-

“Aren’t you going with them?” Battalion Chief Williams asked. Buck hadn’t even realized she’d shown up, hadn’t even realized she was standing next to him as he looked around and tried to figure out everything he needed to do next..

“Someone needs to make sure the engine makes it back to the station,” was his dumb reply. “I’m- I’m the only other certified to drive it.”

“It’ll be taken care of,” she said, and then shifted her gaze to somewhere past him. “Sergeant Grant! I have one of yours.”

He turned, saw Athena arguing with Eddie. He’d assumed she would have gotten into the ambulance with Bobby, but it made sense. Her patrol car was here, after all.

“Chief,” he tried as both the sergeant and his husband headed his way. “There’s-”

“Go,” the chief interrupted. “You’ve done enough.”

Buck wanted to argue, felt like he should argue. But… But he really liked the idea of sitting down for a bit. And he really, really liked the idea of going to the hospital and seeing the others for himself.

He took a breath in.

He let the breath out.

And then he met Athena and Eddie halfway.

After the Bridge Fell

Chapter Summary

In which, the aftermath.

The adrenaline crashed on the way to the hospital and the panic attack that Buck had been putting off for so long finally rolled over him like a wave. He was in the back of Athena’s patrol cruiser with Eddie, barely minutes away from the site of the bridge collapse with the sirens wailing and one of the ambulances just ahead, when it struck. And it struck hard.

Jones was dead. Someone he saw just about every day, someone he thought of as a friend, was gone. And he very nearly wasn’t the only one, because if Bobby hadn’t fallen the way he had, if he had fallen anywhere else, if he hadn’t been protected by that small pocket of space, he could’ve been dead too. And Eddie! If Eddie had still been in that van when it fell the rest of the way, if that van hadn’t been caught the way it had, then- Then-

And Chimney! Hen! If the van hadn’t stopped the ambulance, if things had landed just slightly different, if Chimney hadn’t been able to catch himself on his way out, if- If- If-

Buck’s heart was a bird caught in too small of a cage, his breath a bowling ball being squeezed through a silly straw.

They came so close, so very close, to all of them being lost. If he hadn’t managed to get them out like he had, if Ravi hadn’t been up above to throw him the ropes the way he did, no one would have been rescued in time. The camper van would have fallen and pancaked with Eddie inside it. The ambulance would have followed, taking Hen and Chimney and Buck for the ride.

Bobby might’ve been the safest of them all, pinned and trapped but sheltered.

But things had shifted when the camper fell, and there was no way to say that they wouldn’t have shifted different if the ambulance had fallen with it, if things weren’t going to shift again while the crews worked, so maybe Bobby wouldn’t have been safe after all, maybe he would have been crushed instead of pinned, maybe-

There were words being spoken, lost in the panic he was drowning beneath. There was someone holding Buck’s hand, pressing his palm against a firm chest with a steady drumbeat behind it. There was a hand gripping the back of his neck, thumb rubbing circles into his skin.

Buck pulled his knees up a bit, stuck his head between them, tried to get his lungs to actually work. He yanked at his coat with his free hand, undoing the fastenings so it wasn’t as constricting, so he wasn’t as trapped.

“Just breathe,” a voice instructed, familiar and loved and calm. “That’s all you have to do right now, mi sol, just listen to me and breathe. Sólo escucha. Sólo respira. You did so good, you were so wonderful. Tan hermoso. Mi hermoso sol. You got me out, you got all of us out. We’re all safe.”

He shook his head, because that wasn’t true for all of them, and it very nearly wasn’t true for any of them.

“Just breathe,” that voice insisted. “In… Out… In… Out…”

Buck did his best to follow along. He took a breath when he was told and didn’t let it out until he was told. In, hold, out, hold. His vision expanded from a pinprick between his boots, the black edges receding that he hadn’t even noticed encroaching.

The bird in his chest settled.

“Okay,” he gasped out, raising his head again. “I’m- I’m okay.”

“How long had that been building?” Athena asked, worried gaze fixed on him through the rearview mirror.

“Um.” Buck licked at his lips. “How long ago did the bridge collapse?”

“Ah.”

“You good?” Eddie asked, his eyes worried when Buck turned to meet them. They were also creased with pain; the way he was holding Buck could not be feeling good for his ribs.

“I’m good,” Buck promised, grabbing his husband’s arm and lowering it back to his side. “I’m- I’ll be fine. What about you? Did you get looked at?”

The hand pressed against Eddie’s chest was tugged free of his grip, and he ran it down his side. A pressing of fingers had his husband leaping in his seat with a curse.

“He refused to take anything,” Athena tattled from the front. “We’re here.”

“Here” being the hospital they were taking everyone. Buck apparently spent more time caught in the throes of his delayed panic attack than he knew, because he couldn’t actually remember the trip. They had to wait for Athena to let them out after she parked, but then he was helping Eddie inside while they tried to catch up with the paramedics.

“We’ve got an abdominal puncture wound,” Hen was reciting, helping Albert and Clarke push Chimney along to the waiting nurses and doctors. “Seems to have missed his major organs, already pushed two milligrams of morphine.”

“More like four,” Chimney took off his oxygen mask to say. “I was sideways, hard to keep track.”

Hen barely paid his quip any attention, already turning to meet Buck and Eddie.

“This one’s-” she started, but Buck cut her off.

“Paramedic Wilson,” he said, shifting his hold on his husband, “was unconscious for several minutes, showed signs of being disoriented, and vomited. Definite concussion.”

“Buck, I’m-” the paramedic started, but she suddenly swayed in place, gloved hand going to her head. Albert caught her before she started to fall and hurried her to a nearby chair. “O- Oh. Yeah, I’m- I’m definitely gonna need a CT scan.”

“Doctor!” a nearby nurse called, already pulling a penlight out of her pocket to check Hen’s pupillary response.

Buck shoved Eddie into the seat beside the paramedic with firm instructions not to move - and gave Albert firm instructions to make sure a doctor also looked at him and his broken ribs - before rushing to intercept Bobby, who paramedics from the 133 were bringing in. Athena was right at his side, gripping the captain’s hand. The one not in a sling, at least.

“Probable AC joint sprain,” one of the paramedics reported. “Multiple lacerations and bone bruises. He was found pinned, so further crush injuries are likely.”

“But hey, you’re in one piece,” Buck said, falling into step with them. “Gonna take more than having a bridge dropped on you to take you down.”

“Everyone else?” Bobby asked.

“Hen and Eddie are right over there,” he said, pointing. “Waiting for the doctor that Albert flagged down. Chimney got taken just ahead of you.”

“Okay,” the captain sighed, nodding.

Buck couldn’t follow them all the way, stopping just short of the swinging doors. Athena, standing beside him, laid her hand on his arm.

“I’ll make sure Karen’s been called,” she said. “And I’m pretty sure I saw Maddie hurrying in.”

“Good.” Shit, he’d totally spaced on calling his sister to make sure she knew which hospital. “That’s… That’s good. I’m gonna go make sure Eddie’s not being stubborn.”

“Buck.” Her voice stopped him just as he started to turn and start off for where he’d left his husband. He looked back at her and the hand she’d put on his arm was raised to cup his face.

“Y- Yeah?’

“You did good today,” she said, smile soft. “I’m so very proud of you. And Bobby is, too.”

“Th- Thanks, Ma,” he stammered out, ducking his head in a futile attempt to hide the way his face burned at the words. “I- I’m gonna…”

He didn’t run away.

(Yes he did.)

Eddie was the first one to be discharged, after getting his (confirmed broken) ribs x-rayed and a prescription of painkillers. Albert, who’d lingered around, took them back to the station in the ambulance with Clarke. Buck would have liked to stay, would have much preferred pacing the hospital waiting room in the vain hopes of getting word about the conditions of everyone else just that little bit faster, but he’d started getting worried texts from Abuela, Pepa, Carla, and Christopher.

Apparently, the bridge collapse was on the news and the number on the still dangling ambulance was perfectly visible from the angle they shot. So there was very little chance of downplaying the severity.

They were practically swarmed the moment they arrived home, Buck helping Eddie through their front door. Abuela was tutting in Spanish, Carla was asking questions, Chris just bullied himself in the front to throw his arms around them both. It was only due to Buck’s quick reflexes that their kid didn’t accidentally get one of Eddie’s bad ribs.

There was no way that wouldn’t end in anything except tears for everyone.

“We didn’t wash at the station,” Buck said. He’d washed the blood off his face while Eddie got x-rayed, but that was it. “So we’re still covered in bridge. Do you all mind if…?”

“Of course!” Carla said, stepping back and clearing the way for Eddie.

“I’ll go heat something up,” Abuela said, already marching for the kitchen.

“Are you okay?” Chris worriedly asked, following behind them with Hoover. 

“Just some hurt ribs, mijo,” Eddie assured him, messing Chris’ hair. He started to bend forward to do the same to the dog, but the movement had him grimacing.

“Did they…?” Carla let the question die when Buck pulled the prescription bottle out and rattled it.

“I’m gonna make him swallow a couple when we eat,” he said. “Maybe wrap it in cheese first.”

“I’m not the dog,” his husband complained.

“Then take your pills like you’re supposed to. Without whining.”

Getting Eddie out of his turnout gear had been an exercise; getting him out of his uniform shirt was torture for them both. Buck did most of the work, stripping his husband as quickly and as pain-free as he could manage, pausing to press soft kisses at the bruises that were starting to bloom on Eddie’s torso. They were mostly red at the moment, but some were already turning blue and purple. 

It could have been worse. It was so close to being worse.

“Hey.” Eddie grabbed his gelled curls, tugged until he was looking. “I’m okay. You had my back, and I’m okay.”

“Yeah. Yeah, I- I know.”

“Good, now get naked. Give me a show.”

Buck rolled his eyes and stood. The act of yanking his shirt off himself was much easier, but the face that Eddie was wearing when his vision was clear of fabric had him pausing.

“What?”

“That,” his husband said, reaching out to poke at his chest. Buck looked down and grimaced at the sight of his soon-to-be technicolor skin. “Did you get looked at?”

“I’m fine, nothing’s broken,” he said, tossing his shirt to the floor with Eddie’s clothes, his pants joining shortly after. “Was that enough of a show?”

Eddie hummed.

“Give it a twirl.”

Buck rolled his eyes and grabbed his arm to drag him into the bathroom.

“So I saw your ambulance dangling off a bridge on the news. Well, part of a bridge.”

“Yeah, that was… A mess.”

“They said that one of you…?”

“Jones. He, uh. He drove the engine.”

“Shit, man, I’m sorry. But is everyone else okay?”

“Beaten up, but yeah. We’re totally gonna make it to the wedding, Teek.”

“Oh. Uh. About that…”

“...what happened?”

“Carlos’ dad got shot at his home. He… He didn’t make it.”

“Shit.”

Chris wanted to stay home from school, the day after the bridge collapse. And honestly? It didn’t take much for them to let him. The kid had clung to them both after they came back out of their bedroom the evening before, and they’d clung right back. And honestly, him being there was a real help. Buck ached something fierce, and the pain meds had Eddie in a haze, so another pair of hands was much needed.

Mostly, he was given the job of keeping Gavin from climbing all over Eddie, because the toddler wasn’t quite able to grasp the concept of broken ribs just yet.

At the moment, they were camped out in the living room, everyone except for Abuela; she was in the backyard. Eddie was half-conked out, propped up by cushions and leaning heavily onto Buck’s shoulder. Hoover was curled up at their feet, head laying on his front paws. Chris was working on work for school, so Gavin was currently squirming in Buck’s lap, trying to crawl into Eddie’s and complaining when he wasn’t allowed.

“Your dad’s hurt,” Buck told him. “So you’re stuck with Pops hugs for now.”

This was clearly not good enough for the one-year-old, who smacked him on the arm and shouted,

“No!”

“Yes, little guy.”

“No!”

“Yes.”

“No!”

“Si, si,” Eddie mumbled, patting at the kid’s face without even opening his eyes. “Escucha a tu padre.”

The action derailed Gavin’s building tantrum, causing him to giggle as he nearly toppled over backwards. Buck gently nudged so Eddie was leaning on the arm of the couch instead of him and got to his feet, swinging the toddler a little as he did so.

“Why don’t we go out with Hoover and-”

There was knocking at the door. A polite rapping that he didn’t recognize. He adjusted Gavin against his shoulder and went to answer it.

He wasn’t sure who he was expecting, but it definitely wasn’t Battalion Chief Williams, in full uniform.

“Ch- Chief!” he managed to get out, opening the door wider. “Um, come in. Sorry about any mess, and Eddie, the meds-”

“It’s fine,” Williams assured him, giving him a friendly smile as she stepped inside. She nodded to Chris when he shyly waved to her. “This little guy is Gavin, right?”

“Y- Yes, ma’am. And the dog is Hoover.”

“It’s nice to meet you, Mr. Gavin,” the chief said, shaking the hand the toddler stuck out. “And Mr. Hoover. How is Eddie?”

“Uh, mostly asleep,” Buck told her, gesturing at the man himself on the couch. “Just some busted ribs.”

“I’m glad to hear that.”

“So uh, is there… Is there anything I can do, ma’am? Or- Or…”

“I came by to tell you that your engine made it back to the station,” she said. “And that we managed to arrange cover at the station until everyone is back on their feet. In particular, we have someone to be the driver-engineer for A shift, at least for the short-term. Maybe longer, depending.”

“Oh,” Buck said. The reminder that they needed to replace Jones, the knowledge that they’d found someone to replace him with so quickly, was not pleasant to hear. It was necessary, they couldn’t very well not have someone driving the engine, but still. “Wait, but I thought I’d be filling in? Like I told you yesterday, ma’am, aside from Ortiz, I’m the other one certified for the role.”

“You can’t very well drive from the captain’s seat, Diaz.”

What, he thought.

“What,” he said.

“Captain Nash’s injuries are going to keep him out for a month, at least,” Williams told him. “It’s been decided that you’ll be filling in for him until his return. If you want it, of course.”

“I- But-” Buck shook his head. “But back in August…”

“Back in August, we were still neck deep in the PR disaster that Taylor Kelly opened us up to,” the chief said. “And yes, you did become collateral in that due to someone’s concern.”

“So what changed?”

“Someone reached retirement, someone else got promoted,” Williams said. “The mess got cleaned up, time marched on. And now, here we are.”

“Just like that?”

“Just like that.”

“She wrote a book,” Buck couldn’t help but point out. “About us.”

“Oh, I am well aware. I’m pretty sure half the department has read it,” she said, and he did his best to not cringe at the words. “Why did you never tell anyone that Wilson and the older Han went to Taylor Kelly?”

“What?”

“Henrietta Wilson and Howard Han. They purposefully went to the reporter, sought her out to involve her. But apparently, you never shared that with Alonso. Even when Wilson was made substitute captain over you. Why?”

“They’re my friends,” Buck said, giving her an awkward shrug. “I didn’t want to throw them under the bus like that.”

The battalion chief stared at him for a moment and hummed to herself, before finally nodding.

“Well, you’ll need to report early to your next shift, Captain Diaz,” she said. “Give your husband my well wishes when he wakes up. Goodbye, Christopher, Gavin. Hoover.”

Buck stood there for a moment after she walked out, staring at the closed door, until Gavin started squirming in his arms. He let him down and turned to meet Chris’ wide-eyed gaze.

“That- That just happened, right?”

“Right,” Chris said, giving a decisive nod.

“So I didn’t dream that up?”

“No, Pops, you’re awake.”

“Okay. Okay, that’s- Okay.”

There was a pressing question at the moment, and it was this: Who did he run to and tell first?

(The answer was Abuela.)

 

Fin.
